(d) Improvements in affinity of compound B allowed a more efficient inhibition of this competitive pathway 3.7.2 Multi-specificity and Impact on Receptor Turnover Rate Considerations regarding receptor dynamics and receptor turnover rates are of vital importance in the design of antibody-based therapeutics and in particular for multi-specific modalities. Many of the pharmacological receptors addressed by antibody-based therapeutics have complex dynamics. The internal pool of the receptor can appear on the cell surface by the underlying processes that regulate receptor trafficking (Fig. 3.11a). Upon surfacing, the antigen/receptor could remain on the cell membrane or undergo shedding and be released. The shed receptor could remain active or be devoid of any pharmacological activities. These dynamic processes could be influenced via interaction with their cognate ligands or modalities that address the target. For example, monovalent antibodies (antibodies with one binding site) may not be able to stabilize the receptor upon surfacing if the receptor has a fleeting existence on the surface of the cell. However, receptor stabilization may be achieved by a bivalent antibody (two binding sites) via the avidity effect making for a bivalent interaction with two receptors on the cell. Recently, we had the opportunity to examine the pharmacokinetic properties of an antibody with one binding

38 Shed 1-Arm Antibody 2.5 2.0 1.5 1.0 0.5 0.0 0.0 0.5 1.0 Dose (mg/kg) No Internalization Internalized TMC 2-Arm Antibody Shed site (one-arm) vs. one with two binding sites (one-arm) against a target with complex dynamics. Although it was not possible to evaluate the binding of the one-arm or two-arm antibodies in relevant blood cells in vitro, interesting pharmacokinetic properties were observed in vivo with respect to the target-mediated clearance (TMC) properties as shown in Fig. 3.11b. As highlighted previously, membrane-associated antigens that internalize can greatly enhance the antibody clearance through a target-mediated, specific process. The results reflected in Fig. 3.11b indicated that the one-arm antibody did not undergo TMC as evidenced by the linear clearance across a wide dose range; however, over a similar range of concentrations, the two-arm antibody clearance decreased by fourfold. Interestingly, there was no difference in the interaction of either the one-arm or two-arm antibodies with the shed antigen in serum as measured by a relevant bioanalytical method (i.e., determination of bound soluble antigen to either antibody). These results clearly reflect the importance of considering receptor properties and affinity in construct design when more than one target is being addressed. 3.8 Summary Evaluation of affinity design goals is a complex process contingent on many critical variables. Chapter 4: Epitope Characterization and Isotype Selection Gopalan Raghunathan, Edward Hsieh, and Grigori Ermakov

42 G. Raghunathan et al. project and identification of thousands of genes and proteins have triggered a new post-genomic era. The need to obtain critical information on structures of these proteins was enabled by the implementation of newer techniques in protein crystallography, NMR (nuclear magnetic resonance) and cryo electron microscopy. Moreover, the post-genomic period has witnessed not only refocusing of some older tools such as hydrogen-d euterium (HD) exchange and small-angle X-ray scattering but also the emergence of newer tools such as NGS (next-generation sequencing), display platforms, advanced protein arrays, and computational modeling and simulations. The emergence of these discoveries has been further facilitated by faster computer processors, expanded memory, increased storage capacity, and newer algorithms. The physical forces that govern interactions between two functional proteins are fundamentally different from those between protein homodimers. The energy terms at the two interfaces are different. Entropic forces between protein and water result in a hydrophobic interface when a protein forms a homodimer. The hydrophobic nature of the interface in homodimers is abundantly clear in several protein crystal structures. However, the interface between two functional proteins or that between an antibody and its targeted ligand (antigen) needs to be specific, and it is stabilized by polar, hydrogen bonds, and electrostatic interactions. An analysis of antigen-antibody complexes reveals that amino acids that interact at the interface are predominantly polar (Raghunathan et al. 2012). The region of the target protein that is recognized by another protein or by an antibody is termed the epitope. An epitope can also be viewed as that part of the ligand (an antigen) recognized by its receptor (i.e., B cells, T cells, antibodies, etc.). Regions of adjacent amino acids in a protein chain comprise a linear epitope. Regions of the protein separated in sequence but closer in space are considered as conformational epitopes. Antibodies bind with exquisite specificity to target proteins of interest. Characterization of multiple epitopes on an antigen provides critical information for developing new and improved therapeutics. From a commercial perspective, novel epitopes are critical for protecting the intellectual property and for discovery of new therapeutic modalities. This chapter discusses advances in the structural evaluation of protein-protein interactions, epitope identification relative to biological function, as well as critical considerations for drug design and intellectual property protection. 4.2 Structural Evaluation 4.2.1 X-Ray Crystallography Advances in X-ray crystallography and modern biology have a symbiotic relationship. Progress in biology has historically been strongly facilitated by structural information on (a) the early fiber diffraction data of KMEF (keratin, myosin, epidermin, and fibrinogen), coiled-coil proteins (see Astbury and Street 1932; Astbury and Bell 1938a, b); (b) the structure of the alpha helix (Pauling and Corey 1953); (c) the fiber diffraction-based double helix structure of DNA (Watson and Crick 1953); (d) the triple helical structure of collagen (Ramachandran and Kartha 1954); and (e) the

4 Epitope Characterization and Isotype Selection 43 X-ray crystal structures of hemoglobin (Kendrew et al. 1958) and myoglobin (Perutz et al. 1960). These studies together formed the foundation on which modern molecular and structural biology has evolved. Single crystal X-ray diffraction is the most powerful technique for obtaining detailed information on the atomic positions in molecules. The X-ray wavelength is in the order of 10−8 cm and is within the same order of magnitude as the atomic distances in molecules. In brief, scattering of X-rays by matter produces interference providing information on the structure of the scattering matter. In the electromagnetic field of X-rays, the electron begins to vibrate with the frequency of the field, and each electron becomes the starting point of scattered spherical waves. When the scattered waves are superimposed in various directions, they result in an interference pattern. An image is then formed by applying a Fourier transform of the amplitudes and phases of each reflection. The resulting image is the electron density distribution of the atoms within the unit cell of the crystal.1 Based on the foundations of physics and mathematics, the field of crystallography has significantly improved since the early days of spreading the electron density printouts on the table and using a plumb line to represent the third dimension. Advancements in several areas such as availability of powerful energy sources like synchrotron radiation, protein expression systems, automation in aiding conditions for crystallization, availability of structures of several single protein domains for use as templates for constructing larger assemblies, modern high-speed computers, and energy-based methods for structure refinements such as X-plore (Brünger et al. 1987) have significantly contributed to the study of large protein assemblies and protein-protein interactions. As a result, a protein crystal structure is considered the gold standard for obtaining critical structural topographies and features. 4.2.2 NMR and Epitope Mapping Nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy, most commonly known as NMR spectroscopy, is a technique that exploits the magnetic properties of certain atomic nuclei. NMR is based on the precession of atomic nuclei in an external magnetic field and the time taken for the spin states to return to the thermal equilibrium state. Some atoms/isotopes such as 2H, 13C, 15N, and 31P have a magnetic field. For instance, a proton (1H) has a magnetic moment with two spin states α and β in the presence of an external magnetic field; the lower energy state is slightly more populated. When a pulse of electromagnetic radiation in the form of an RF (radio frequency) pulse, with a strength equaling the difference between the α and β states, is applied to the spinning proton, resonance is achieved. The different frequencies, termed chemical shifts, depend on the chemical environment of the proton and are 1 Sir William Lawrence Bragg received the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1915 jointly with Sir William Henry Bragg “for their services in the analysis of crystal structure by means of X-rays”; Bragg’s law makes it possible to calculate the positions of the atoms within a crystal from the way in which an X-ray beam is diffracted by the crystal lattice.

44 G. Raghunathan et al. measured in ppm (parts per million) relative to the shift of a standard. Several factors contribute to the observed shifts including time and the nature of the nuclei (i.e., proton, carbon, nitrogen, phosphorus, etc.). Chemical shifts have been conventionally used since the early days for characterizing small molecules. In addition, coupling constants can provide the torsional angle for a set of four consecutive atoms. The power of NMR for protein structure determination witnessed significant advances in the mid-1980s. Between 1982 and 1984, Kurt Wuthrich pioneered structural determination of proteins in solution by publishing several seminal papers (e.g., see Williamson et al. 1985). These studies were followed by the structure determination of tendamistat (an alpha-amylase inhibitor) independently using X-ray crystallography by Huber and coworkers and using NMR by Wuthrich and coworkers. Both structures were virtually identical. Kurt Wuthrich was awarded the Nobel Prize in Chemistry in 2002 for “development of nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy for determining the three-dimensional structure of biological macro molecules in solution.” Additionally, several computational advances enabled significant breakthroughs which led to the application of NMR for determination of protein structure(s). One important development in NMR is NOESY (nuclear Overhauser enhancement spectroscopy). This approach offered a new method to measure the transfer of magnetization from one nucleus to another when sequentially apart but close in space. As this effect is proportional to the inverse sixth power of the distance between the two nuclei, the accuracy is reduced significantly if the distance between two nuclei exceeds 5 Å. The protein chemical shifts help determine which residues are close in space. In addition to NOESY, multidimensional NMR (transfer of magnetization between nuclei of different types) and simulated annealing for structural interpretation of NMR data have made determination of protein structures in solution a reality. NMR has also been used to study structural interactions between proteins and has offered a faster alternative for epitope mapping when compared to X-ray crystallography. Isotope labeling has been used to reduce the complexity of NMR signals. Often 2H (deuterium), 13C, and 15N isotopes are used. In a 15N heteronuclear single-quantum coherence (HSQC) experiment, the position of 15N is highly sensitive to protein conformation. The method for epitope mapping is illustrated in Fig. 4.1. Initially, the spectrum of the unbound labeled protein is recorded. Next the spectrum of the complex of the labeled protein and the unlabeled antibody is captured. Since the antibody is not labeled, its residues do not produce any NMR signal. Residues that exhibit a change in spectrum correspond to those on the protein that interact with the antibody. Since in the bound state the antigen is protected from solvent, binding of the antibody to protein results in a reduced exchange rate. NMR has been used to map the epitopes of several proteins. For example, it has been applied to screen potential ligands for Derf2, the group 2 major allergen of the house dust mite (Ichikawa et al. 2009). Epitopes of allergens are mostly observed in the flexible regions, which are often not observed with a high degree of confidence in crystallography. NMR can be uniquely used in such situations for allergens.

4 Epitope Characterization and Isotype Selection 45 A A N15 N15 B B H1 H1 B B A A N15 labeled N15 labeled

Fig. 4.1 Schematic illustration of epitope mapping by NMR. On the left bottom panel, residues A and B of a 15N-labeled protein antigen are shown on the structure. They lead to generation of a single peak each in a 15N-HSQC experiment as shown on the left top panel. Upon antibody binding (shown on right bottom panel), epitope residues, such as B, are in a different chemical environment. Consequently, the NMR signal of residue B is altered as shown on the right top panel. The antibody is unlabeled, and its signals do not appear in 15N-HSQCs. By comparing the antigen-free and antigen-bound NMR spectra, epitope residues can be identified from the changes in their signals (Figure adapted from Bardelli et al. 2014) 4.2.3 Comparisons Between X-Ray and NMR Both X-ray crystallography and NMR have been used to study the structure and dynamics of proteins. High-resolution crystal structure of an antigen-antibody complex provides detailed description of the interface at the atomic level and is still considered the gold standard for epitope-paratope delineation. X-ray crystallography provides a space and time averaged snapshot of atomic positions and hence does not capture the motions of the flexible regions of proteins in solution where atoms exhibit larger thermal motions. Crystallographers assign B factors to characterize the motion of atoms; however, the uncertainty in the placement of loops is often reflected in large B factors for these regions. NMR provides both an average of the ensemble structure and also individual structures. The average structures in X-ray crystallography is over a much longer time scale (i.e., hours) than in NMR (i.e., seconds). Crystallography needs relatively large quantities of proteins to be expressed and purified and requires crystals that diffract well. Furthermore, crystal

46 G. Raghunathan et al. symmetry and molecular packing can have an effect on the observed structure, particularly in local regions. An NMR-based epitope mapping method is significantly faster than X-ray crystallography and requires no need for obtaining quality crystals that diffract well. Protein sizes larger than 200 residues may not be suitable for NMR. Development of newer techniques such as transverse relaxation-optimized spectroscopy (TROSY), a version of 15N-HSQC, has helped in characterization of larger proteins (Bardelli et al. 2014). Other criteria for NMR analysis are availability of several milligrams of pure protein at mM (millimolar) concentrations with isotopic labeling. 4.2.4 Computational Methods 4.2.4.1 Computational Approaches for Peptide Mapping Computational methods are essential for structure determination of proteins by crystallography and/or NMR. In the case of crystallography, an observed diffraction pattern is a Fourier transform of the spatial coordinates of the atoms. In the case of NMR, several computational methods are used to obtain structural features based on measurements such as coupling constants, NOE (nuclear Overhauser effect), or multidimensional NMR. Molecular mechanics and molecular dynamics based on software packages such as CHARMM (Brooks et al. 1983) and AMBER (Weiner et al. 1984), and techniques for simulated annealing and conformational sampling, have significantly aided the determination of protein structures and consequently for the characterization of epitopes. 4.2.4.2 Computational Approaches for B-Cell Epitope Prediction Computational methods have been used for predicting B-cell epitopes. There have been several reports on the use of sequences and structures/models to predict potential epitope regions. An example of such a method is described by Rubinstein et al. (2008, 2009). In this machine learning approach, antigen-antibody complex structures and antigen sequences were used to train and recognize several immunogenic properties based on a set of physicochemical, structural, and geometrical properties. In the case of structures, these investigators used patches for water accessible sites with a typical patch consisting of 20 sequence neighbors. For every patch and in relation to the overall protein, several properties were included in this computational approach. Properties used included secondary structure, solvent accessibility, relative accessibility of the patch with respect to a larger probe of 9 Å to approximate the antibody CDR, average curvature of patch atoms, and hydrophobicity/ hydrophilicity based on previously available data. They report a success rate of 70% for predicting epitopes. 4.2.4.3 Computational Protein Docking Protein-protein interactions are essential for molecular recognition and in regulation of signaling in biological systems. Computational docking of two proteins is a notoriously difficult problem. Protein interfaces are vastly diverse, and capturing all

4 Epitope Characterization and Isotype Selection 47 of the underlying interactions at the molecular level is challenging. The interfaces between an antibody and an antigen are mostly stabilized by polar and electrostatic interactions. The docking challenges are further complicated by the flexibility inherently associated with the binding regions, such as with conformational epitopes or CDR loops in the antibodies. Thus, the degree of success for docking methods can vary significantly depending on the resolution needed for the understanding of the protein interactions. In most methods, the starting point for a target protein is either a crystal structure or a theoretical model constructed based on a crystal structure of a template protein with a high degree of sequence homology to the target. Several groups have developed methods for studying protein-protein interactions using docking methods. In most cases, geometry and energy are used as target functions that are minimized to arrive at the most stable low-energy structure. Conventional molecular mechanics utilizes energy functions such as van der Waals forces, electrostatics, hydrogen bonding, and solvation. The accuracy of these methods depends on the algorithms used for sampling the 3D space and the success rate of the energy terms used for rank ordering of the docked structures. For such methods to provide realistic solutions, availability of a large amount of computer time is a prerequisite. In the case of antibody-antigen docking, the search space is limited to the CDR regions. Protein-protein docking is typically performed in multiple stages. Initially, placement of the second protein with respect to the first protein is explored by rigid body transformations. At this stage, even a reduced representation considering only the backbone atoms of the protein should suffice to obtain low-resolution docked poses. In the next stage, more resolution is added by exploring allowed side-chain conformations and finally by adding flexibility to the backbone structures as well. Side-chain placements are explored by Monte Carlo sampling of side-chain rotamer distributions. A typical rotamer distribution is reported by Dunbrack and Karplus (1993). Backbone sampling is significantly reduced by applying the limited allowed regions in a Ramachandran map (Ramachandran et al. 1963). Over the years, several methods for docking have been developed (Kuntz et al. 1982; Sandak et al. 1998a, b; Halperin et al. 2002, 2008; Gray et al. 2003; Gray 2006; Kozakov et al. 2006; Andrusier et al. 2007; Vakser and Kundrotas 2008; Pierce et al. 2014). A short review of methods is outlined by Carvalloza et al. (2012). Due to challenges in protein docking, several groups have collaborated to establish an international forum— CAPRI, Critical Assessment of Predicted Interactions (CAPRI; Janin et al. 2003) (http://www.ebi.ac.uk/msd-srv/capri/)—to test and compare joint docking prediction experiments using various algorithms. In the CAPRI project for 2014, 25 targets, for which the complex structures were available but not yet published, were given to various research groups. The predicted structures were evaluated vis-à-vis crystal structures using standard CAPRI assessment protocols. Detailed results are available on the CAPRI website (URL: http://www.ebi.ac.uk/msd-srv/capri/). Over the years, the performance of automated docking servers has significantly improved. While ab initio protein-protein docking is still an academically challenging endeavor, it can be useful for drug discovery and molecular engineering if guided by constraints of proximal pairs of residues on the two proteins. A pair may consist of

48 G. Raghunathan et al. the binding residue on the antibody (paratope) and the corresponding region/residue on the antigen (epitope) that the antibody recognizes. Information on interacting residue pairs can be obtained from approaches such as hydrogen-deuterium exchange, NMR, or site-directed mutagenesis. When such pairwise constraints are available, the problem is much more manageable and can lead to reliable docking models for further rational design and testing. When a high-quality docked model is available, residues at the interface can be substituted, and the conformations can be sampled to arrive at energetically favorable substitutions at the interface. This “virtual screening” strategy of computational protein design is highly useful for rationally guided selective substitutions or for generating combinatorial libraries for selecting binders using a display system. For instance, one of the authors (see Davis et al. 2008) used such an approach for successfully redesigning the dimer interface of activin A, a member of the TGF beta family that plays a critical role in stem cell differentiation. The designed variants result in expression of SOX17, a marker for differentiation. 4.2.5 Hydrogen-Deuterium Exchange (HDE) Mass Spectrometry (MS) Hydrogen-deuterium exchange mass spectrometry (HDX-MS) is a technique to monitor protein conformation and structural dynamics. The technique is based on the ability of amide hydrogens on the protein backbone to exchange with deuterium in the surrounding solvent. Early experiments with hydrogen-deuterium exchange detected changes in deuterium labeling with methods such as density gradients (Hvidt and Linderstrom-Lang 1954), infrared spectroscopy (Haggis 1957), and proton NMR (Dubs et al. 1979). HDX-MS is an extension of these experiments with modern protein mass spectrometry technology as a detection method. A number of factors influence the hydrogen-deuterium exchange rate for an amide hydrogen atom. These include solvent accessibility, hydrogen bonding, labeling time, temperature, and pH (Akasaka et al. 1975; Hvidt and Nielsen 1966; Woodward et al. 1982). In an HDX-MS experiment, the labeling time, temperature, and pH can be controlled. The remaining factors are structural properties of the protein that are altered when it is bound in a complex. The specific hydrogens being exchanged are the amide backbone hydrogens because their rate of exchange is in a detectable range with modern methods. Hydrogens covalently bonded to carbon will not exchange at any meaningful rate, and side-chain hydrogens will exchange too quickly to be measured (Hamuro et al. 2003). In a typical HDX-MS experiment, the protein of interest is incubated in a buffer with deuterium oxide in excess (Fig. 4.2). After a given time, the sample labeling is quenched by the addition of a cold quenching buffer that lowers the pH and temperature. The sample is prepared for LC-MS/MS analysis by digesting the proteins into peptides using a low-pH compatible protease such as pepsin. The peptides are separated over a reverse-phase chromatography column and ionized by electrospray into the mass spectrometer. The percentage of amide hydrogens labeled in a peptide

49 D2O exchange with target protein Quenching and DO Enzymatic labe 2 ling digestion Peptide 1 Protein Deuterium exchanges on 100 Protein X Protein X:mAb amide backbone Analysis by 80 hydrogens Reverse Phase LC- D2O exchange with target and antibody MS DO 2 labeling Time Antibody can be determined by measuring the difference between the observed and the theoretical unlabeled mass. For an epitope mapping experiment, a comparison is made between the labeling of the antigen-alone sample and the antigen/antibody complex. The differences observed will be due to blocking of solvent accessibility by

the antibody or allosteric conformational changes caused by the binding. Crucial to the wider adoption of HDX-MS has been advances in automation and software that have removed much of the tedium in HDX-MS. Automated sample preparation and loading enable reproducible loading volumes and labeling times, which are critical to reducing variability in experiments where sample volumes are often low and labeling times may only be a few seconds. The number of peptides that are measured in an HDX-MS experiment can vary from dozens to hundreds depending on the size of the proteins and their amenability to protease digestion, and an experiment may have multiple replicates and time points, resulting in thousands of data points that need to be extracted. Automated software analysis is crucial to improving the throughput of these experiments. A unique aspect of HDX-MS is the reversible nature of the deuterium label on the amide backbone. This presents a challenge because the steps following labeling dilute the deuterium buffer with hydrogen and given enough time, all deuterium labeled sites will back-exchange with hydrogen. The low pH and temperature quenching step after labeling reduces the rate of back-exchange by several orders of magnitude, but the back-exchange does not completely cease (Englander and Poulsen 1969). The quenching conditions themselves also present a challenge as there are very few proteases characterized that are effective at low pH and low temperature. The most common protease in use is pepsin, though some research has tueD % 4 Epitope Characterization and Isotype Selection 60 40 Quenching and 20 Enzymatic 0 digestion 30s 300s 1500s4500s9000s13500sFull-D Hydrogens protected from solvent exposure will exchange at a reduced rate Fig. 4.2 Overview of a hydrogen-deuterium exchange mass spectrometry method for epitope mapping. The antigen of interest is incubated in deuterium containing buffer. The labeling is quenched by adding a chilled quench buffer that lowers the pH and temperature of the sample. The labeled protein sample is then digested into peptides, and the resulting peptides are analyzed by LC-MS to measure the level of deuterium labeling on the peptide. An additional labeling experiment is done with the antigen in complex with the antibody. The data for each peptide can be presented as a deuterium labeling plot that shows the deuterium incorporation over time for the two experimental conditions. The epitope is determined by identifying peptides that show lower deuterium incorporation when the antibody is present

50 G. Raghunathan et al. been done to identify and optimize additional compatible proteases (Cravello et al. 2003; Zhang et al. 2008). The method described above can be referred to as “bottom-up HDX-MS,” in reference to “bottom-up proteomics,” where the experiment is based on the proteolytic digestion and analysis of peptides. An alternative method called “top-down HDX-MS” excludes the proteolytic digestion and instead relies on gas-phase fragmentation of the protein (Abzalimov et al. 2009). Deuterium labeling levels are determined by measuring the masses of the resulting fragments. This method offers the advantage of circumventing the proteolytic digestion and the surrounding back-exchange and enzyme efficiency concerns. The disadvantages are that protein sequence coverage by gas-phase fragmentation decreases as the protein size increases and posttranslational modifications can make analysis difficult due to additional masses that must be accounted for. In an ideal scenario, the only labeling changes observed would be from blocking the solvent accessibility of the epitope by the antibody. In actuality, binding by the antibody can cause allosteric effects throughout the antigen that affects deuterium exchange levels (Bereszczak et al. 2013; Pandit et al. 2012). This can make epitope determination difficult as there is no way to distinguish between labeling changes caused by direct antibody binding or allosteric conformational changes. While HDX-MS is a powerful technique for epitope mapping, in these cases additional data from orthogonal technologies are useful in elucidating the correct epitope. 4.2.6 Other Relevant Methods Crystallography, NMR, and HD-MS approaches in combination with computational modeling provide direct platforms to map epitopes in atomic detail. Alternate approaches have also been used. A comparison of the different methods for epitope mapping is shown in Table 4.1. One such approach is site-directed mutagenesis (SDM) where alanine substitutions are introduced systematically at several sites and individual variants are cloned and expressed in cells and then tested in assays. The process is laborious and time-consuming. The mutations that increase or decrease binding are mapped onto the structure of the protein in question (Sidhu et al. 2003; Wells 1991; Sutherland and Maynard 2009). Based on the molecular structure, a set of solvent exposed residues are targeted for a combinatorial library. In another approach, peptide scanning has been used for identifying linear epitopes. In some cases, the peptides are displayed on the surface of microorganisms (Christmann et al. 2001; Petersen et al. 1995). Often overlapping peptides are used for scanning. Rockberg et al. (2008) described a method wherein the gene of an antigen is fragmented and the resultant peptide libraries are displayed on a Grampositive bacterium, Staphylococcus carnosus, and the displayed libraries are directly FACS sorted (Rockberg et al. 2008). More recently, Van Blarcom et al. (2015) used a combination of rational library design, yeast display, and NGS (next-generation sequencing) to map epitopes of several antibodies that neutralize alpha toxin from Staphylococcus aureus.

For example, Poljak and coworkers (Lescar et al. 1995) demonstrated how two antibodies with significant variations in their CDR loop sequences and conformations can recognize the same epitope on lysozyme (see Fig. 4.3). This work demonstrated the tolerability in the surface recognition features while preserving the underlying atomic interactions necessary for recognition of the helix. In this case, a particular α-helix on lysozyme with the hydrophilic amino acids on one side of the helix is presumably the dominant recognition element and is recognized by two antibodies which differ in sequence. 4.3.2 Different Epitopes on the Same Protein: Impact on Pharmacokinetics The converse of the previous observation, namely, two antibodies recognizing two different conformational epitopes has also been observed. CD27 is a co-stimulatory protein belonging to the TNF receptor family and contains several cysteine-rich domains (CRD). CD27 is expressed on B cells, mature dendritic cells, and activated T cells. Recently, Gilliland and coworkers (Obmolova et al. 2017)

Fig. 4.3 (a, b) Comparison of two antibodies for lysozyme (Lescar et al. 1995). Left panel is for the antibody F9.13.7-GEL (pdb 1fbi), and the right panel is for the antibody HyHEL10-HEL (pdb 3d9a) complex with lysozyme. Antibody light and heavy chains are shown in cyan and green. Lysozyme is shown in magenta. The dominant recognition element on lysosome, the alpha helix (yellow), is recognized by two antibodies which differ in sequence (a vs. b) Fig. 4.4 Two antibodies recognizing two different epitopes of a target (Obmolova et al. 2017). Ribbon representation of the 2177 crystal structure (pdb code 5tlj) of CD27 ECD (yellow) in complex with two Fabs, M2177 (cyan) and M2191 (purple) 2191 CRD1 CRD2 Membrane proximal C-terminal reported the crystal structure of a complex of two antibodies bound to CD27 (Fig. 4.4). The two antibodies, 2177 and 2191, recognize distinctly different regions of CD27 with comparable affinities. However, the two antibodies showed significantly different pharmacokinetic (PK) profiles (Obmolova et al. 2017). Antibody 2177 cleared much faster than clone 2191. The authors attributed the faster clearance of 2177 to the pI being close to that of the FcRn (pI = 6.0), resulting in electrostatic repulsion and impeding recycling. However, this hypothesis is contrary to observations reported previously (Igawa et al. 2011). The observed pharmacokinetic differences between the two antibodies may also be attributed to the distinct epitopes they recognize (epitope interaction and correlation with

4 Epitope Characterization and Isotype Selection 53 receptor internalization discussed in Chap. 10). As reported by Obmolova et al. (2017), antibody H2177 interacts with the first CRD of CD27, distal from the cell surface, while clone 2191 recognizes the second CRD (Fig. 4.4). It is possible that the impact of target-mediated clearance processes is correlated to epitope recognition sites. Since the authors have only examined a single dose of each antibody in vivo, the merit of this hypothesis cannot be examined (discussed in Chap. 10). Additionally, as the authors elaborated, interferences with CD27 and the role for trimer-complex formation may be another underlying factor attributing to this observation as the cytoplasmic tails of TNF-related family members (such as CD27) are involved in formation of an internal trimeric complex and association with signaling proteins (discussed in Chap. 3). 4.3.3 Different Epitopes on the Same Protein: Impact on Neutralizing Activity Interleukin-1β is well known for its role in inflammation and immune response. It forms a signaling complex with IL-1R type I (IL-1R1) and interleukin-1 receptor accessory protein (IL-1RAcP). Blech et al. (2013) reported crystal structures of two antibodies that bind to two different epitopes on IL-1β. Figure 4.5a, b compares the binding of canakinumab and gevokizumab forming complexes with IL-1β. The two antibodies recognize different regions of IL-1β. Canakinumab makes more extensive contacts with IL-1β than Gevokizumab. The difference in the epitopes recognized by these two antibodies offers variations in the mechanisms for altering the IL-1β pathway. Canakinumab neutralizes IL-1β and precludes the IL-1β: IL-1R interaction. Gevokizumab acts as a regulatory modulator reducing the affinity of IL-1β to IL-1R1, resulting in reduced signaling of the IL-1R1 and IL-1RAcP pathways. The authors also used NMR for studying the epitopes recognized by the two antibodies. There is consistency in the epitopes between NMR and crystal structures for both the antibody-IL-1β complexes. Very little change is observed in the structure of IL-1β following binding to either canakinumab or gevokizumab (Fig. 4.6). The variations observed in the exposed loops are normal and expected. The antibodies by binding to IL-1β inhibit the association of IL-1β to form the IL1-β: IL-1R1: IL-1RAcP complex. This ternary complex is shown in Fig. 4.7a,b. The epitopes recognized by canakinumab and gevokizumab are in different regions of IL-1β. Presumably, canakinumab interacts strongly enough and at the correct epitope on IL-1β to totally block binding to IL-1R. Gevokizumab binds to a relatively much smaller region of IL-1β. Canakinumab binds to a spatially distinct epitope on IL-1β and also interacts with a portion of IL-1R1. The authors have performed binding experiments in the presence and absence of soluble IL-1R1. Canakinumab does not bind to the IL-1β-sIL-1R1 complex. Gevokizumab shows a 25-fold reduction in binding to IL-1β. This example highlights the need for selection of the pharmacologically appropriate epitope to address the desired functional properties.

Coordinates of pdb files 4g6j and 4g6m are used for the complexes of canakinumab and gevokizumab, respectively 4.3.4 Structural Diversity: Implications for Receptor Recognition Ligands responsible for cell signaling bind to receptors on the cellular surface and trigger intracellular signaling. These ligands either bind to monomer or multimeric receptors. There are several variations in the themes of ligand-receptor binding and functional activation. The ligand binding to monomers often promotes dimerization, introducing several atomic contacts between the monomers. For example, in the case of EGFR (epidermal growth factor receptor), it was shown that while ligand binding promotes EGFR dimerization, dimerization does not increase ligand binding (Bessman et al. 2014). A more rigorous understanding of ligand and receptor interactions and identification of relevant epitopes can aid in optimal design and development of therapeutics. Crystal structures of EGF and EGFR complexes (Burgess et al. 2003) have helped further the knowledge of binding and have formalized the mechanism of interaction. Crystal structures have also enabled subsequent experiments to be proposed that probe in greater detail the molecular specifics of the interaction. Another area of current importance is immuno-oncology, wherein regulatory T-cell pathways are targeted. T-cell responses are modulated (either enhanced or suppressed) by the interaction of molecules on APCs (antigen- presenting cells) and receptors on T cells and are further aided by antigen- independent co- stimulatory signals involving receptor-ligand interactions. Many of the co-stimulatory molecules belong to the TNFR (tumor necrosis factor receptor) superfamily.

56 G. Raghunathan et al. TNFR-like receptors are type 1 transmembrane proteins with elongated structures and contain a few cysteine-rich domains held by disulfide bonds with protruding small loops. The ligands in the cleaved soluble mature form have

“jellyroll” β sandwiches. They have hydrophobic regions on their surface that promote a trimeric ligand. The receptor forms 3:3 complex with the ligand. The interaction between the ligands and receptors is highly promiscuous with ligands/ receptors recognizing more than one partner. The TNF family of ligand and receptor interactions can influence T-cell responses in several ways. A few of the many functions include T-cell activation, inflammation, and innate immunity. Characterization of the interactions and protein engineering can influence their co-stimulatory activities with altered functions. Hence, one would surmise that the site of interaction of the ligand and receptors for other families can be deduced based on the crystal structure of one of the members of this family. Several crystal structures of the apo form of this family of ligands have been determined. A cursory glance of Fig. 4.8 would reveal that the packing among the ligands and the length of the loops connecting the core beta strands are very different among the individual members of this family. This example highlights the structural diversity among ligands belonging to the same family. Also, it demonstrates the role of “convergent evolution” in arriving at a common structural folding motif for the monomers and trimeric assembly for this family of ligands. individual domains, the latter in turn predisposing the epitopes on the ligand for recognition by their cognate receptors. 4.3.5 Blocking Antibodies Monoclonal antibodies are capable of blocking receptor-ligand interactions and may act through competitive and noncompetitive mechanisms. Competitive inhibitors usually recognize epitopes overlapping with the natural ligand or receptor binding site. Monoclonal antibodies blocking immune checkpoints have demonstrated unprecedented therapeutic benefits in clinical trials and provided a major breakthrough in oncology (Lee et al. 2016). Well-known examples of antibodies that block receptor-ligand interactions that are approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) include ipilimumab (blocks CTLA-4), pembrolizumab (blocks PD-1), nivolumab (blocks PD-1), and atezolizumab (blocks PD-L1). They have proven to be efficacious for the treatment of melanoma, non-small cell lung cancer, renal cell carcinoma, Hodgkin lymphoma, and bladder cancer (Lee et al. 2016). Figure 4.9 shows epitopes on the immune checkpoint molecule PD1 recognized by the natural ligand PD-L1, pembrolizumab, and nivolumab (Lee et al. 2016). Figure 4.10 shows epitopes on CTLA-4, another immune checkpoint molecule, recognized by the natural ligands B7-1 and B7-2 and the competitive blocker tremelimumab (Lee et al. 2016). Selection of these types of molecules is even more challenging than blocking anti-bodies and often involves accurate epitope mapping and rigorous screening in functional assays. Epitope mapping has helped to bridge mouse and human studies in developing MK-4166, a humanized agonist monoclonal antibody against human GITR (Sukumar et al. 2017). GITR, a member of the TNFR superfamily, provides co-stimulatory signals to T cells leading to enhanced cellular and humoral immunity. The agonist anti-mouse GITR antibody DTA-1 has been shown to have robust antitumor effects in murine models and primarily acts by attenuation of Tregmediated immune suppression. To explore the potential utility of GITR agonists in cancer immunotherapy, MK-4166 was developed to bind to an epitope analogous to that of DTA-1. The amino acid residues important for DTA1 and MK-4166 binding to murine GITR and human GITR, respectively, were determined by site-directed mutagenesis. An alignment of the region containing these amino acids and the key residues identified as important for binding are shown in Fig. 4.11. It was determined that six out of the seven key residues were different in mouse as compared to humans and when these were replaced with corresponding residues from human GITR, MK-4166 was able to bind to the modified mouse GITR (Fig. 4.11). Similar to the effects of DTA-1 on mouse lymphocytes, MK-4166 enhanced the proliferation of both naive and tumor infiltrating T lymphocytes (TILs). MK-4166 induced phosphorylation of NFkB and increased expression of DUSP6 in TILs, indicating that MK-4166 can signal through GITR via the NFkB and Erk signaling pathways. MK-4166 offers an alternative immune-modulatory mechanism to the

The safety and tolerability of MK-4166 in patients with advanced solid tumors is currently being evaluated in a phase I study (NCT02132754). 4.3.7 Action at a Distance: Effect of Fc Isotypes on Antigen- Binding Affinity Antibodies have dual functions. The function of the variable region is to bind to antigen, and the constant region is responsible for effector functions such as complement activation and interaction with Fc receptors (discussed in Chap. 2 and First Edition). The “Y”-shaped antibody structure separates the antigen-binding region from the regions responsible for effector function by a distance significantly large for direct interaction between the two regions. Hence, one would expect that isotype switching (variations in constant regions) leads to differences in effector activity with no change in affinity to antigen. Our conceptual understanding of the interplay between the constant and variable region has been influenced by the pioneering study of Benacerraf et al. (1959) that showed antigen-bound antibody is cleared faster from serum than the free antibody. This observation can be rationalized by either of the two fundamental underlying mechanisms: (a) either the binding to antigen introduces allosteric changes in the antibody which strengthens the mn 504 DO STBA 4 Epitope Characterization and Isotype Selection 1.0 0.5 0.0 10000 1000 100 10 1 GITR variant conc. (ng/ml) Fig. 4.11 Epitopes of DTA1 and 36E5. (a) Partial sequence alignment of the mouse and human GITR. Residues critical for DTA1 binding to mouse GITR and MK-4166 binding to human GITR are boxed. Residue numbering is according to Nocentini et al. (1997). (b) Binding of MK-4166 (36E5.A5) to modified mouse GITR (GTR80) and other GITR variants as determined by ELISA. 60 G. Raghunathan et al. interaction of the constant regions to FcRs and/or (b) FcR cross-linking improves the affinity of the antibody to its antigen. As discussed subsequently, a few studies have shown that isotype changes in the constant region impact the binding of the V region to antigen. If the constant domain alters or modulates antigen binding, it offers an alternative mechanism to improve affinity of antibodies by simple isotype switching. It is valuable to explore and understand the fundamental structural basis for how the change in constant region brings about a change in affinity. One of the causes could be due to differences in the hinge region lengths, the location and number of disulfide bonds in the hinge, and the potential differences in elbow motion possibly due to isotype variations. Alternatively, the difference may come from the relative orientations of the variable region with respect to CH1 and CL. Yet another possibility could be due to changes in the CH1 domain and potential structural variations in some exposed loops in this domain. An early demonstration of the impact of the hinge was by Horgan et al. (1993) who observed that hinge deletion in IgG4 improved the affinity of an antibody. The influence of the C region on the V region and altering binding and specificity will have a direct impact on the selection of isotypes for designing therapeutic antibodies and vaccines. Pritsch et al. (1996) observed that IgA1 and IgG1 human antibodies IgA1PER and k IgG1PER with identical VH and VL sequences bound tubulin with significantly difk ferent affinities. IgA1PER binds with an affinity of 2.85 × 10−8 M, whereas IgG1PER k k binds with an affinity of 1.02 × 10−6 M. Affinity values are 3.29 × 10−8 M for F(ab′)2 and 6.04 × 10−8 M for Fab from IgA1PER. The authors could not measure correk sponding values for IgG1PER since association rates were too slow for measurek ment. The authors attribute the differences in binding of the isotypes for the full-length antibodies to structural differences in the CH1 domain. Tudor et al. (2012) have also shown that the affinity and specificity of an antibody is altered by the CH1 region. They used a broadly neutralizing anti-HIV antibody 2F5, a human IgG1, and its IgA2 counterpart sharing the same sequences for the VH, VL, and CL regions. By screening a random peptide library, they showed that IgG1 binds not only to the 2F5 epitope (LDKWA) located on the gp41 helix, which is proximal to the membrane, but also to an additional spatially proximal region (gp41 N-helix with sequence SMTLTVQAR). In contrast, 2F5 IgA2 recognizes a different epitope (NYTSLIHSL) located on the N-terminal of the C-helix. This region is nearly 300 Å away, too far to be recognized by the same antibody Fab. The authors conclude that the CH1 region is the cause for the variations in the epitopes recognized by IgG1 and IgA2 versions of 2F5. However, it is not clear if the structural changes are limited to the Fab region or if they are extended through the hinge to the constant regions. The thermodynamic analysis of 2F5 IgG and Fab by Crespillo et al. (2013) directly addressed this issue. They measured the thermodynamic properties of 2F5 in the Fab and IgG formats. Using isothermal titration calorimetry (ITC), they report a nearly 60-fold increase in affinity for IgG over Fab for a variant (N16N peptide). In this case, binding Gibbs energy for the mAb has decreased by −2.4 kcal/mol. Binding enthalpies are comparable with the Fab favored slightly by −0.8 kcal/mol. The authors attribute the difference to the

4 Epitope Characterization and Isotype Selection 61 uncomplexed Fab being more flexible and becoming more organized with a larger decrease in conformational flexibility on complex formation when compared to the mAb. Normally, Fab and full-length mAb have similar affinities. Accuracy of affinity measurements depends on factors such as changes in structure potentially introduced by processes used for cleaving a Fab from a mAb. Similar results have been obtained on isotypes of murine antibodies. Torres et al. (2007) have retained the variable region of a mouse antibody 3E5 IgG3 for Cryptococcus neoformans glucuronoxylomannan (GXM) and studied variations of the constant regions with mouse IgG1, IgG2a, and IgG2b using ELISA, SPR, and ITC. They find differences in binding affinities among the different isotypes to the 12 amino acid peptides mimetic of GXM, although the interactions have relatively low binding affinity. These mouse antibody studies further confirm an allosteric effect of the C region on the variable region. Blake et al. (2005) have studied allosteric binding of antigens to antibodies. They have observed allosteric behavior in 5–10% of the nearly 250 antibodies that they studied. In some cases, when one of the two binding sites on the antibody for an antigen is occupied, the affinity for the other binding site is increased. When a third molecule binds to an antibody in the constant region, they observe a change in affinity of the antibody to its antigen. The third molecule studied included polyclonal Fabs and protein G, both of which bind to the Fc region and a covalent modification of an antibody. Most of the antigens in their study were small molecules. Other recent studies have reported similar effects of the V-region binding difference among variations in isotypes (Tomaras et al. 2013; Dodev et al. 2003). Sela-Culang et al. (2012) analyzed the crystal structures of 49 antibodies in free and antigen-bound states. They observed that upon binding, antibodies undergo structural changes in a loop in the CH1 region and the elbow angle between the variable and constant regions. A comparison of the constant and hinge regions of human IgG1, IgG2, IgG3, and IgG4 antibodies is shown in Fig. 4.12. Amino acids differ only in a few sites in each domain. However, significant variations occur in the hinge regions both in the lengths and in the number of disulfide bonds. The isotypes also differ in the disulfide bond that connects the light chain and the heavy chain. The pattern is similar in IgG2, IgG3, and IgG4, all of which differ from IgG1. One would anticipate the flexibility or rigidity of the hinge region would lead to less structural variations among the isotypes in the order IgG3 > IgG2 > IgG4 > IgG1. There is only one crystal structure of a full-length human IgG antibody (hIgG1) in the pdb database (1hzh). Unfortunately, key regions of the molecule in the hinge and a loop in CH1 region are missing. These regions were modeled. A portion of this antibody covering the CH1 and CL regions of one of the fab arms and the two hinges is shown in Fig. 4.13. A loop on CH1 (shown as sticks) is near the hinge and proximal to the light chain constant region. Hence it is quite likely that changes in the isotypes are communicated to the distal variable region via the differences in hinge lengths, disulfide bonds in the hinge, and conformational changes in the CH1 loop and interaction of the latter with the hinge. These structural

Fig. 4.13 CL:CH1 interface and a portion of the disulfide-bonded hinge. CL is shown in green and CH1 in yellow. Hinge region is shown as cyan and yellow lines. HC1 and HC2 denote heavy chains 1 and 2 respectively. The loop of CH1 shown in stick representation is close to the hinge and can impact the relative orientation of constant and variable regions changes presumably result in changes in elbow angles between the constant and variable domains. It may be further noted that while there are very few sequence variations in the CH1 domains, IgG1 and IgG2 differ at two positions (S → N and L → F) and both are non-conserved substitutions with the smaller amino acids in IgG1 replaced by bulkier ones in IgG2. While one or two amino acids may not make a significant difference in an IgG domain, the stereo chemistry and the local environment (a loop in CH1) are very critical and can add to the other changes in the local region of the tripartite interaction involving this CH1 loop, hinge region, and constant region of the light chain. While it may not be achievable with all antibodies, isotype switching can afford an alternative mechanism for altering the affinity of an antibody to the antigen. 4.4 Epitopes and Intellectual Property Therapeutic antibodies are highly specific in recognition of their targets and hence have superior function. They have recently become a rich source of revenue for biopharmaceutical companies. Hence there is a compelling necessity for patenting and protecting any intellectual property. In general, a patent’s broad claim is for an antibody that binds to a target and alters its function. Since the completion of the sequencing of the human genome and with the advent of NGS (next-generation sequencing), it is becoming increasingly more difficult to patent naturally occurring proteins unless there are some unique exceptions, such as difficulty in obtaining the protein or a highly unique function for the protein. Likewise, since the methods for production of antibodies are routine processes, patenting an antibody requires proving sufficient novelty and modulation of some efficacious biological function. A generic claim can be “an antibody X binding to a protein Y….” Often,

64 G. Raghunathan et al. it is difficult to obtain such broad claims and enforce them in US courts. The claim is strengthened if other properties such as improvements in binding or alteration of function are established. In some instances, the complete antibody and/or DNA sequence is provided along with its effect (activity) on cells. In the absence of complete sequences, specific CDR sequences are provided with a clause such as “other antibodies that have a defined percentage similarity/identity” (such as 70–80%) to the claimed antibody CDR sequence. The patentability is significantly increased if one can establish structure-function relationships with the antigen protein interaction(s). If that is the case, the claim can be “An antibody X binds to protein Y with a binding constant B, binding to the epitope region E of the protein resulting in function F.” Sometimes a negative clause such as “binding to epitope E1 and not binding to epitope E2” can provide more exclusivity. Unique and novel epitope-based claims help to clearly get around existing patents from a structure-function perspective. When epitope information from techniques such as crystallography, NMR, SPR, or site- directed mutagenesis is available, it is always an advantage to have that information in a patent application for building intellectual property protection. Some high- profile cases for claims on sequences, structures, and epitopes are described below: (a) Rituximab is a chimeric anti-CD20 antibody developed by Biogen (Biogen- Idec) and co-marketed with Genentech. Biogen’s original patent in 1994 (Patent No. 7,682,612) covered anti-CD20 antibodies that bind to B cells. GSK developed later another anti-CD20 antibody that binds to a different epitope comprising a smaller loop and with better affinity than rituximab. Biogen sued GSK, but the patent court ruled in favor of GSK since it was of the view that the original Biogen patent was limited in scope and covered only anti-CD20 antibodies with similar affinity and specificity as rituximab. (b) PCSK9 is a liver enzyme that binds to low-density lipoprotein (LDLR). Two companies, Amgen and Sanofi/Regeneron, have developed antibodies against PCSK9. Amgen’s antibody Repatha® was approved by the FDA on August 27, 2015. Sanofi and Regeneron collaborated and developed Praluent®, and it was approved on July 27, 2015. Even though Amgen’s approval was a month later, their patents predate those of Sanofi and Regeneron. Both antibodies have the potential to be blockbusters for several years. The Amgen antibody binds to an epitope that blocks receptor signaling. X-ray crystallography was used for demonstrating the epitope for the antibody. Amgen also performed epitope binning experiments on several antibodies that compete for binding to Repatha®. The Sanofi legal team argued that Amgen did not provide enough structural evidence for the epitopes explicitly. On March 2016, a jury decided in favor of Amgen. Sanofi and Regeneron asked a judge to overturn the jury’s decision. In January 2017, the judge granted Amgen’s request for an injunction blocking Praluent® from the market starting on Feb 21, 2017. Subsequently, Sanofi and Regeneron pursued the legal battle with a Federal Court of Appeals and on February 8, 2017, obtained a stay order to suspend the execution of the previous judgment that would have blocked selling Praluent®. This case represents strong

4 Epitope Characterization and Isotype Selection 65 evidence for legal enforce ability of a patent covering structural details of the epitopes, data on competing antibodies, and carefully crafting claims based on experiments. (c) IL-12 and IL-23 are involved in adaptive immune response. The P40 subunit is present in both IL-12 and IL-23. In US patents 6,914,128 and 7,504,45, AbbVie claimed antibody JOE9 that binds and neutralizes the activity of IL-12. They claim nearly 300 antibodies obtained using phage display, initially identifying an antibody JOE9 which had low affinity to IL-12. Other antibodies had mutations in the CDR. AbbVie obtained another antibody Y61 with an improved affinity. Subsequently, they used site-directed mutagenesis to obtain several other antibodies. Since all these antibodies were derived from JOE9, all of them had the same human germline gene for the heavy chain (VH5) and a lambda light chain. All other sequences had very high sequence identity to JOE9 as expected, with more than 200 variants differing only in one position. The claims were based only on function and no structure-function relationships were described. After Centocor obtained patent 6,914,128 for Stelara® (an IL-12 blocking antibody), Abbott filed a lawsuit against the claims made by Centocor/ Janssen. Stelara® was derived from a transgenic mouse. It had a human germline for the heavy chain (VH3) and a kappa light chain. In addition, the sequence of Stelara® was more than 50% different from JOE9, the lengths of CDRs were very different, and they recognized an epitope that was different from that claimed by AbbVie. A Federal Circuit Court agreed with the decision of the district court that AbbVie patents did not adequately cover the structural diversity of antibodies covered in the Centocor patent. Conclusions In this review, we have discussed several methods for protein structure determination leading to epitope mapping. The methods discussed include X-ray crystallography, NMR, HD exchange mass spectroscopy, computational methods, and site-directed mutagenesis. Several examples are provided wherein antibodies recognize different epitopes on the same target protein, highlighting how recognition of the biologically relevant epitope is important for function and also for securing intellectual property. We have also discussed studies wherein the changes in the Fc region of an antibody have an effect on antigen binding. References Abzalimov RR, Kaplan DA, Easterling ML, Kaltashov IA. Chapter 5: Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics Andrew W. Drake, Yasmina N. Abdiche, and

5.1 Introduction Development of therapeutic monoclonal antibodies (mAbs) requires rigorous measurements of the kinetic and thermodynamic binding properties of antibody–antigen complexes for antibody drug candidate efficacy optimization and the design of clinical dosing strategies via pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic modeling. Quantitatively describing the binding characteristics of antibody–antigen complexes is possible by considering the interaction to be a simple bimolecular reversible interaction. Subsequently, chemical binding principles can be applied to describe the association rate constant (k), the dissociation rate constant (k), and the a d equilibrium dissociation constant (K ), also known as the “affinity” of the interac- D tion. The association rate constant is a measure of how fast two reactants A and B form AB complex: ka AB A+B® (5.1) where k, often referred to as the “on-rate,” is defined as the number of AB coma plexes formed per second/unit volume in a 1 molar solution of A and B and has units of inverse molarity-seconds (M−1 s−1). The forward reaction rate (r) is directly proa portional to k and to the concentrations of A and B ([A] and [B], respectively) and a is described by the differential rate law: | | r =k | [A][B] | (5.2) | | — | —- | —— | —– | | | a a | | | The dissociation rate constant describes the dissociation of AB formed in Eq. 5.1 above: kd A+B AB® (5.3) where k is defined as the fraction of complexes that decays per second and has units d of inverse seconds (s−1). The dissociation rate constant is a measure of the inertness of the AB complex, and is often referred to as the “off-rate.” The rate of the dissociation of AB (r) is directly proportional to k and to the concentration of formed AB d d ([AB]) and is described by the differential rate law: | | r =k | [AB] | (5.4) | | — | —- | —- | —– | | | d d | | | Note the contrast between the reaction rates (r, r) and the rate constants (k, k): | | | a d | a d | | — | — | — | — | reaction rates change as [A], [B], and [AB] change the course of a bimolecular interaction while k and k are characteristic constants which quantitatively describe a d the more important and useful intrinsic binding properties of, in this example, the interaction between binding partners A and B. At equilibrium, the rate of complex formation (r) in Eq. 5.2 equals the rate of complex dissociation (r) in Eq. 5.4. a d Hence at equilibrium: k [A][B]=k [AB] (5.5) a d

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 73 Rearranging Eq. 5.5 derives an expression for the equilibrium dissociation constant: [A][B] k = d =K (5.6) [AB] k D a where K is expressed in units of molarity from the ratio k/k and describes the D d a overall strength of the reversible association between A and B. The lower the magnitude of K, driven by either a high k, indicating a fast formation of AB, or a low D a k, indicating a slow dissociation of AB, or driven by a combination of both a high d k and a low k, the more stable or “tighter” the interaction between A and B, referred a d to as a “high affinity” interaction. Several complex factors can influence the ideal K for a therapeutic mAb, namely D the nature and prevalence of the therapeutic target in the diseased tissue, as well as the desired functionality of the mAb. Hence, predicting the optimal efficacious affinity for a therapeutic mAb to its target antigen is difficult. In general, however, affinities ranging from 1 nM to less than 10 pM should not be unreasonable design goals. Measuring K, k, and k for such high affinity mAbs is challenging to the D a d biophysical chemist for three reasons: (1) the time to reach equilibrium for the mAb–antigen complex can be on the order of days, (2) the k can be so low that long d periods of data acquisition may be needed for an accurate off-rate measurement, and (3) whenever the k isn’t unusually slow (>5 × 10−4 s−1), the k can be extremely d a fast (>1 × 107 M−1 s−1). Fast k can be challenging to measure by Biacore due to a mass transport limitations (vide infra). However, these kinetic rate constants and affinities can be reliably estimated when the correct instrumentation is utilized properly, experiments are designed correctly, and data is processed optimally. Fluorescence polarization anisotropy can be used to measure the binding affinity between two molecules by monitoring the increase of polarized fluorescence emission as polarized light excites a solution sample containing a fluorescently tagged molecule binding to a larger molecule. The larger complex formed “tumbles” in solution more slowly, thus emitting more polarized light as compared to the smaller uncomplexed fluorescently tagged partner which “tumbles” in solution more rapidly (Nasir and Jolley 1999; Jameson and Mocz 2005). However, with larger antigens (>60 kDa) there often isn’t enough of a molecular size discrepancy between unbound antigen and the antibody–antigen complex to affect a detectible difference in rotational diffusion (Nasir and Jolley 1999). Fluorescence polarization anisotropy also requires that one of the binding partners be modified with a fluorophore. Another common label-free method used to measure the binding affinity between macro molecules is Isothermal Titration Calorimetry, ITC (Doyle 1997). In ITC, the amount of heat absorbed or released (depending on whether the association reaction is endothermic or exothermic) is monitored as a sample of a one binding partner (usually a small molecule or protein) associates with a macromolecule in an adiabatic chamber (Pierce et al. 1999). Measuring the magnitude of heat change as a function of the sample concentration injected into the adiabatic chamber allows for a direct calculation of the equilibrium dissociation constant. ITC is limited,

74 A. W. Drake et al. however, in that relatively high (micromolar) protein concentrations are needed to detect the heat of binding within the sensitivity range of the calorimeter. The sensitivity of ITC heat detection is often limited when lower protein concentrations are required for measuring sub-nanomolar protein–protein affinities (Doyle 1997), and a competition experiment is then needed to determine the K of sub-nanomolar D interactions. As with the previous version of this book (Tabrizi et al. 2012), this chapter on biophysical considerations for the development of antibody therapeutics describes in detail surface-based, solution-based, and cell-based biophysical techniques ideally suited for measuring the binding constants of therapeutic monoclonal antibody–antigen complexes. Each of the three experimental formats is critically evaluated and comparisons between the technologies are supported with examples of previous studies from the literature. Theoretical binding concepts that must be considered in properly designing an experiment and evaluating data using these biophysical methodologies are also discussed. This updated chapter provides greater detail on epitope binning methods while also discussing biophysical characterization of bispecific antibodies and additional approaches for acquiring surface-based, solution-based, and cell-based binding constants. Often these approaches are presented in the context of the capabilities of the very latest in biosensor instrumentation. 5.2 Biacore Technology The premier technology for measuring the binding affinities of antibody–antigen complexes is surface plasmon resonance (SPR)-based Biacore instrumentation (Karlsson and Fält 1997). With Biacore, one of the binding partners is captured or covalently immobilized (the “ligand”) to a modified gold surface while the other reactant (the “analyte”) is flowed across the surface. The most common commercially available Biacore gold surface modification is a dextran matrix containing carboxyl groups for covalent protein coupling. As the flowed reactant binds to the surface, plasmons, or quantum mechanical electron density waves on the gold surface generated by an evanescent wave of multi-angled totally internally reflected light, are influenced by the bound protein mass. A resonance effect between surface plasmons and the evanescent wave shadows the intensity of the totally internally reflected light at an angle dependant on the amount of protein mass bound to the surface. As protein mass binds to or dissociates from the surface over time, the shadowed reflected angle shifts in proportion to the mass change at the surface. The magnitudes of these detected angle shifts are then converted into arbitrary resonance units (RU). In simpler terms, as the flowed protein binds to the biosensor surface, the detected RU signal increases; as the protein dissociates from the surface, the detected RU signal decreases, thus providing real-time binding of the reactants. The signal trace as a function of time recorded by the Biacore instrument, called a “sensorgram,” contains binding association and dissociation phase information, allowing for a direct measurement of k and k for biomolecular interactions. a d

75 Equilibrium Association Dissociation (k ) (k ) a d antigen buffer antigen only Regeneration High pH? Low pH? buffer only buffer only 0 The K can then be calculated from the quotient k/k (Eq. 5.6). Figure 5.1 illustrates D d a

the kinetic binding information contained in a schematic of a sensorgram. Standard Biacore instruments feature the ability to inject analyte with a single automated injection syringe over four independent flow cells individually or simultaneously with available instruments varying in sensitivity levels. Higher throughput Biacore instruments like the Biacore 4000 (GE Healthcare) allow the user to inject analyte with four individual syringes with each syringe flowing over one, two, or three spots per injection out of a total of five spots available per flow cell. The Biacore 8K (GE Healthcare) has eight individual injection syringes flowing over one or two spots each. Bio-Rad’s (Hercules, CA) ProteOn XPR 36 has a criss-crossing six-channel flow cell that allows for the use of a 36-spot array. The MASS-1 and MASS-2 instruments from Sierra Biosensors (Greenville, RI) each have eight flow channels that can each flow over as many as two or four spots, respectively. The IBIS MX96 SPRi from Wasatch Microfluidics (now Carterra Inc., Salt Lake City, UT) can generate 96 sensorgrams in one injection using a single flow channel over a 96-spot array. In 2018 Carterra Inc. will release the LSA Platform which will allow for as many as 384 simultaneous label-free interactions with a single injection of analyte.
