(Tabrizi et al. (2009) Drug Discovery Today 14:298–305. Reproduced with permission)

76 A. W. Drake et al. later in this chapter. Even though there are several choices for SPR instrumentation with varying throughput and sensitivities, the experimental design approach for measuring antibody–antigen interactions is essentially the same for all platforms, and Biacore instruments are the most widely used. Because one binding partner is immobilized to a biosensor surface with Biacore, the technology is often criticized for having an experimental geometry which makes it prone to generating artifactual data. The inception of the surface dextran was to provide a solution-like environment where immobilized protein can maintain some degree of rotational freedom (Karlsson et al. 1994), but there is concern in the literature that affinities measured using Biacore biosensors will differ from pure “solutionphase” affinity measurements where neither of the binding partners is tethered to a surface. These concerns might have some merit if not for the fact that most published Biacore data is the result of incorrect experimental design and less than optimal data processing techniques. In addition, a majority of published articles that contain Biacore data provide insufficient information with regard to their Biacore experimental protocol and often forego displaying sensorgram data, instead providing only a table of numerical results. The reader is then prevented from reproducing the experiment and is unable to fully critique whether the Biacore data was either generated correctly or was of sufficient quality to provide reliable results (Rich and Myszka 2010). Myszka (1999b) was the first to provide an extensive annual review of the biosensor literature reporting the use of Biacore and other biosensor technologies. Rich and Myszka (2000, 2001, 2002, 2003, 2004, 2005, 2006, 2007, 2008, 2010) then continued the arduous task of annually reviewing and critiquing the biosensor literature for over 10 years. In their last review, Rich and Myszka (2010) noted that out of over 1400 papers that published biosensor data, only 5% appeared to have shown a proper use of the technology and a proper communication of their use of the technology. Again, the technology itself is often blamed for providing erroneous kinetic data when the real culprit is often from the incorrect use of the technology by the scientist or from the condition of the reagent samples being studied (Rich and Myszka 2010). Most potential sources for generating artifactual data in a Biacore biosensor instrument can be avoided with the proper experimental design and processing of the data while using the highest quality protein reagents. 5.2.1 Biacore Experimental Design When measuring the binding kinetics of an antibody–antigen interaction using a Biacore instrument, the bivalency of the antibody dictates that it should be immobilized to the surface while the antigen should be injected over the biosensor surface. This orientation (Fig. 5.2a) not only ensures that the more characteristic site- binding equilibrium dissociation constant is being measured, but also provides an experimental design which would generate kinetic data that could most likely be described by a simple 1:1 kinetic binding model wherein k and k can be reliably estimated. a d The incorrect reverse orientation shown in Fig. 5.2b where antibody flows over immobilized antigen promotes cross-linking of the bivalent antibody to the

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 77 a Correct Orientation Ag Flow Site Binding Constant Immobilized mAp b Avidity Effects mAb Flow Complex Kinetics Probable Immobilized Ag c Improbable Solution mAb Flow Low Biacore Response Immobilized Ag Fig. 5.2 Biacore experimental designs, both correct and incorrect, for measuring antibody–antigen binding interactions. (a) Antibody is correctly immobilized to the biosensor while antigen flows over the surface. This ensures the intrinsic site-binding K is being measured, especially for D monovalent antigen. (b) Flowing antibody over immobilized antigen risks avidity effects from surface cross-linking which increases the probability of generating complex kinetic data and/or rate constants that may change as a function of antigen immobilization density. (c) Immobilizing a low antigen surface density more than likely does not obviate artifacts associated with flowing bivalent antibody over antigen. (Roskos et al. (2007) in: S Dübel (ed.): Handbook of Therapeutic Antibodies: Molecular Engineering II: Antibody Affinity. Figure 7.6, p. 160. 2007. Copyright Wiley-VCH Verlag GmbH & Co. KGaA. Reproduced with permission) immobilized antigen (avidity). This is problematic for two reasons: (1) avidly binding mAb can yield artificially low measurements for k leading to an artificially tight d K and (2) the risk of generating complex kinetic sensorgram data is greatly D increased. The latter point is especially crucial because choosing the correct complex binding model can take an inordinate and painstaking amount of effort. In fact, selection of the correct complex kinetic model usually involves collecting extrabiosensor data to affirm the complex model chosen. In practice, several different complex models could successfully fit the same complex Biacore data set while

78 A. W. Drake et al. estimating different rate constants. Theoretically, this complexity could be avoided at low antigen surface densities as shown in Fig. 5.2c. Experience has shown, however, that even when antigen is immobilized at a low surface capacity it is still difficult to rid sensorgrams of kinetic complexity when mAb is flowed across a surface with immobilized antigen. The experimental design when using Biacore to measure antibody–antigen binding kinetics becomes more challenging when the antigen is itself multivalent or if a monovalent antigen tends to form soluble multimers in solution. Here a higher probability of multiple antigen cross-linking exists, thus increasing the probability of observing complex kinetic sensorgrams. Figure 5.2c depicts the most reasonable experimental approach when both reactants are multivalent: immobilize the minimal amount of either multivalent binding partner that appears to maintain a detectible and usable RU response when the other multivalent protein is injected over the surface. This design may decrease the occurrence of multistep binding at the surface but it does not necessarily guarantee that the resulting binding sensorgrams can be described by a simple 1:1 binding model. Even in cases where, for example, kinetic sensorgrams of multivalent antigen binding to immobilized antibody do fit well to a simple 1:1 interaction model, it is still ambiguous whether the K measured repre- D sents the intrinsic site (microscopic) binding constant or a stoichiometric (macroscopic) binding constant representing two sites from the multivalent antigen interacting with the two mAb binding sites. In other words, it is possible, although unlikely, that the interaction between multiple binding sites of a multimeric antigen and the two binding sites of an immobilized bivalent antibody can fit a 1:1 interaction model as well as a monovalent antigen binding to a single binding site of an immobilized antibody. The drawback to the ambiguity of the nature of the Biacore affinity measurement of multivalent antigen binding to bivalent antibody is that it is always preferable, from a biophysical perspective, to measure the single site-binding equilibrium dissociation constant because it is uniquely characteristic of the bound complex. A macroscopic or stoichiometric affinity constant from a multimeric bimolecular interaction only provides information from a molecular thermodynamic perspective with no information regarding the individual binding sites that intrinsically drive the stoichiometric binding. Moreover, if a bivalent binding partner flowing over the Biacore surface does indeed cross-link two different immobilized multivalent ligands (Fig. 5.2b), then the macroscopic affinity constant should more correctly be considered an avidity constant. The obvious dilemma here is that the avidity binding constant can change as the density of immobilized ligand changes. In fact, an avidity binding “constant” that can change as a function of surface ligand density might arguably be more correctly referred to as an equilibrium dissociation “inconstant.” Some scientists might claim that a Biacore-generated avidity equilibrium “constant” measured by flowing antibody over immobilized antigen is more mechanistically representative of in vivo functionality, but this claim does not hold true upon further consideration. For instance, it is impossible to know if the immobilized antigen density on the biosensor surface corresponds exactly to the receptor density found on the target cells in vivo. Again, almost any antibody–antigen avidity that

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 79 was desired could be measured depending on the antigen surface capacity at which the experiment was performed. Of course, if both binding sites of the flowing antibody can bind to only one immobilized multivalent antigen and no cross-linking of two antigens takes place, then the avidity constant would not change as a function of the immobilized antigen density and would be more representative of the true inherent biophysical binding constant from a macroscopic perspective, but still not from a site-binding perspective. In summary, interpretation of Biacore data can be ambiguous when both binding partners are multivalent and the data can be described by a simple 1:1 interaction model. Does the measured binding affinity represent the intrinsic site-binding affinity or the stoichiometric affinity (avidity)? If the latter case, does the protein immobilization level dictate the measured affinity because of potential cross-linking of the flowing analyte to multiple immobilized binding partners, in which case a binding “inconstant” is being measured? Additionally, when the Biacore sensorgram data show complex kinetics, it may be advisable to instead perform equilibrium-based measurements using a different biophysical method rather than attempting to use a complex binding model without additional evidence to support the mechanism described by the complex model. Finally, no matter whether the K measured from D data that is described well by a simple 1:1 kinetic binding model is a site or avidity K, researchers still consider the measurement useful during the selection stages of D finding therapeutic antibody candidates possessing the optimal affinities required to move towards further drug development stages. 5.2.2 Biacore Experimental Methods In addition to the importance of deciding which binding partner in a Biacore experiment should be immobilized and which should be flowed to measure the most reliable and meaningful k and k of an antibody–antigen complex, it is equally a d important that the experimental design also includes techniques that have been shown to ensure the highest quality results. Myszka (1999a) has written one of the foremost primers on optimal Biacore experimental methods and biosensor processing protocols that provide the highest quality kinetic results. Highlights of the Biacore protocol suggestions from this publication are briefly summarized and discussed in more detail below. Myszka’s suggestions (1999a) are especially applicable for measuring antibody–antigen kinetics assuming antigen is correctly flowed over antibody immobilized to a Biacore surface (Fig. 5.2a). It should be noted that before an experiment is even initiated, it is of utmost importance that regular cleaning and maintenance of a Biacore instrument are meticulously followed so that the microfluidics in the instrument always perform optimally. 5.2.2.1 Avoiding Mass Transport One specific criticism of solid-phase biosensors in the measurement of kinetics is the technology’s apparent susceptibility to collecting kinetic data under mass transport conditions. Briefly, mass transport limitations occur when the k at the Biacore a

80 A. W. Drake et al. surface is so fast (>105–107 M−1-s−1), the ability of Biacore to accurately measure k a is limited to the rate at which the flowed antigen can be delivered to the biosensor surface through bulk solution, while the ability to measure k is compromised by d antigen rebinding effects. Rigorous theoretical considerations of mass transport in Biacore biosensors have shown when a mass transport term (k ) describing a prom tein diffusion rate through bulk solution is included as a fitting parameter along with a standard 1:1 interaction model, k can greatly improve the theoretical fit of a m kinetic Biacore data set containing some mass transport influence (Myszka 1997; Myszka et al. 1997, 1998a; Karlsson 1999). In cases where the k is very fast a (>107 M−1-s−1), mass transport artifacts may be unavoidable and an accurate measurement of k and k at the biosensor surface may not be possible. Karlsson (1999), a d however, has shown that because mass transport affects both the on-rate and the off-rate equally, one can still determine K from the acquired kinetic data. Briefly, D Karlsson’s theoretical derivation (Eq. 5.7) shows how the mass transport term (k ) m mathematically cancels, leaving the ratio k/k to calculate K, but not the individual d a D values for k and k (Karlsson 1999). a d k k k K = d m = d (5.7) D k k k a m a Myszka (1999a) recommends two methods that can help obviate mass transport effects: (1) the amount of antibody immobilized should provide the lowest possible maximum antigen binding response level (R ) and (2) the antigen injection flow max rates should be high, preferably 100 μL/min. The optimum antibody immobilization level, of course, will theoretically depend on the molecular weights of both the antibody and the flowed antigen. An unnecessarily high antibody surface density may provide such a vast “sink” of antibody to which antigen can bind that the association reaction becomes almost entirely diffusion controlled when the k is extremely fast, a and the dissociation reaction appears artifactually slow owing to the increased probability of antigen rebinding. A fast flow rate ensures the antigen sample is delivered to the biosensor surface as rapidly as possible, and additionally it assists in delivering a more consistent antigen plug across the surface. In addition, it’s important to note that many of the recently available SPR instruments are sensitive enough to allow the user to obtain reliable kinetics at R levels below 5 RU (Kamat and max Rafique 2017a, b) which means surface capacities today can be prepared even lower than in the past to help minimize mass transport. 5.2.2.2 Antigen Concentration Myszka (1999a) also recommends that antigen samples be injected ideally at several concentrations ranging from at least 10- to 100-fold above and below the K. In D cases where the binding affinity is relatively low, antigen concentrations 10- to 100-fold above the K may exceed high triple-digit nanomolar or single-digit micromo- D lar levels, which often exhibit significant nonspecific binding to the ligand immobilized on the biosensor surface. Conversely, when the binding affinity is very tight, antigen concentrations 10- to 100-fold below picomolar K ’s may not display D enough of a detectible signal to provide any significant kinetic sensorgram

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 81 information. Still, an antigen concentration range as wide as reasonably possible around the K value should always be used. In addition, the higher antigen concen- D trations should show enough curvature in the association phase of their sensorgrams to allow the fitting model to reliably estimate k, and show enough signal decay in a the dissociation phase to reliably estimate k. In extreme cases where the on-rate is d unusually slow, the necessity to inject low analyte concentrations to be near the picomolar K of a complex might require sample injections on the order of hours D instead of the conventional 1–2 min for most antibody–antigen interactions (Navratilova et al. 2005); the long injection time requirements is beyond the capability of currently available biosensors without the use of extremely slow flow rates that should be avoided for reasons already discussed. There have been instances, however, where researchers have prepared a large volume of a low analyte concentration and used the running buffer inlet to inject this analyte over the SPR chip surface at a high flow rate for multiple minutes or even multiple hours (Myszka et al. 1998b). It is also important to remember that because association rate constants are concentration dependent, the most rigorous methods should be used to measure the antigen concentration, most notably those methods published by Pace et al. (1995) and Grimsley and Pace (2003) which describe the proper calculation of a protein extinction coefficient based on amino acid sequence and pH (if an accurate extinction coefficient is not already available), and the correct light scattering correction when measuring protein absorbance. In fact, a Biacore instrument can determine the total active protein concentration when experimental conditions are designed to favor data generated under mass transport-limited conditions. Consider when the mass transport of antigen from bulk solution is much faster than the k, the concentration of antigen will be the same a at the biosensor surface as it is in the flowing bulk solution, and any measured binding will exclusively represent the true antibody–antigen binding kinetics. In the contrasting case where the mass transport of antigen through the bulk solution is rate limiting, any measured binding signal is directly proportional to the active antigen concentration and independent of the true binding kinetics (Christensen 1997; Van Regenmortel et al. 1998). Experimental conditions and theoretical treatment of mass transport-limited data for biosensor concentration determination methods are well documented by Karlsson et al. (1993), Christensen (1997), and Van Regenmortel et al. (1998), among other works. One of the more significant recent applications developed by GE Healthcare specifically for Biacore instruments is Calibration-Free Concentration Analysis (CFCA). Here the active concentration of an analyte can be measured without the need for a calibration standard. The CFCA method measures the response rates of analyte flowing over ligand at multiple flow rates under mass transport conditions. Then by taking advantage of the known fluid dynamics of the Biacore’s flow cell as well as the molecular weight and diffusion coefficient of the analyte, the active analyte concentration can be calculated (GE Healthcare 2008; Pol et al. 2016). CFCA is a proprietary protocol that may be used only with applicable Biacore instruments.

82 A. W. Drake et al. 5.2.2.3 Biacore Surface Regeneration Regeneration of the biosensor binding surface between antigen injection cycles is necessary because it is normally impractical to wait for the dissociation phase of a sensorgram to decay back down to baseline, especially when studying highly stable antigen–antibody complexes with antibody covalently coupled to the biosensor surface. Randomly injecting antigen concentrations in duplicate or triplicate provides assurance that a sensorgram generated early in the experiment is reproduced several cycles later with the identical antigen concentration. This is only possible when the optimal surface regeneration conditions have been determined and the surface is stable for the duration of a full kinetic experiment. For capture experiments where antigen is injected over antibody captured to a high-density covalently immobilized anti-species polyclonal antibody surface, one to two short 15 s pulses of ~146 mM phosphoric acid or glycine-HCl, pH 1.7 usually works well to remove all captured antibody bound and unbound with antigen while maintaining a reproducible capture surface. There is certainly no “magic” regeneration formula that can be applied to all captured or covalently immobilized antibody surfaces, but regeneration reagents of either low pH (i.e., phosphoric acid, glycine-HCl) or high pH (i.e., NaOH) should be injected at relatively short pulses of ~10–30 s each. Often multiple pulses of a regeneration reagent may be needed. Researchers often make the mistake of attempting to match the baseline RU before an antigen injection cycle with the postregeneration baseline RU to assess regeneration success (Andersson et al. 1999; van der Merwe 2001; Murphy et al. 2006). However, the fluid-like dextran matrix on the biosensor surface apparently can shift the baseline RU before and after an injection, which usually makes this practice futile. A successful regeneration scheme is achieved when multiple, properly referenced sensorgrams of identical antigen concentrations are reproducible. Drake and Klakamp (2011) detailed the Drake- Klakamp Method as a systematic, seven-step experimental approach to more efficiently determine the optimal regeneration conditions for Biacore surfaces with covalently coupled proteins. Even more helpful are SPR instruments that feature multiple injection paths (see introduction in Sect. 5.2) which allow users to assess multiple regeneration conditions in a single experiment. Finally, an injection of sample buffer should always be flowed over the Biacore flow cells at the start of an antigen injection cycle and immediately after the regeneration injections to more completely wash out the microfluidics system. There are SPR protocols that can generate reliable kinetic data without the need for surface regeneration. There is always the rare possibility that all attempts at regenerating the biosensor surface are unsuccessful. There is also the possibility that reagent availability is too limited to allow for performing regeneration scouting experiments. SPR instruments having multiple injection ports can simultaneously flow multiple concentrations of analyte in a single injection over the identical surface-bound ligand (Abdiche et al. 2016; Yang et al. 2016; Kamat and Rafique 2017a). A full kinetic profile of a single set of multiple analyte concentrations is thus generated where a regeneration protocol is unnecessary. These instruments are discussed in more detail in Sect. 5.2.3.2. Another method to acquire kinetic data for a range of analyte concentrations without the need for surface regeneration is single-cycle

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 83 kinetics or kinetic titration analysis (Karlsson et al. 2006). This technique involves sequentially injecting multiple concentrations of analyte without any regeneration steps between each injection. Typically, the sequence of analyte is injected from the lowest to highest concentration, resulting in a single continuous stepwise sensorgram concatenated from all analyte injections. The single-cycle kinetic model can float the k, k, and surface capacity (R ) to globally fit all analyte injections in a kinetic titraa d max tion series to estimate k and k (Karlsson et al. 2006). Globally fitting sensorgram a d data will be discussed further in Sect. 5.2.2.5. Single-c ycle kinetic experiments are especially useful for single channel, array-based SPR biosensors where it would be impossible to determine an optimal regeneration protocol for dozens of different antibodies covalently “spotted” to the array (Yang et al. 2016). With these protocols in mind, it is certainly fair to ask if it is even necessary to perform SPR experiments having multi-injection cycles that require surface regeneration. While the time needed to optimize regeneration is certainly avoided with single-cycle methods, what they do lack are the benefits from acquiring multiple replicate sensorgrams of each analyte concentration. First, multiple replicates provide greater statistical power to the estimated rate constants during global fitting. This is especially beneficial when estimating binding constants with “high-r esolution” experiments (Sect. 5.2.3.3) where precision and accuracy are crucial for characterizing purified, lead therapeutic antibody candidates. Secondly, instrument error can occasionally require the removal of erroneous sensorgrams from a data set. Having multiple replicates for each antigen concentration has a greater chance of preserving binding data that represent the complete profile of antigen concentrations, thus again maintaining a greater statistical rigor for estimating the binding constants. 5.2.2.4 Double-Referencing Data Biacore experiments should also be designed to allow for double-referencing of the sensorgram data. Double-referencing data helps correct for artifacts such as bulk refractive index changes, nonspecific binding, systematic instrument noise, and baseline drift, all of which are common in every biosensor experiment (Morton and Myszka 1998). At least one flow cell of a Biacore chip should be used as a reference surface. The reference surface should either be exposed to the same chemical conditions used to covalently couple antibody on the active surfaces, or contain approximately the same amount of antibody capturing reagent as all other non-reference flow cells for a capture experiment (Morton and Myszka 1998). Also, several buffer “blank” injection cycles should be interspersed with the antigen injection cycles during the experiment. Each antigen and blank sample should be injected over the antibody surfaces and the reference surface simultaneously. To double-reference sensorgram data, both the reference surface responses and the blank responses are subtracted from the analyte sensorgrams during sensorgram processing. The former corrects for refractive index shifts and nonspecific binding while the latter corrects for systematic instrument noise and baseline drift. Often the quality of sensorgram data sets cannot be assessed without double-referencing, and normally data cannot be reliably fit for kinetic rate constants without double-referencing. The processing power of double-referencing is most effective (and impressive) when low surface

84 A. W. Drake et al. capacities are used, often salvaging noisy-looking, low signal raw data sets of seemingly questionable quality into surprisingly useful and reliable kinetic data. 5.2.2.5 Global Fitting All processed sensorgram data should be simultaneously (“globally”) fit to a simple 1:1 nonlinear kinetic interaction model. Global fitting yields the most rigorous and accurate values for k and k from the entire data set, including and where appropria d ate, data sets collected from multiple independent flow cells in a single experiment. All sensorgrams, of course, should possess the same association and dissociation rate constants for a given interaction on multiple flow cells. However, the amount of information about each parameter will vary (Morton and Myszka 1998) for sensorgrams collected at different antigen concentrations on a particular surface for a given surface capacity of mAb (the density of mAb immobilized). For example, a high antigen concentration might saturate the surface quickly during the association phase, thus providing minimal on-rate information but providing ample surface capacity (R ) information, while lower antigen concentrations which show a max slower rate of curvature in the association phase can provide a better estimate for k a but a less than optimal estimate for the surface capacity. Globally fitting the entire data set combines all information contained in each binding response curve, thus improving the statistical power of the estimated rate constants (Morton and Myszka 1998). In addition to double-referencing Biacore data, Scrubber software (BioLogic Software, Campbell, Australia) also contains global fitting functionality. Scrubber is preferred for data processing and fitting by many experts in the field. 5.2.2.6 Complex Data Complexity in sensorgram data is normally most apparent when the dissociation phase appears to be biphasic with relatively rapid signal decay after the end of the injection over a relatively short time span immediately followed by much slower signal decay. Complex association sensorgram data also appears biphasic. Of course, it is inadvisable to attempt to globally fit complex data with a simple bimolecular interaction model and assume the resulting kinetic rate constants are acceptable estimates. As discussed previously, when complex sensorgrams are observed it is also inadvisable to “surf” for a binding model that can describe the shape of the sensorgrams without any further rigorous scientific evidence to support the use of the complex model. There are several other potential artifacts besides the multivalent cross-linking issues described in Fig. 5.2b, c which can result in complex sensorgram data. These include impure antibody immobilized to the biosensor surface, impure antigen, heterogeneity of antibody binding epitopes introduced by the covalent immobilization chemistry, poor sensorgram processing, improperly referenced and subtracted nonspecific binding of the antigen, antigen forming multimers in solution, a general improper use of the Biacore instrument, or perhaps from poorly performing instrumentation due to improper cleaning and maintenance. If complex data appears to be unavoidable even with proper Biacore experimental design and data processing, solution-based equilibrium methodologies independent of kinetic measurements may have to be used for a more reliable affinity measurement.

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 85 5.2.2.7 SPR and Bispecific Antibodies With over 30 currently in clinical development, bispecific antibodies have recently been established as important next generation antibody therapeutics. In general, bispecific antibodies are engineered so that each variable region binds to a different antigen as opposed to monoclonal antibodies which are target monospecific. One of the main advantages of bispecific antibodies is they replace combination monoclonal antibody therapies with a single antibody construct, thus simplifying development and potentially reducing toxicity (Spiess et al. 2015). The binding properties of bispecifics to their target antigens of course remain important parameters for their development into viable therapeutics. Using SPR to measure the individual affinities and kinetics of each antigen to their respective binding site on the bispecific antibody is straightforward; each antigen is injected separately at a series of concentrations over the immobilized or captured bispecific antibody over two separate experiments (Schaefer et al. 2010; Kitazawa et al. 2012) or the bispecific is injected at a concentration series simultaneously over both targets immobilized on two independent flow cells (Moore et al. 2011). Since the mechanism of action for bispecifics relies on their simultaneous binding to two target antigens, it is more informative to qualitatively and quantitatively assess the binding of the bispecific antibody in the presence of both targets. Schaefer et al. (2010), for example, confirmed the ability of a bispecific antibody to simultaneously bind hAng-2 and hVEGF-A with an SPR “sandwich” assay. Here the response of the bispecific antibody binding to hVEGF-A immobilized to the biosensor chip surface was followed immediately by a response detected of hAng-2 injected over the bispecific antibody complexed to the surface-bound hVEGF- A. This type of qualitative “sandwich” method will be discussed further in the epitope binning section of this chapter. To measure the actual binding kinetics of each individual antigen to the bispecific antibody in the presence of the other antigen, Karlsson (2015) suggested injecting a concentration series of the bispecific antibody premixed with saturating amounts of antigen “A” over surface-bound antigen “B.” Dissociation of bispecific antibody from surface-immobilized “B” is followed in the presence of a high concentration of antigen “A” to maintain saturation of the bispecific antibody with antigen “A” during the dissociation phase. This method ensures the measured kinetics represent the interaction between the bispecific antibody and antigen “B” in the presence of constant (saturating) antigen “A.” The orientation of the antigens would be reversed to measure the kinetics of the bispecific–antigen “A” interaction in the presence of antigen “B” (Karlsson 2015). This technique however could be limited by the amount of time and volume the SPR instrument allows for a co-injected analyte. If the dissociation of the bispecific antibody from antigen “B” on the surface is slow, the subsequent injection time of antigen “A” may not be long enough to maintain saturation of the bispecific to allow for a detectable dissociation signal of the bispecific from antigen “B.” An alternative approach would instead involve capturing or immobilizing the bispecific antibody to the SPR chip and then performing the bispecific–antigen “B” kinetic binding experiment with the running buffer and sample preparation buffers containing a saturating concentration of antigen “A.” This maintains the presence of antigen “A”

86 A. W. Drake et al. during both the association and dissociation phases of the bispecific–antigen “B” interaction (Klakamp 2017). Either method can provide an indication as to how the presence of one antigen may or may not affect the binding of the other antigen when compared to binding experiments for each separate antigen. 5.2.3 Low-, Medium-, and High-Resolution Biacore Kinetic Experiments One of the many advantages of using Biacore instrumentation for the development of therapeutic antibodies is its throughput flexibility. Biacore techniques can be catered to the rigor of the binding information necessary at various screening stages. Kinetic and affinity information can be measured from 1 to 3 antibodies in a single experiment to several hundred antibodies in a single experiment. Often the progression for determining which antibody candidates have the tightest affinity to their purified antigen target begins with Biacore instrumentation performing as an invaluable high-throughput (relatively speaking for SPR experiments) screening tool. Here “low resolution” kinetic data can allow for rapid affinity ranking of dozens to hundreds of unpurified antibodies in supernatant (Canziani et al. 2004; Säfsten et al. 2006). Low resolution approaches can also yield qualitative epitope binning information on multiple antibodies. Following a low resolution experiment, a “medium resolution” approach may be used to evaluate more rigorous kinetic information from the 4 to 8 highest affinity antibody supernatants to determine the 1–3 lead candidates. These selected mAb candidates are then purified and subjected to the most rigorous “high-resolution” antibody–antigen kinetics measurements. Approaches to low-, medium-, and high-resolution Biacore experiments and the data contained therein are discussed in more detail in the sections below. 5.2.3.1 Low Resolution Screening A typical cycle in a low resolution antibody screen includes capturing a series of monoclonal antibodies and then injecting antigen at a single concentration. When antigen binding is complete, capture surfaces are regenerated so that the cycle can be repeated with a new series of antibodies, and so on. By injecting antigen at a single concentration only, the number of antibodies that can be screened is maximized. As long as the concentration of injected analyte is high enough to elicit curvature in the sensorgram, it is possible to yield reliable estimates for k and k a d from the injection of a single antigen concentration (Canziani et al. 2004) when enough curvature can be collected in the association and dissociation phases in a matter of minutes to estimate k and k. As discussed in Sect. 5.2.1, immobilizing a d antigen first and then injecting mAbs in supernatant is not recommended because of potential avidity effects yielding complex and/or erroneous data. This approach suffers additionally from the fact that concentrations of injected mAbs from supernatants or lysates are typically unknown, thus making it impossible to obtain a value for k. In some cases, the characteristics of an antigen (e.g., bivalency as in Fc-fusion a constructs) may make the standard low resolution kinetic screening of supernatants

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 87 approach less desirable. In the case of phage display antibody discovery efforts, Fab fragments are generated in periplasmic extracts (PPE) at early campaign stages; therefore, there is the flexibility to reverse the orientation of the typical antigen– antibody screen if the antigen is multivalent. Here the Fabs in PPE are injected as analytes over any potentially multivalent antigen captured to the biosensor chip (Steukers et al. 2006). For quantitating the unknown concentrations of Fab in the PPEs prior to this reversed kinetic screen, both Steukers et al. (2006) and Myszka (2007) have suggested using SPR to calibrate the initial binding rates of the unknown Fabs to known Fab standards under mass transport-limited conditions (Karlsson et al. 1993). Optimally any PPEs to be used as analytes should be subjected to a buffer exchange to match as closely as possible the running buffer used in the SPR experiment so that the large refractive index changes inherent in flowing supernatants as analytes is minimized. If the antigen is a bivalent Fc-fusion protein, researchers should weigh time constraints of either generating a monomeric version of the antigen from the fusion protein or performing the additional step of quantitating Fab in PPE so that the PPEs can be used as analytes injected over the fusion antigen. Of course, it isn’t always possible to successfully “clip” fusion proteins, in which case the latter choice may be the only option for a low resolution SPR kinetic screen of phage-derived Fabs in PPEs with multivalent antigens (Leonard et al. 2007). Commonly, screens found in the literature will rank dissociation rate constants in an attempt to correlate values of k with K. However, a tight K can result from a d D D fast k, as well; meaning off-rate screens could miss potential high affinity mAb a candidates. Plus, the K is normally the more interesting and useful binding param- D eter for pharmacokinetic evaluations under steady-state dosing conditions. Hence a full quantitative affinity screen yielding both k and k is always preferable and more a d informative than off-rate ranking protocols. Advancements in Biacore and other SPR instrumentation have been primarily designed to address higher throughput low resolution screening campaigns. With only four available flow cells, traditional Biacore instruments could only capture three antibodies per cycle while leaving one flow cell as a reference surface (Canziani et al. 2004). Although promising inroads in throughput were made with Biacore’s Flexchip array platform that could simultaneously analyze the interaction of antigen with as many as 96 printed mAbs (Wassaf et al. 2006), this technology has been discontinued. The high-throughput Biacore 4000 platform subdivides each of the four traditional flow cells into five individual spots which provide the capability of capturing eight different antibodies in a single cycle. The 4000 also contains a rack hotel that is capable of housing ten 96-well (or 384-well) plates. Figure 5.3 shows a schematic of the four independent flow cells and the five spots within each flow cell in the 4000 instrument. In low resolution screening protocols, spots 1, 2, 4, and 5 of each flow cell all contain immobilized capturing antibody. In each binding cycle, four different anti-bodies are captured on spots 1 and 5 in all four flow cells. Spots 2 and 4 serve as internal reference surfaces for their juxtaposed spots 1 and 5, respectively. Once capturing is complete, antigen is then injected over all four flow cells simultaneously followed by surface regeneration so that another cycle can be repeated with

Drake et al. Spot 1 Spot 2 Spot 3 Spot 4 Spot 5 Spot 1 Spot 2 Spot 3 Spot 4 Spot 5 Flow cell #1 Flow cell #2 Spot 1 Spot 2 Spot 3 Spot 4 Spot 5 Spot 1 Spot 2 Spot 3 Spot 4 Spot 5 Flow cell #3 Flow cell #4 Fig. 5.3 Experimental design for a low resolution antibody screen for the Biacore 4000. Each of four flow cells is subdivided into five spots. Capturing antibody (shown in gray) is immobilized on spots 1, 2, 4, and 5 of each flow cell. Different antibodies (shown in black) are then captured on spots 1 and 5 of each flow cell leaving spots 2 and 4 as reference surfaces. A single antigen concentration (black circles) is then injected simultaneously over all four flow cells to generate eight sensorgrams of antigen binding to each captured antibody. All capture surfaces are then regenerated so that another cycle of eight antibodies can be studied eight more unique mAbs (Säfsten et al. 2006). Another approach captures a unique mAb over spots 1 and 2 simultaneously over all four flow cells which is followed by using the hydrodynamic addressing of the Biacore 4000 to selectively regenerate spot 1, leaving spot 2 with captured mAb. Then a different mAb is captured over spot 1. When this is repeated for spots 4 and 5 in all four flow cells, the sensor chip in the Biacore 4000 ultimately has captured 16 unique mAbs for low resolution kinetic screening in a single cycle (Kamat and Rafique 2017b). The 4000 has also performed high-throughput screens for single chain variable fragments using similar capture protocols (Leonard et al. 2007). As mentioned in the introduction of Sect. 5.2, other more recent high-throughput platforms include the Biacore 8K and the MASS-1 (16 available spots, 8 injection ports), the MASS-2 (32 available spots, 8 injection ports), the ProteOn XPR 36 (36 spots, 6 injection ports; discontinued but supported until 2020), the IBIS MX96 SPRi (96 spots, 1 injection port), and the upcoming Carterra LSA in 2018 (384 spots, 1 injection port). In most of these higher throughput SPR instruments some of the available “spots” must be used as reference spots. The ability to successfully screen numerous monoclonal antibodies is dependent on capture surfaces that can be reliably and reproducibly regenerated for numerous cycles. For antibodies, this is accomplished by covalently immobilizing high-density surfaces (~7000–12,000 RU) of polyclonal anti-IgG (Fc specific) which are commercially available and are normally robust for the duration of an affinity

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 89 screening experiment. Replicate antibody capture levels should ideally be reproducible within ~5%. If antibody appears to dissociate from the capture surface during an antigen injection, the signal drift can be subtracted by double-referencing with a cycle consisting of mAb capture followed by a buffer injection. This drift subtraction method is not technically correct since the standard kinetic fitting model assumes the surface capacity of antibody remains unchanged during the antigen injection, but it is a practical method for relatively ranking the binding affinities of several hundred mAbs. Rigorously speaking, drifting sensorgram data should be fit with an interaction model that takes into account the drifting signal; the correct model takes into account the changing surface capacity during the association and the dissociation phase of the binding reaction as has been previously published (Joss et al. 1998). As mentioned in Sect. 5.2.2.3, regeneration conditions for polyclonal anti-IgG capture surfaces after each mAb capture/antigen (or buffer) injection cycle typically include short pulses of phosphoric acid or glycine at low pH. When processing sensorgrams from a low resolution screen, a large range of response levels is to be expected since antibody capture levels due to differing titers, antibody binding site activities, and affinities will vary throughout the screening samples. Of course, the reliability of the estimated rate constants will depend on how well the sensorgram profiles with appropriate association and dissociation phase curvature fit a 1:1 kinetic binding model. In cases where long off-rate candidates display virtually no dissociation phase decay, the k may have to be held cond stant at a reasonable minimum value (~10−5 s−1) during sensorgram fitting. Complex sensorgram data are often observed with some antibody candidates and the biochemical significance of these complex profiles can be almost impossible to reliably evaluate. The complexity is most likely due to one of the pitfalls discussed previously and is probably not real in a true biophysical sense. In short, an accurate affinity ranking of multiple therapeutic antibody candidates in a low resolution screen results from sensorgram data possessing enough curvature in both the association and dissociation phases so accurate estimates of k and k can be extracted from a fit a d to a 1:1 kinetic interaction model. Parameters derived from 1:1 fits of individual sensorgrams can be ranked in either tabular or graphical form. Logarithmic plots of k versus k with K affinity isotherms (Canziani et al. 2004) provide a straightford a D ward visual presentation as well as complete kinetic and equilibrium information (Canziani et al. 2004; Säfsten et al. 2006). Figure 5.4 shows an example of a kinetic isotherm plot from Säfsten et al. (2006). 5.2.3.2 Medium Resolution Experiments A medium resolution experiment provides more rigorous kinetic data on the several (5–8) supernatants that showed the highest affinity ranking from a low resolution kinetic screen. Normally 4–6 additional antigen concentrations are injected over the captured supernatants in 1–3 replicates instead of a single high antigen concentration injection as in most low resolution kinetic screens. Medium resolution experiments are limited in the amount of antigen dissociation data that can be measured because captured antibody may begin to dissociate from the capture surface itself after ~45 min. Any signal decay during the dissociation phase would then be a

90 10-5 10-4 10-3 10-2 Affinity isotherms 10 nM 10-1 100 nM 1 µM 10 µM 1 104 105 106 107 Association Rate (M-1S-1)

function of three dissociation events: (1) antigen/mAb complex dissociation from the capture surface, (2) antigen dissociation from captured mAb, and (3) unbound mAb dissociation from the capture surface. Therefore, stable capture surfaces are needed in order to not compromise the measurement of k. In this circumstance, d high-resolution Biacore measurements would be required to measure long off-rates, the particulars of which are discussed below. In fact, it will become apparent in the discussion of high-resolution Biacore experiments that a medium resolution approach to captured purified antibodies, as opposed to antibodies in supernatant, where long off-rate data acquisition is not necessary, is indistinguishable from a high-resolution Biacore experiment. The latest high-throughput, multi-port SPR instruments have opened the ability of researchers to perform high-throughput screening of antigen–antibody kinetics in a medium resolution mode. Kamat and Rafique (2017a, b) recently assessed the reliability of the SICK (single injection cycle kinetics) method for the MASS-1 instrument which features each of its eight flow channels flowing over two independent spots. In this assay, the same antibody is captured at similar levels over single independent spots across the eight flow channels. Subsequently, varying concentrations of the same antigen are simultaneously injected through the eight different injection ports over the mAb captured on the different flow channels. The k and k values of multiple antigen–antibody interactions obtained a d with the SICK method using the MASS-1 were all within twofold of the rate constants obtained with the more traditional MICK (multiple injection cycle kinetics) approach using a Biacore 4000 and the MASS-1 (Kamat and Rafique 2017a, b). )1-S( etaR noitaicossiD A. 5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 91

In a higher throughput mode, the MASS-1 (and the similar Biacore 8K) and the MASS-2 (four spots for each of the eight flow channels) can capture each mAb to, for example, 2–3 independent spots and then perform SICK using different concentrations of antigen over the flow channels corresponding to the capture placement of the identical mAbs, thus generating medium-resolution data for multiple antibodies in a single cycle. Additionally, the six criss-crossing flow channels of a ProteOn XPR 36 instrument allow for capturing six unique antibodies in a “vertical” flow direction and then performing “single- shot kinetics” of six different analyte concentrations in a “horizontal” flow direction over all six captured mAbs in a single cycle (Bravman et al. 2006; Nahshol et al. 2008; Abdiche et al. 2016; Yang et al. 2016). These high-throughput SPR instruments require only days to provide robust medium resolution kinetics on hundreds of unpurified mAbs while traditional Biacore instruments (one flow channel, four flow cells) would require weeks of instrument run time to generate similar results (Kamat and Rafique 2017a, b). 5.2.3.3 High-Resolution Experiments High-resolution Biacore experiments are most often performed when a precise and accurate affinity measurement for a specific purified antibody candidate is needed. Commonly this “hard number” approach assists with internal reporting, publication results, patents, and/or Investigative New Drug (IND) applications, among other examples. A high-resolution Biacore experiment involves injecting six to eight antigen concentrations (at the appropriate range) in triplicate over the immobilized antibody in addition to buffer blanks for double-referencing, and globally fitting the resulting 30–40 sensorgrams. It should be noted that when it is apparent that there is very little signal decay in the dissociation phase in all sensorgrams of a high-resolution data set, it indicates the antibody–antigen complex has a very slow k d (<5 × 10−4 s−1) which is common in very tight complexes (K < 100 pM). Without a D detectible dissociation response, the fitting model would be unable to estimate the off-rate. In this case, several hours of dissociation data are recorded from several additional injections of high antigen concentrations double-referenced with buffer injections (Drake et al. 2004). These additional sensorgrams are globally fit with the full antigen range. Relatively slow dissociation rate constants on the order of 10−5 s−1 have been measured for single-digit picomolar K ’s using this method (Drake et al. D 2004). An example of a high-resolution Biacore data set using this “long off-rate” method is shown in Fig. 5.5a. In this experiment, a monoclonal antibody was covalently coupled at various surface capacities to three flow cells of a Biacore biosensor chip with the fourth flow cell serving as a reference surface. The three panels on the left side of Fig. 5.5a show double-referenced sensorgrams from eight antigen concentrations which were simultaneously injected over each flow cell. Each black line represents three replicate sensorgrams on top of one another, indicating a very reproducible surface. Note how there is almost no signal decay in the dissociation phase data in the left panels. The panels on the right in Fig. 5.5a show three additional double-referenced replicates of the highest antigen concentration injected over the same antibody surfaces but with the dissociation followed for 4 h. With the long off-rate acquisition, enough

The k/k ratio from this high- resolution a d a data calculated a single-digit picomolar affinity for the antigen–antibody complex, which was corroborated by generating similar results using KinEXA®, an independent solution-phase biophysical method, thus validating this novel long off-rate Biacore technique (Drake et al. 2004). KinExA is discussed below in more detail in Sect. 5.3. 5.2.4 Epitope Binning 5.2.4.1 Functional Significance of Epitope The innate ability of antibodies to bind their target antigens with exquisite specificity is leveraged in the discovery of therapeutic antibodies. At the molecular level, this specificity is embodied in the “epitope/paratope” interaction, where the “epitope” refers to the contacts within the target antigen that interact with the antibody and, conversely, the “paratope” refers to the complementary interacting contacts within the antibody structure. In the context of therapeutic antibodies, characterizing an antibody’s epitope is essential to understanding its mechanism of action, predicting antigens for use in vaccines, and providing intellectual property opportunities (Abbott et al. 2014). Since an antibody’s epitope largely dictates its biological function, proper epitope selection is a high priority in discovering therapeutic leads. However, while antibody generation is highly commoditized, with modern in vivo and in vitro technologies producing enormously diverse genetic outputs, proper epitope selection remains an empiric process for two reasons. First, in silico methods have limited utility in predicting epitope/paratope interactions (Blythe and Flower 2005; El-Manzalawy and Honavar 2010; Gao et al. 2012). Second, while most biophysical properties of an antibody, such as its affinity for its specific antigen, melting point, or homogeneity, can be optimized by standard protein engineering methods, an antibody’s epitope cannot be shifted rationally by engineered means. This is exquisitely demonstrated by the observation that an antibody’s precise epitope can be retained upon humanization and affinity maturation (La Porte et al. 2014; Feinberg et al. 2014). Indeed, antibodies that differ slightly in their epitope footprints may have different functional properties (Faleri et al. 2017; Klein et al. 2013), reinforcing the need for biophysical techniques that can resolve fine epitope differences early in the drug discovery process to guide lead selection. Antibodies targeting novel epitopes administered as single agents or in combinations are showing clinical promise in various therapeutic areas, notably cancer (Pedersen et al. 2010; Klitgaard et al. 2013; Teeling et al. 2006; Emde et al. 2011; Ascierto et al. 2015; Pardoll 2012; Jamnani et al. 2012) and vaccines (Sapparapu et al. 2016; Zwick et al. 2003).

94 A. W. Drake et al. 5.2.4.2 Biophysical Tools for Defining an Epitope The gold standard for defining the architecture of an epitope/paratope interface is via atomic level structural data as provided by biophysical techniques such as X-ray crystallography (Abbott et al. 2014), nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy (Blech et al. 2013), or, more recently, cryo-electron microscopy (Renaud et al. 2016). Mass spectrometry is also used to probe the residues at the epitope/paratope interface via electrospray (Millar et al. 1998) or hydrogen-deuterium exchange (Faleri et al. 2017; Chalmers et al. 2011). However, in acquiring atomic level details of an antibody’s epitope, there is trade-off in terms of practical factors, since these techniques are both time-consuming and resource-intensive, limiting their application to the characterization of lead molecules rather than being early-stage predictive tools for guiding decision-making. Even when the “structural epitope” is well defined, the energetic contributions of the encompassed and peripheral residues to the affinity of antigen binding, whether critical or incidental, must be determined experimentally, often via antigen mutagenesis strategies. The results from these empirical studies thus define the “functional epitope” (Rojas et al. 2014), which often represents a small subset of the residues defined by the structural epitope (Clackson et al. 1998; See also Chap. 4). 5.2.4.3 Epitope Binning Using Label-Free Biosensors In contrast to the methods described above, label-free real-time biosensors, such as those based on surface plasmon resonance (SPR) or biolayer interferometry (BLI) detection, are commonly used to perform epitope binning assays on panels of anti-bodies to sort them into families or “bins” based upon their ability to block one another in a pairwise and combinatorial manner for binding to their specific antigen (Abdiche et al. 2009). The results of these assays reveal the depth and breadth of an antibody panel’s epitope coverage in a relative sense, without providing any details about the epitope contacts, but are tremendously useful in comparing antibodies both within and across libraries (Abdiche et al. 2016), discriminating fine specificities (Abdiche et al. 2014; Sivasubramanian et al. 2016), and identifying antibodies with novel epitopes (Mousa et al. 2016). In an epitope binning experiment, two antibodies that can bind simultaneously to their specific antigen are presumed to bind at nonoverlapping epitopes, forming a so-called “sandwich pair,” whereas two antibodies that cannot bind simultaneously to their specific antigen are presumed to target overlapping or identical epitopes, and are termed a “blocking pair.” However, cross-blocking of two antibodies is required but not sufficient to place them into the same bin. Only those antibodies sharing similar or identical blocking behaviors are grouped together in a “bin,” which is a relative term defined by the epitope diversity of the antibodies in the test panel. Adding one more antibody to a preexisting panel may provide sufficient discriminating power to fracture a preexisting bin into sub-bins. Indeed, the epitope resolution of a binning study increases with the size of the antibody panel, allowing fine differences to be discriminated. Furthermore, epitopes can be structurally inferred from a binning experiment by including within the panel, if available, antibody standards with crystallographically defined epitopes (Sivasubramanian et al. 2016; see also Chap. 4).

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 95

5.2.4.4 Visualizing the Results from an Epitope Binning Experiment Binning results are typically represented in terms of a blocking interaction matrix or “heat map” where sandwiching and blocking antibody pairs are represented by contrasting colors as shown in the left panels of Fig. 5.6. Sorted heat maps show the primary antibodies or “ligands” (indicated by the rows) and their transposed counterparts as secondary antibodies or “analytes” (indicated by the columns). The “selfblocking” controls are shown along the diagonal of the matrix (as bold and shaded cells), indicating the binning outcome when the same antibody is used in the role of both analyte and ligand. Epitope bins are also visualized as community clusters or networks that emphasize the blocking relationships, as popularized by Wasatch Microfluidics Inc’s (Salt Lake City, UT) software. In a “network blocking plot” (Fig. 5.6, right panels), antibodies that block one another are connected by a chord and antibodies sharing the same blocking relationships are inscribed by an envelope to denote the “bins.” Some antibody panels yield several discrete bins (Fig. 5.6a), while other antibody panels yield an intricate network of overlapping bins (Fig. 5.6b). Heat maps (left) and corresponding network blocking plots (right) for (a) a panel of 16 antibodies that fall into 9 discrete antibody clusters (adapted from Abdiche et al. 2014) and (b) a panel of 19 antibodies that fall into 8 bins of which all but one were part of a highly overlapping network (previously unpublished result)

96 A. W. Drake et al. Since bin members target similar or identical epitopes, they often share functional similarities. By selecting “bin representatives” for further characterization, a large panel of antibodies can be efficiently distilled to a subset that is representative of the entire panel’s epitope diversity. Epitope binning assays are therefore appealing to early stage drug discovery where large numbers of antibodies are available in limited quantities because they can be performed in high throughput and with minimal sample consumption, especially when employing array-based SPR imaging. Furthermore, these assays are universally applicable to any antibody panel because they only require the antibodies and the antigen, making them simpler and more convenient to perform than epitope mapping studies, which require specialized reagents, such as a panel of antigen variants or fragment display libraries (Hudson et al. 2012), and functional cell-based assays that must be tailored per target, may require highly skilled staff (e.g., when performing neuron survival assays or applying electrophysiology protocols), and generally require a solid understanding of the biology of the model system being studied. Merging binning results with data from independent assays such as functional screens, cross-reactivity data, or sequence data allows a deeper appreciation of the unique properties of each antibody, thereby informing the selection of clones with therapeutically relevant properties (Yeung et al. 2016; Mousa et al. 2016). 5.2.4.5 Epitope Binning Assays Can Be Performed in Various Assay Formats In the context of a label-free biosensor experiment, epitope binning assays can be oriented in various ways and are typically performed in one of three assay formats: in tandem, premix, and classical sandwich (Fig. 5.7). These formats differ in whether the antigen is on the sensor (in tandem) or in solution (premix and classical sandwich) and whether the antigen binds the first (or primary) antibody in solution (premix) or on the sensor (in tandem and classical sandwich). Each assay format is now described in turn along with its strengths and limitations. a b c Antibody 2 Homogeneous antigen Antibody 1 Sensor surface Fig. 5.7 Outline of three biosensor-based assay formats that can be used to perform epitope binning experiments on a panel of antibodies; (a) in tandem blocking, (b) premix blocking, and (c) classical sandwich. When using a homogeneous, monovalent antigen, the binning outcome is typically invariant of the assay format employed. For example, if two antibodies target nonoverlapping epitope on their specific antigen, they will appear to form a sandwich pair in all assay formats. Conversely, if they target overlapping epitopes, they will appear to block one another

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 97 In Tandem Assay Format In an “in tandem” format (Fig. 5.7a), two antibodies (herein, termed the primary antibody and the secondary antibody) are presented one after another to an immobilized antigen. If the primary antibody saturates its epitopes on the antigen, it should block or not block the binding of a secondary antibody if the two antibodies target overlapping or nonoverlapping epitopes, respectively. To promote a solution-like presentation of an antigen’s epitopes upon its immobilization, care must be taken to preserve the antigen’s native conformation. Therefore, it is preferable to employ a reversible capture approach via the use of a tag (e.g., a Histidine-tag or an Fc-fusion partner), rather than direct coupling and cross-linking the antigen to the surface. However, site-directed captures can mask epitopes that reside at or near the capture site. Despite these limitations, antigen capture requires knowledge of only one regeneration condition, namely that of the capture surface itself, so can be routinely implemented with any new antigen/antibody panel with minimal optimization. Capturing the antigen also bypasses the need to regenerate it, which may otherwise adversely affect its binding properties (Abdiche et al. 2012). A significant practical drawback of the “in tandem” format, in addition to the possible exclusion of epitopes upon even gentle antigen immobilization, is that it relies upon the primary antibody not only saturating its epitopes on the antigen, but also remaining bound during the binding of the secondary antibody. This can necessitate the use of high concentrations of the primary antibody, typically 100-fold its K towards the anti- D gen, or higher, due to the inherent inaccessibility of some epitopes as a function of the antigen being immobilized. Even when a primary antibody saturates its epitopes, those with fast dissociation rate constants will start falling off and freeing up epitopes that can be subsequently bound up by the secondary antibody step, resulting in false “positive” signals that may confound the interpretation of the results (Abdiche et al. 2009). Premix Assay Format In a premix assay format (Fig. 5.7b), the antigen is incubated in solution with a molar excess of the primary antibody sufficient to saturate its epitopes, and this equilibrated mixture is then presented to a secondary antibody, immobilized on the sensor. If the solution and immobilized antibodies compete for overlapping epitopes on their specific antigen, then no signal will be detected by the immobilized antibody. Conversely, if the solution and immobilized antibodies bind at distinct, nonoverlapping epitopes, the immobilized antibody will detect the antigen/primary antibody premixed complex, giving a signal that is often larger than that for antigen alone, due to the associated increase in mass. Like the “in tandem” method, this assay format is also amenable to multivalent antigens but requires that the primary antibody is premixed at a high enough concentration to saturate its epitopes. Sometimes, an antigen/antibody premixed complex diffuses to the immobilized antibody at a slower rate than the antigen alone, manifesting as an intermediate signal that is smaller than that of antigen alone, during the allowed association time, which may be erroneously interpreted as a “partial block.” To determine whether the binding response is due to antigen alone or an antigen/antibody premixed complex,

98 A. W. Drake et al. an appropriate secondary detection can be appended to the binding cycle to differentiate the solution and immobilized antibodies from one another. For example, if a mouse antibody is in solution and a human antibody is immobilized, an injection of an anti-mouse secondary polyclonal antibody will show binding to the surface-bound premixed complex of mouse antibody/antigen and will not bind to surface-bound antigen alone. In practice, a premixed antibody that fully blocks binding to its immobilized counterpart, a so-called “self-block,” confirms that the premixed antibody is saturating its epitopes on the antigen, per the detection sensitivity of that antibody, bearing in mind that a high affinity antibody may be able to detect a lower free antigen concentration than a low affinity antibody. The concentration of premixed antibody used should be sufficient to saturate (or nearly saturate) its epitopes on the antigen, but no higher, to avoid introducing unspecific cross-reactions between the antibodies themselves (on surface and in solution), which can confound the interpretation of the results as an additional source of binding that is independent of antigen. Another source of potential “false positive” signals that can be introduced when binning is performed on an antibody array, as used for both the premix and classical sandwich formats (discussed below), is that solution antibodies can sometimes bind nonspecifically to immobilized antibodies. This can be assessed by screening the solution antibodies over the antibody array, in the absence of any antigen. Additionally, interspersing replicate cycles of the antigen alone at its identical premixed concentration throughout the binning experiment not only provides the expected binding response for each immobilized antibody (Abdiche 2010), but also monitors the activity of each immobilized antibody as the experiment progresses. Classical Sandwich Assay Format In a classical sandwich assay format (Fig. 5.7c), solution antibodies are tested for their ability to bind antigen that is first captured via an immobilized antibody. It is probably the most widely employed due to its facile setup and ease of interpretation. Unlike the in tandem and premix methods that rely upon efficient saturation of the antigen with a concomitant need for high antibody concentrations, antibodies can be used at low concentrations when analyzed in a classical sandwich assay format. While a practical limitation of the classical and premix methods is the need to regenerate the antibody surfaces to enable their efficient reuse, a regeneration condition can often be scouted that is successful for most surfaces. Depending upon the throughput of the assay and the platform employed, regeneration may be bypassed entirely, for example, by employing the Octet’s single-use disposable sensors (Abdiche et al. 2014). A significant advantage of the classical sandwich assay format is that it can reveal cases of antibody displacements, wherein two antibodies kinetically perturb one another’s binding to their specific antigen, via the formation of a transient “sandwiching pair” that rapidly decays to expel one antibody and retain the other (Fig. 5.8a). When epitope binning assays are performed in high throughput, the probability of identifying such behaviors increases and are easily recognized by an inverted sandwiching response (Fig. 5.8b). Displacements observed for antibodies with

99 a b Analyte * Antigen Displace Ligand Transient sandwich complex Time (s) crystallographically defined epitopes imply that antibodies with closely adjacent or minimally overlapping epitopes can displace one another (Abdiche et al. 2017). 5.2.4.6 A Heterogeneous Antigen Can Confound the Interpretation of a Binning Assay Due to an interplay or practical factors, including the available quantity and quality of the antigen and antibodies, the valency of the antigen, and the antigen-binding affinities of the antibodies, one assay format may be deemed more appropriate than another to perform (Table 5.1). With a homogeneous antigen, the binning outcome should be independent of the assay format employed. In contrast, binning with a heterogeneous antigen can lead to an assay-dependent discordance in the results (Fig. 5.9), which can be used to diagnose whether the antigen comprises a mixture of subpopulations, e.g., a native form and a denatured form (Abdiche et al. 2012). 5.2.4.7 Higher Throughput Label-Free Platforms Enable Higher Resolution of Epitope Diversity The emergence of array-based label-free platforms that enable highly parallel interaction analysis has allowed large-scale epitope binning studies to be performed routinely. These studies reveal a deep insight into the epitope landscape of an antibody panel and facilitate the discrimination of fine epitope specificities that would otherwise be overlooked. For example, in a study by Yeung et al. (2016), 67 unique antibodies produced from single B-cell cloning targeting the staphylococcal protein IsdB, involved in iron acquisition, were comprehensively tested for their pairwise cross-blocking using SPR imaging in a 96-ligand array format, and sorted into 24 epitope bins, named A–X.
