In another study, Sivasubramanian et al. (2016) performed an epitope binning on a human in vitro antibody library targeting hen egg white lysozyme using SPR imaging in a 384-ligand array format. Emerging from this study were two main bins and several rare bins with unique epitopes when benchmarked against literature clones with known epitopes. In addition, the study enabled the identification of library-derived antibodies that could discriminate the epitopes of two near-identical literature antibodies (HyHEL-5 and F10.6.6), differing only by three amino acids. The results herein demonstrate the molecular level insights into epitope diversity that is enabled when binning assays are performed in high throughput, which can be used to inform the discovery of therapeutic antibodies with novel epitopes. 5.3 KinExA Technology Another biophysical method that has gained traction for measuring the affinities of antibody–antigen complexes is KinExA (kinetic exclusion assay) technology (Blake et al. 1999; Ohmura et al. 2001; Darling and Brault 2004). The KinExA instrument is essentially a flow spectrofluorimeter designed to measure the amount of free monoclonal antibody binding sites in equilibrated solutions of various antigen concentrations titrated into a constant monoclonal antibody binding site concentration (twice the molecular concentration). To accomplish this, each

102 A. W. Drake et al. equilibrated antibody–antigen solution flows through a flow cell containing a small column (~8 μL) of bead resin to which antigen is adsorbed or covalently coupled. As each solution flows through the packed bead column, a portion of unbound antibody sites bind to the resin while free antigen and antibody completely bound with antigen (both binding sites complexed with antigen) flow through the column (Fig. 5.10). A secondary fluorescently labeled polyclonal anti-species Fc specific or antiheavy and light chain antibody is then flowed through the resin followed by a brief wash of the column. The amount of fluorescence detected from an excitation lamp directed through the bead pack is then converted to a percentage of free antibody binding sites in each equilibrated solution. A plot of the percent free antibody binding sites as a function of total antigen concentration in each solution is then fit to a simple 1:1 equilibrium binding model to estimate the K of the antibody–antigen D complex. The abundance of choices for fluorescently labeled secondary polyclonal antibodies available commercially makes KinExA ideally suited for measuring antibody–antigen interactions. Furthermore, the fluorescence-based nature of the instrument provides a very low detection limit allowing measurement of very tight equilibrium dissociation constants. | Ag | Ag | | | — | — | — | g A | Ag Ag | | | | —– | — | — | Ag g | A | A | | | — | — | — | g | AAgg Ag Ag | AgAg | | | ———- | —- | ——— | | Ag | Ag | | | Ag Ag | Ag | | | Ag | Ag | < 0.5 sec | | Ag | Ag | | | Ag Ag | Ag | | | Ag | Ag | | | Ag Ag | Ag | | Ag Ag Ag Ag Ag Ag g A Fig. 5.10 Kinetic Exclusion Assay (KinExA) technology for measuring K. Equilibrated solu- D tions of mAb–antigen complexes at various concentrations of antigen and a constant concentration of mAb binding sites flow through a column of beads coated with antigen. A small fraction of unbound mAb binding sites are captured to the bead resin where fluorescence from a secondary fluorescently labeled antibody is directly proportional to the concentration of free mAb binding sites in each equilibrated solution. The % free antibody binding sites plotted as a function of antigen concentration is fit to a simple 1:1 equilibrium model to estimate the K of the mAb–antigen D complex in solution. (Reproduced with permission. Sapidyne, Inc., Boise, ID)

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 103 5.3.1 Kinetic Exclusion The kinetic exclusion concept behind KinExA technology assures that the amount of antibody captured on the column resin represents a true quantitative representation of the amount of free antibody binding sites in each equilibrated solution. A measure of the antibody binding site concentration in equilibrated mixtures of antibody and monovalent antigen yields the preferred intrinsic site-binding K of the D antibody–antigen complex instead of just a stoichiometric or macroscopic equilibrium constant. The exposure time of solution to the bead pack volume is less than half of a second, resulting in only ~5% capture efficiency of any free antibody binding sites (Ohmura et al. 2001). Hence, the short contact time assures that any dissociation of antibody–antigen complexes while solution passes over the bead resin is kinetically excluded. In other words, no significant equilibrium shift of the antibody–antigen solution occurs while flowing over the bead pack, resulting in a true measurement of free binding sites in each equilibrated sample. Of course, an antibody captured to the bead pack with either one or both binding sites not complexed with antigen produces the same fluorescent signal. This would appear to prevent the fluorescent signal from correctly quantifying the true free antibody binding site concentration in solution. However, based on mathematical arguments, antibodies with only one antigen bound are only captured with half the probability of antibodies having both binding sites free (when the capture efficiency to the bead pack is ~5–30%), hence the total fluorescent signal is truly representative of the free antibody binding site concentration (Lackie and Glass 2001; Ohmura et al. 2001; Glass and Winzor 2014; Sapidyne Instruments Inc. 2015a, b). 5.3.2 K -Controlled and Antibody-Controlled KinExA D Equilibrium Titrations It is crucial to consider the experimental titration conditions that can affect the shape of a KinExA equilibrium titration curve and what information is available from the curve. The shape of the titration curve in KinExA, in fact, depends on the ratio of the antibody binding site concentration to the K of the interaction. When the anti- D body binding site concentration is much greater than the K, the curve shape is D insensitive to changes in the K but sensitive to changes in the active antibody bind- D ing site concentration. This “antibody-controlled” curve mostly contains antibody binding site concentration information and minimal affinity information. When the antibody binding site concentration is near or less than the K of the antibody–anti- D gen complex, the shape of the titration curve is affected by changes in the K but D will not change as the antibody binding site concentration changes within this concentration range. This “K -controlled” curve contains affinity information and very D minimal information on the antibody binding site concentration. It is preferable to generate both types of equilibrium curves and then simultaneously fit both curves in a dual curve analysis to estimate the active antibody binding site concentration and the K. An example of a KinExA dual curve analysis for an antibody–antigen D

104 A. W. Drake et al. complex is shown in Fig. 5.5b and will be discussed in more detail in Sect. 5.3.6. More than two titration curves may also be used in an “n-curve” analysis to even more rigorously determine the K and free mAb binding site. Dual and n-curve D analyses provide more rigorously obtained values for the K and the antibody bind- D ing site concentration as compared to values obtained if individual curves were fit locally. Having multiple titration curves simultaneously (and successfully) fit to a simple 1:1 interaction model also provides extra assurance that a 1:1 binding model correctly describes the nature of the equilibrium binding reaction. With a wellbehaved antibody–antigen system, it is likely that a single K -controlled curve gen- D erated under the proper titration conditions will yield a K value equivalent to the D K value generated from a multi-curve analysis and provide accurate concentration D information (Bee et al. 2012). This can be beneficial if either the affinities of several different antibody–antigen complexes or several replicate K measurements of a D single antibody–antigen complex need to be quickly measured. For the latter case, several measurements of course provide a 95% confidence interval based on several independent replicate experiments for a more statistically rigorous K value that D considers errors from independent experiments. KinExA software, in contrast, provides a 95% confidence interval of the parameters derived from fitting a single data set and do not represent a statistical precision of several independently measured K D values for a single antibody–antigen complex. 5.3.3 KinExA Kinetic Measurements KinExA can also be used to measure the association kinetics of antibody–antigen complexes. The simplest technique is the “kinetics direct” method which measures the free antibody binding site concentration as a solution of antigen and antibody approaches equilibrium. A bimolecular rate equation is subsequently fit to the % free antibody binding site concentration as a function of time to calculate k. An a example of a kinetics direct association curve for an antibody–antigen complex approaching equilibrium is shown in Fig. 5.5c and will be discussed further in Sect. 5.3.6. A kinetics direct experiment follows the association kinetics for several hours in KinExA, whereas restrictions in the amount of volume that can be injected in Biacore experiments limit association data acquisition time to 2–10 min (assuming Biacore flow rates are not slow enough to induce artifacts from mass transport). Another kinetic method called the “kinetics injection” method is performed by mixing various antigen concentrations with a constant antibody concentration. A simple reversible bimolecular rate equation then estimates k from a plot of the % free a antibody binding site concentration as a function of the antigen concentration. Because the KinExA instrument must initially preload sample lines and charge the first bead column, it typically takes a post-mixing time of approximately 7–9 min to acquire the first data point in a kinetics direct experiment. Acquiring each subsequent data point in the direct method is normally on the order of 5–15 min, therefore complexes that equilibrate faster than an hour or so are more suited for the kinetics injection method or another technique called the “manual kinetics direct” method

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 105 which is only possible with a KinExA 3200 model having an available autosampler connected as a separate module, or with the more recent KinExA 4000 model which features an integrated autosampler. An autosampler not only allows the user to charge the bead column prior to mixing the antigen and antibody samples, but it also does not require pre-charging of a sample line. Therefore, for the manual kinetics direct method the user can choose to acquire a single data point at any time after mixing the samples. The user can then “back fill” early kinetic data time points of a quickly equilibrating antibody–antigen complex that would have been missed with a traditional kinetics direct method (Sapidyne Instruments Inc. 2014). The dissociation rate constant is not normally or easily measured directly with KinExA instrumentation. Unlike in a high-resolution Biacore experiment where k and k are a d measured to calculate K (Eq. 5.6), KinExA requires both an equilibrium experi- D ment to measure K and an independent kinetic experiment to measure k, and then D a k is calculated from multiplying K by k. d D a Recent modifications have been added to the KinExA control software for measuring directly the k of antibody–antigen complexes that are extremely slow. Here d fluorescently tagged antigen is added to a solution of antibody pre-equilibrated with unlabeled antigen while the bead resin is coupled with an unlabeled anti-species polyclonal antibody to the mAb of interest (a “chase pulse” experiment in a sense). The experiment is run at antigen and mAb concentrations that lead to stoichiometric or quantitative binding. The rate of formation of antibody complexed with labeled antigen is monitored with KinExA by measuring the increase in fluorescence over time as more and more labeled complexes are formed from dissociation of unlabeled antigen and hence captured by the bead resin. If the dissociation rate of the unlabeled antigen is very slow, the increase in the fluorescence may only be measurable when data points are taken at intervals on the order of hours or even days, hence the flexibility in the software for the KinExA user to select an appropriate interval time between cycles. The resulting increase in fluorescence is then modeled with a kinetic algorithm to estimate the k of the unlabeled antibody–antigen complex. d Ideally, a simultaneous fit with data from a reversed orientation KinExA experiment (a “pulse chase” experiment) measuring the decrease in fluorescence over time when unlabeled antigen is added to a solution of antibody pre-complexed with labeled antigen can ensure that the dissociation rate constant is identical for antibody binding to both the labeled and unlabeled forms of the antigen, thus proving the labeling of the antigen did not artifactually alter the dissociation kinetics of the first experiment described above. 5.3.4 KinExA Experimental Methods As with any biophysical technique, generating reliable high quality data using a KinExA instrument depends on correctly designing and performing equilibrium and kinetic experiments. First, for equilibrium experiments, antibody–antigen solutions must be allowed enough time to equilibrate. Time to equilibrium of any complex is driven by k and the reactant concentrations relative to the K, hence d D

106 A. W. Drake et al. antibody–antigen complexes that have extremely slow off-rates (often corresponding to single-digit picomolar, pM, K values) can take days to weeks to reach equi- D librium (Drake et al. 2004). For example, consider an antibody–antigen complex having a k and k of 7 × 105 M−1 s−1 and 2 × 10−5 s−1 (K = 29 pM), respectively, and a d D a solution containing 29 pM each of antigen and antibody binding sites. This reaction requires 35 h to reach equilibrium (Drake et al. 2004). Second, in order to generate a K -controlled curve for extremely tight antibody–antigen complexes, the D antibody binding site concentration may have to be in the single-digit picomolar range or lower. Because of the low capture efficiency of free antibody binding sites to the bead pack, this could require tens of milliliters of equilibrated solution to flow through the KinExA bead pack in order generate a detectible and reproducible signal. Tens of milliliters of sample are needed for KinExA kinetic experiments as well. Preparation of these larger volumes of samples should follow traditional analytical volumetric techniques for the most reliable kinetic and equilibrium results. For example, meticulously cleaned glass volumetric flasks should always be used, and the use of properly calibrated (and cleaned) reusable glass pipettes should always be favored over disposable plastic pipettes for sample preparation. Third, per the discussion above to generate K -controlled equilibrium titration curve data, the D active antibody binding site concentration used should be no greater than threefold above the K so that the titration curve possesses as much K information as possi- D D ble. If the KinExA equilibrium data is of exceptional quality, it is possible to determine a K even with the antibody binding site concentration being as much as D tenfold greater than the K. This is helpful for measuring very tight affinities in the D single-digit picomolar range (or lower) in that it allows for a lower volume of antibody–antigen sample to be flowed through the bead pack for detectible and reproducible fluorescent signals, thus decreasing experimental run time. Because of the larger sample volumes needed for very tight affinities, KinExA equilibrium experiments can require multiple days of instrument time. Fourth, when generating an antibody-controlled equilibrium curve, the antibody binding site concentration should be greater than or equal to tenfold above the K. Fifth, when designing a D kinetics direct experiment, always choose final binding site concentrations after mixing of antigen and antibody so that at least 80% of the total antibody binding sites will be bound at equilibrium. This can be monitored by comparing the fluorescent signal acquired at equilibrium compared to the fluorescent signals seen in equilibrium titrations at 100% free antibody and 0% free antibody. Furthermore, choose antigen and antibody binding site concentrations that will provide enough points within the 5–10 min/point time resolution of the kinetics direct method so that the fitting model has enough data points in the curved portion of the exponential to accurately estimate k. Sixth, for the kinetics inject method, choose a range of antia gen concentrations that result in 20–100% of the chosen constant antibody binding site concentration to be bound in the short mixing time at the given flow rate for the experiment. Lastly, because the 1:1 interaction models that fit KinExA equilibrium and kinetic experiments are dependent on the hard-coded values of antigen concentration used in the assay, the most rigorous concentration measurement methods should always be used for all protein reagents (Pace et al. 1995; Grimsley and Pace

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 107 2003), keeping in mind the binding site concentrations must always be used for data analysis. Of course, measuring an accurate antigen concentration does not necessarily give any indication of the active antigen concentration. It is therefore important that careful attention is paid to the “ABC” (antibody binding site concentration) parameter returned by the KinExA “standard affinity” equilibrium model, especially if an antibody-controlled curve is globally fit in a dual-curve analysis with a K -controlled titration curve. The standard affinity model assumes the hard-coded D antigen concentrations are 100% active while the ABC is allowed to float. In a dual-curve analysis, the ABC parameter should match closely with the nominal ABC used in the experiment when fitting with the standard affinity model. If the nominal ABC falls outside the lowest value of the returned 95% confidence interval range of the calculated ABC, then it signifies the activity of the antigen is significantly lower than the hard-coded nominal antigen concentration. Here the standard affinity model is forced to increase the estimation of ABC in accordance with the discrepancy between the active antigen concentration and the input nominal antigen concentration. As a result, the calculated K is artifactually higher (less tight) than the D true value. Barring any significant antigen concentration measurement errors or sample preparation pipetting errors, one would instead have to make the nonsensical assumption that the active ABC is greater than 100% of the nominal ABC to believe the standard affinity model. It makes more physical sense to assume a less than 100% active antigen. Therefore, when the returned ABC in a dual- or multi-curve analysis is much greater than the nominal ABC using the standard affinity model, equilibrium data should instead be analyzed using the “antigen unknown” model in the KinExA software. The antigen unknown model assumes full activity in the hard-coded ABC value(s) while floating a correction factor for the hard-coded antigen concentration values. A ligand concentration multiplier (LCM) is generated from this model which is the factor that corrects the nominal antigen concentrations to active concentrations. It should be remembered that if the antigen unknown model is used (when necessary) to measure the K in a KinExA equilibrium experi- D ment, the antigen activity calculated from the LCM must be applied to the same lot of antigen when used for acquiring complementary binding data with other independent biophysical methodologies (i.e., SPR). In other words, all SPR measurements should use the active antigen concentration (antigen nominal concentration × LCM) determined from the KinExA experiments. Of course, it would be ideal if the active concentrations of both binding partners could be determined. As described in Sect. 5.2.2.2 of this chapter, Biacore’s Calibration-Free Concentration Analysis (CFCA) is a valuable technique to complement KinExA measurements by measuring protein activity directly (Bee et al. 2012, 2013). 5.3.5 Antibody Intramolecular Binding Cooperativity When a simple 1:1 equilibrium model fails to properly describe a KinExA equilibrium data set, cooperativity phenomena between the two antibody binding sites must be considered (Blake et al. 2003). When both binding sites of an antibody act

108 A. W. Drake et al. independently with the same characteristic affinity whether one or both sites become occupied with antigen, KinExA data should follow a simple 1:1 equilibrium binding model. When it appears the %free mAb binding site titration curve decreases more steeply than what would be predicted from an independent binding site model, the only conclusion that can be drawn is that antigen binding to one antibody binding site is having a positively cooperative effect on the affinity of the second antibody binding site. In other words, after one mAb binding site is bound, the remaining site has an increased affinity for antigen. At lower antigen concentrations, most antibodies bind to one antigen with the same affinity. At higher antigen concentrations, more antigen is available to bind to the second unoccupied antibody binding site, therefore the effects of positive cooperativity become apparent. The “n-curve” module in the KinExA software includes the option to fit for positive cooperativity. This positive cooperativity model should only be used with great caution and all other sources such as a large amount of experimental noise in the titration curves should be ruled out as the cause of a poor fit to a 1:1 equilibrium model. Alternatively, it is more difficult to distinguish the cause of a KinExA equilibrium curve showing a shallower decrease in %free antibody binding sites than expected. Such “flattening” of a KinExA curve could not only result from a singly bound antigen negatively affecting the affinity of the second antibody binding site (negative cooperativity), but also from the existence of structural variations among the antibody binding sites in solution providing multiple classes of independent antibody binding sites (Blake et al. 2003). Hence an assumption of negative cooperativity cannot be made in this case whereas positive cooperativity can be the only cause of a steeper decrease of %free mAb binding site seen in a KinExA titration curve (Sato et al. 2012). 5.3.6 KinExA and Biacore Comparison The major advantage KinExA has over Biacore for measuring affinities of antibody–antigen complexes is that binding data are collected after equilibrium has already been reached. In contrast, Biacore affinity measurements rely on real-time kinetic data which can occasionally yield sensorgrams deviating from a simple 1:1 kinetic binding model, especially if the injected antigen is multivalent. If complex kinetic binding responses are acquired using Biacore, the data cannot be described with the correct complex binding model without the assistance of a more rigorous examination of the structural biology of the binding partners. KinExA measures K D independently of kinetics and avoids this limitation. In fact, most KinExA equilibrium binding data for antibody binding to multivalent antigen are described well by a simple 1:1 equilibrium binding model, but the same binding constant ambiguity exists as was described for Biacore: when equilibrium KinExA data of antibody complexing with multivalent antigen is described well by a simple 1:1 binding model, it is unknown without further studies beyond the scope of KinExA whether the affinity measured is the intrinsic binding-site equilibrium dissociation constant or a stoichiometric equilibrium dissociation constant.

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 109 One of the first comparisons of solution-based biophysical methods, specifically ITC and stopped-flow fluorescence, to surface plasmon resonance showed equivalent binding constants were obtained with small molecules binding to carbonic anhydrase II (Day et al. 2002). Drake et al. (2004) later showed solution-based KinExA and surface-based Biacore yielded similar kinetic and equilibrium binding constants for three antibody–antigen interactions having affinities ranging from single-digit nanomolar to single-digit picomolar. Panels b and c in Fig. 5.5 show, respectively, KinExA equilibrium and kinetics data sets measuring the binding constants of the same “antigen-2′/mAb 2” complex measured using the long off-rate Biacore technique as described in Sect. 5.2.3.3 (Drake et al. 2004). Figure 5.5b shows a dual curve analysis of both a monoclonal antibody-controlled curve (red line) and a K -controlled curve (blue line). The equilibrium data fit well to a simple D 1:1 interaction model. Figure 5.5c shows the percent free antibody binding site concentration measured as a function of time as a solution of antigen-2′ and mAb-2 approached equilibrium. The K of the antibody–antigen complex as measured in D this KinExA replicate was 3.8 pM and the measured k was 2.5 × 106 M−1 s−1, which a results in a k of 9.5 × 10−6 s−1. The k measured with KinExA for the data set shown d a in Fig. 5.5c (2.5 × 106 M−1 s−1) was virtually identical to the Biacore measurement shown in Fig. 5.5a (2.5 × 106 M−1 s−1). The calculated KinExA value for k was also d very close to the k measured with Biacore (1.6 × 10−5 s−1), hence the affinities mead sured using KinExA (3.8 pM) and Biacore (6.1 pM) were also comparable. Table 5.2 reproduces the results of the binding constants for three antibody–antigen complexes measured in multiple replicates using Biacore and KinExA (Drake et al. 2004). Table 5.2 represents data from the first rigorous comparison study between Biacore and KinExA for the measurement of such tight antibody–antigen complexes. First, the results in Table 5.2 show the measured K ’s varied on average D by ~50% between the two techniques across a three order magnitude range of affinity, which is very good considering the 95% confidence intervals. Second, it is also apparent the k ’s measured by Biacore using the long off-rate method for the antid gen- 2′/mAb-2 complex were very precise, varying by only ~12%. Third, there was very good agreement in measuring both rate constants where the k varied on avera age by only ~33% and the k differed on average by only ~44% between the two d technologies. Overall, the data in Table 5.2 show that similar results were obtained Table 5.2 Equilibrium dissociation constants and rate constants measured for three antibody– antigen complexes with Biacore and KinExA Interaction Method k a [M−1 s−1] k d [s−1] K D [pM] Antigen-1/mAb-1 Biacore 4.6 (0.1) × 105 1.2 (0.1) × 10−3 2500 (500) Antigen-1/mAb-1 KinExA 3.3 (0.6) × 105 4.3 (0.7) × 10−4 1300 (300) Antigen-2/mAb-2 Biacore 6.6 (1.5) × 105 2.1 (0.5) × 10−5 33 (11) Antigen-2/mAb-2 KinExA 1.1 (0.2) × 106 1.3 (0.3) × 10−5 12 (1) Antigen-2′/mAb-2 Biacore 2.7 (0.6) × 106 1.6 (0.2) × 10−5 6.2 (0.8) Antigen-2′/mAb-2 KinExA 2.8 (0.5) × 106 1.1 (0.2) × 10−5 4.0 (1.9) Errors in parentheses are 95% confidence intervals of multiple replicate measurements. 110 A. W. Drake et al. from two independent biophysical techniques, one surface-based and the other solution- based. With proper experimental design, these results highlight that two different and complementary biophysical methodologies can reliably measure binding parameters for very tight antibody–antigen interactions. Other studies have shown how solution-based and surface-based biophysical technologies can provide rigorous complementary information when studying more complex interactions between monoclonal antibodies and multivalent antigen (Abdiche et al. 2008). Certainly, it is not totally correct to assume that when properly using a surface-based biosensor instrument that all kinetic rate constants and equilibrium dissociation constants measured for antibody–antigen complexes will exactly match those measured using a solution-phase technique, especially for complexes having picomolar affinities. Drake et al. (2012) performed 53 independent measurements of the kinetics and affinity of a single antigen/mAb complex using both SPR kinetics and solution-based methods and showed that significant discrepancies in the measured binding parameters existed not only between SPR and solution-based measurements, but also between Biacore measurements conducted with biosensor chips containing varying surface charges. In this study the three Biacore chips used (CM5, CM4, C1) contained varying levels of carboxyl groups and dextran, specifically the CM4 and C1 chips have 30% and 10% of the total carboxyl groups, respectively, as a standard CM5 chip, which therefore parallels the differing negative surface charges of each chip. In addition, the CM5 and CM4 chips feature carboxyl groups within a fluid-like dextran matrix while the CM1 chip has no dextran layer (GE Healthcare 2005). Kinetics were measured with each of these chips when the mAb was covalently immobilized using amine coupling, captured to a high-density surface of polyclonal anti-human Fc antibody, and immobilized via aldehyde coupling (CM4 chip only). The binding constants measured with Biacore were compared to solution-phase equilibrium and kinetic measurements using KinExA and to equilibrium measurements using a solution-phase Biacore methodology. The results of the binding constants from this study are listed in Table 5.3. For the Biacore solution-phase equilibrium method, a high-density surface of the mAb was amine-coupled to the Biacore CM5 chip while samples were prepared by serially diluting the mAb into a constant concentration of antigen and then allowing the titration to equilibrate. Each equilibrated sample was then injected in triplicate over the high-density surface of mAb under a low flow rate (i.e., mass transport conditions) and then the binding response level for any unbound antigen was recorded at an identical time point. Both K -controlled and antigen-controlled titra- D tion curves were acquired using the Biacore solution-phase method and therefore a dual curve analysis of the response levels as a function of antibody binding site concentration were globally fit to a 1:1 equilibrium model to estimate the K. Here D the high-density Biacore chip surface of mAb is analogous to the bead column in a solution-phase KinExA experiment. It is important to note that a monovalent reagent should be held constant in a Biacore solution-phase titration equilibrium experiment because then the only response detected by SPR is the mass from unbound monovalent protein. If the orientation of the experiment was reversed and the Biacore chip

Reproduced with permission) was instead used to detect the free binding sites of the bivalent antibody, the SPR response would be greater from a mAb singly bound with Ag compared to the response of the same mAb having both binding sites free, thus inflating the overall mass response at the surface. The results in Table 5.3 show that the different immobilization methods resulted in similar kinetic measurements for each Biacore chip. However, as the amount of CM-dextran decreased with each type of Biacore chip, the k measurements a increased by an average of tenfold while the k measurements remained relatively d consistent. Particularly interesting was the fact that the k measurements from a amine-coupled surfaces increased threefold as the negative charge of each biosensor chip decreased approximately threefold. With decreasing negative charge on the surface, the k values measured with Biacore began to increase more closely to the a k values measured with KinExA, even though the affinities measured with a C1 a chip were still approximately tenfold weaker than the standard KinExA results and threefold weaker than the solution-phase Biacore experiments. It appeared that the CM-dextran was greatly influencing the measured k with this Ag/mAb complex. a Additional KinExA experiments were also performed with soluble CM-dextran added to all the samples to attempt to mimic the environment in which the Ag/mAb complexation might take place at the Biacore chip surface. While the affinities measured in these additional KinExA experiments were only threefold less tight than the standard KinExA experiments, it turns out the estimated molar ratios of the dextran concentrations in CM5 and CM4 chips to the dextran concentration used in the KinExA experiments correlated reasonably well with the ratios of the K values D measured on CM5 and CM4 chips to the affinities measured with KinExA in the presence of CM-dextran. Again, it was clear that the CM-dextran was adversely

112 A. W. Drake et al. affecting the kinetics measured using Biacore for this Ag/mAb complex. It was surmised that either the positive charge resulting from an unusually high pI of 9.4 for the mAb and the negative charge of the CM-dextran layer interfered electrostatically with the binding of the antigen to the immobilized mAb, or the more acidic estimated pI range of 5.0–6.5 for the antigen slowed the k due to electrostatic repula sion with the CM-dextran, or there was a combination of the two artifacts (Drake et al. 2012). It should be noted that in no way does the study by Drake et al. (2012) generally invalidate SPR as a reliable biophysical tool to measure high-resolution antigen–antibody kinetics. Instead when measuring high-resolution kinetics, researchers should at least perform multiple experiments on varying types of biosensor chips to note any concerning trends in shifting kinetic values. Any high-resolution SPR measurements should also be validated with an orthogonal solution-phase method like KinExA. When statistically significant differences in binding measurements do arise between the two technologies, assuming the most advanced experimental methods have been utilized to generate binding data with the most optimal data processing protocols, the absence of any “gold standard” to which results can be compared presents the dilemma of having to decide which technology is providing the most accurate antibody–antigen binding information. While both Biacore and KinExA technologies are considered to be “label-free” methods, technically only KinExA can truly make this claim. Consider the fact that one of the binding partners in a typical Biacore experiment often must be coupled to a surface using covalent amine-coupling chemistry similar to external fluorophore protein labeling chemistries. With KinExA, no modifications are made to the binding partners. As emphasized earlier, antigen covalently bound to the bead resin in a KinExA flow cell is used only for detection of free antibody in solution and is not involved in the binding reaction being studied. In some rare cases, even if no chemical modifications are made to an antibody when captured to a pAb or mAb covalently coupled to a Biacore surface, the entropic reduction from capturing the antibody to the solution- like dextran surface could thermodynamically alter the affinity measurement. Also, as shown in the study by Drake et al. (2012), charge effects from the carboxyl groups on Biacore surfaces can perturb electrostatically kinetic measurements in cases where an antigen or ligand of a certain pI may be attracted to or repelled from the surface. It is also possible that the negatively charged CM-dextran layer bound to the highly positively charged mAb covalently immobilized on the surface, thereby leading to artifactual kinetic measurements (Drake et al. 2012). Therefore, from a “purist” perspective, one may conclude that because KinExA technology requires no protein modification under normal use and both purified binding partners interact in homogeneous solution, and because there is a greater potential for multiple sources of artifacts in Biacore technology, KinExA more than likely provides more accurate binding information for purified antibody–antigen complexes when discrepancies arise between the two methods. However, in most cases, the most likely source of significant disagreements between the two methods is an improper use of one or both instruments.

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 113 5.3.7 Other KinExA Uses The utility of the KinExA instrument has also been shown to extend beyond equilibrium and kinetic measurements of purified binding partners free in homogeneous solution. Sasaki et al. (2005) developed a KinExA screening method that can assess several hundred hybridomas for active antibody. In this method, supernatants are flowed through the antigen-coated bead resin and clones are deemed “positive” when a fluorescent signal is generated from the secondary anti-species fluorescently labeled polyclonal antibody. An iterative supernatant pooling method of several hundred clones proved to be more efficient using KinExA when compared to a traditional plate-based enzyme-linked immunosorbant assay (ELISA) screen. The higher sensitivity of the KinExA screen also found more “positives” and determined fewer “false positives” when the same samples were screened using ELISA (Sasaki et al. 2005). However, unlike a Biacore “low resolution” affinity ranking kinetic screen of antibodies out of supernatant, the information generated from the KinExA hybridoma screen is strictly qualitative. Additionally, Xie et al. (2005) and Rathanaswami et al. (2008) developed a cell-based KinExA protocol to measure the on-cell affinities of monoclonal antibodies to cell surface receptors. This method will be discussed in more detail in the next section. 5.3.8 High-Throughput Solution-Based Equilibrium Methods One of the rare drawbacks when using KinExA to measure antibody–antigen equilibrium dissociation constants is the method’s low throughput. This is especially true when measuring high affinity interactions where generating a signal could require sample volumes of tens of milliliters of protein at low picomolar concentrations, translating into multiple hours of instrument run time for a single interaction. Two alternative solution-based methods which allow for measuring multiple antigen–antibody affinities with single experiments at significantly reduced run times are briefly detailed here. First, Estep et al. (2013) developed a plate-based method to measure the affinities of several antigen–antibody titrations in solution by combining the concept of kinetic exclusion with the electrochemiluminescence (ECL) detection of a Meso Scale Discovery (MSD) instrument. Individual spots on an MSD-ECL plate for an MSD instrument contain electrodes which receive an electric pulse which illuminates any bound secondary protein markers having a chemiluminescent label whose signal is detected by a charge coupled device (CCD) camera (Marchese et al. 2009). Estep et al. (2013) equilibrated several antigen–antibody titrations where antibodies were serially diluted into constant concentrations of antigen. Each equilibrated sample was then incubated for a short time (150 s) over individual spots of an MSD- ECL plate coated with the antigen-binding antibody, followed immediately by multiple plate washes. Free antigen bound to the plate was then detected with a chemiluminescent probe by the MSD instrument. A quadratic 1:1 equilibrium model then estimated the affinities from plots of the percent free antigen as a

114 A. W. Drake et al. function of the titrated antibody concentrations. The affinities of over one hundred antibodies complexing with six different antigens measured with the MSD ranged from single-digit picomolar to single-digit nanomolar and differed by less than 2% from KinExA measurements. The authors claim the MSD method allows for assessing the K ’s of hundreds of antibodies per week. They also caution that sample D contact time with the MSD-ECL plates must be kept short to maintain kinetic exclusion and not perturb the equilibrium, or else risk K ’s with inflated magnitudes. The D sensitivity of ECL allows for short contact times that present a low capture level of unbound protein while still generating ample signal, which implies that extremely tight antigen–antibody interactions can be measured with the MSD-ECL method (Estep et al. 2013). Another high-throughput solution-phase technique for measuring antibody–antigen affinities was developed by Salimi-Moosavi et al. (2012) using a Gyrolab instrument. Gyrolab technology uses proprietary compact discs (CDs) containing microfluidics that direct samples through dozens of nanoliter-sized capture columns via the centrifugal force from spinning the CDs. Fluorescently tagged secondary antibodies quantitate the captured protein on the capture columns via laser-induced fluorescence (Eriksson et al. 2006). A Gyrolab is ideally suited for high-throughput antibody concentration determination; however, Salimi-Moosavi et al. (2012) optimized a method where the capture columns in the Gyrolab CDs quantitate the amount of free binding partner in equilibrated antibody–antigen solutions that pass over the columns. Specifically, several anti-digoxin antibodies at constant concentrations were first titrated with varying concentrations of digoxin and equilibrated outside of the Gyrolab in 96-well plates. The Gyrolab injected these equilibrated samples into the microfluidics of the CDs and then passed the samples over individual streptavidin columns pre-coupled with biotinylated digoxin by spinning the CDs. Fluorescent secondary antibodies generated the signal on the capture columns to represent the percent free antibody binding site concentrations as a function of the digoxin concentration, thus allowing for a K to be estimated for each titration. D The affinity values estimated with the Gyrolab ranged from low picomolar to high micromolar and were comparable to measurements performed using KinExA. The authors claim that the Gyrolab can assess more than 20 12-point binding affinity titrations in duplicate over approximately 4 h (Salimi-Moosavi et al. 2012). 5.4 Cell-Based Affinity Assays As shown in the previous two sections, Biacore and KinExA are ideally suited for measuring the equilibrium dissociation constant of monoclonal antibodies binding to purified antigen. However, some cell-surface antigens possess vital transmembrane domains which may prevent purification of the receptor; particularly G-protein coupled receptors (GPCRs). Even if the extracellular domain of a transmembrane antigen can be isolated, its purified conformation may differ from the native structure found on the cell surface and therefore sacrifice native functionality. Hence, cell-based affinity determination methods overcome transmembrane protein

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 115 purification limitations and provide the biophysical scientist the flexibility to be able to study monoclonal antibodies binding to cell surface antigen. 5.4.1 Cell-Based KinExA Affinity Measurements Xie et al. (2005) and Rathanaswami et al. (2008) developed a method to measure the functional affinities of antibodies binding to cell surface receptors using KinExA. In this method, an increasing concentration of cells expressing the antigen of interest is titrated into a constant concentration of antibody and allowed to equilibrate. The equilibrated solutions of antibody mixed with cells are then centrifuged to separate the cells from any unbound antibody in solution. The KinExA instrument then determines the percent free antibody in each solution as described in Sect. 5.3. Dual curve analysis of “K -controlled” and “antibody-controlled” titrations to a simple D 1:1 equilibrium binding model yields the K of the antibody binding to the cell sur- D face antigen. Because antigen is titrated via cells whose antigen expression levels normally cannot be definitively known, the “antigen unknown” fitting model (see Sect. 5.3.4) must be used (Xie et al. 2005; Rathanaswami et al. 2008). KinExA instrument limitations, however, can restrict the throughput of the number of anti-bodies that can be studied per experiment from a logistical viewpoint, although a recently available autosampler for KinExA experiments has improved sample capacity. A second limitation of KinExA technology for cell-based K measure- D ments is that hundreds of milliliters of cells at concentrations on the order of millions of cells per mL may be required to study multiple antibodies. On the other hand, KinExA does allow for a measurement of the k of a protein to a cell surface a using the “whole cell kinetics direct” method (Sapidyne Instruments Inc. 2015a, b). Here several samples of cells and the receptor-binding protein at identical concentrations are mixed together for various lengths of time until each of the mixed samples is centrifuged to remove the cell pellet. The amount of protein free in solution in each supernatant is probed by the KinExA and then fit to a 1:1 kinetic model as a function of the reaction time to estimate the k of the molecule binding to a cell a surface receptor (Sapidyne Instruments Inc. 2015a, b). When combined with a cell-based measure of the functional K, the dissociation rate constant can then be cal- D culated by multiplying K by k (Sect. 5.3.3). KinExA can provide both D a thermodynamic and kinetic binding constants of antibodies binding to cell-bound targets. Of course, the affinities measured with this methodology are functional K D because it is not known whether each mAb is bound to two antigens in an avid interaction on the cell surface, or whether the mAb is bound to only one antigen receptor on the surface. 5.4.2 FACS Affinity Measurement A more practical and higher throughput means to study monoclonal antibodies binding to antigen expressed on cell surfaces is with flow cytometry using

116 A. W. Drake et al. Fluorescence Activated Cell Sorting (FACS) instrumentation. A FACS instrument measures multiple physical and fluorescent characteristics of thousands of individual cells in a fluidic stream directed through an excitation laser (Ibrahim and van den Engh 2003). In a FACS affinity experiment, monoclonal antibody is titrated into a constant concentration of cells expressing the target antigen and allowing enough time for equilibration. The titration of antibody into cells is normally performed directly into a 96-well plate where the use of a multichannel pipette allows for a simultaneous preparation of multiple titrations. After equilibration and a brief washing step, a large excess (to avoid a potential Hook effect) of fluorescently labeled anti-species polyclonal antibody is added to each equilibrated well of the plate. The mean fluorescence intensity of the cells as a function of the titrated antibody concentration in each well is then recorded using a FACS instrument. Because FACS instrumentation can accommodate a 96-well plate format, multiple antibodies can be studied in a single experiment and the acquisition time per well is on the order of seconds to minutes owing to sample volumes of only hundreds of microliters. FACS technology has historically been used for characterizing cell surface interactions for several different systems. FACS has been used to rank the apparent on-cell binding strength of multiple phage-generated antibodies based on relative fluorescence intensities rather than their K ’s (Geuijen et al. 2005). FACS methods D have been used to measure the affinities of Concanavalin A binding to lymphocytes (Gordon 1995), of chimeric mouse monoclonal antibody (C2B8) binding to CD20 expressed on SB cells (Reff et al. 1994), of anti-common acute lymphoblastic leukemia antigen (CALLA) monoclonal antibodies binding to NALM-6 cells (Lebien et al. 1982), and of the programmed death-1 (PD-1) receptor binding to two PD-1 ligands (B7-H1, B7-DC) (Youngnak et al. 2003). 5.4.2.1 Linear and Nonlinear FACS Data Analysis In each of the examples above, nonlinear equilibrium FACS data underwent a linear transformation so that traditional Scatchard Plots could be used to estimate the equilibrium dissociation constants. It has been shown, however, that linearizing nonlinear isotherm data via Scatchard Plots or any other type of single or double reciprocal plot can lead to mathematically skewed or unequally compressed data, which can often lead to erroneously concluding the existence of a complex binding mechanism (Klotz 1997; Martin 1997). Continued use of Scatchard Analysis today appears to be borne more out of tradition when previous technological limitations made linear regression a more practical computational tool. However, the advent of readily available personal computers powerful enough to run numerous software packages that facilitate nonlinear regression has essentially made linear transformation of nonlinear FACS equilibrium binding data outdated and unnecessary (Klotz 1997; Martin 1997; Wilkinson 2004). Nonlinear fitting of binding isotherm data from flow cytometry measurements has given estimates for the equilibrium dissociation constants of an anti-B1 antibody binding to CD20 (Cardarelli et al. 2002), of tacrolimus binding to yeast cell surface-displayed anti-tacrolimus antibodies (Siegel et al. 2008), and of variable lymphocyte receptors (VLRs) binding to fluorescent ligand (Tasumi et al. 2009).

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 117 5.4.2.2 K -Controlled and Receptor-Controlled Data D While each of the aforementioned FACS studies demonstrate the utility of flow cytometry for measuring on-cell binding affinities, it’s also important to note that in each case, whether data was fit nonlinearly or with linear reciprocal plots, the assumption is made that the free ligand concentration in solution at equilibrium is equal to the total ligand concentration titrated into the cells. In fact, this assumption must also be made for linear reciprocal plots such as Scatchard Plots. This assumption is not always correct, however. As with KinExA or any other binding assay, careful consideration must be given to the conditions under which ligand, in this case mAb, is titrated into cells and how they affect the shape of the binding isotherm, and what information is contained therein. More specifically, it is important to remember the ratio of the cell surface receptor in molar concentration (calculated by converting the number of receptors into moles and dividing by the solution volume the cells are contained in; it is assumed the volume the cells occupy is negligible) to the K of the ligand–receptor interaction drives the shape of the titration D curve. For optimal conditions to estimate the K, titration conditions should gener- D ate a “K -controlled” curve, meaning the curve’s shape is sensitive to the K of the D D ligand–cell receptor interaction and not to the receptor concentration. K -controlled D conditions dictate that the cell receptor concentration should be at or below the K, D and usually no greater than threefold above the K. In fact, it can be shown that D assuming the free ligand concentration in each equilibrated solution equals the total ligand titration concentration actually holds under highly K -controlled conditions. D A highly K -controlled binding isotherm can normally be identified by its shape D having ample curvature, which is entirely characteristic of the K of the ligand– D receptor binding pair. As the ratio of the cell receptor concentration to the K D increases, the titration curve becomes more and more “receptor-controlled” where the shape of the curve is influenced more by the cell receptor concentration than the K of the interaction. A receptor-controlled binding isotherm is generated when the D cell receptor concentration is normally 10–20-fold or more above the K. Under D receptor-controlled conditions, ligand at initially low concentrations may stoichiometrically bind to receptor, resulting in an initial linear phase to the binding curve which contains no K information. A receptor-controlled curve contains only mini- D mal K information in the small region of curvature near the saturating plateau of D the titration curve. In the extreme receptor-controlled case, the titration curve will actually show no curvature but instead show an entirely linear approach to a sharply breaking plateau point indicative of the stoichiometry of the interaction. 5.4.2.3 A Four-Parameter Nonlinear FACS Fitting Model In a cell-based equilibrium titration experiment designed to measure the K of a D monoclonal antibody binding to a cell-surface receptor, the cell receptor concentration in molarity is normally unknown, making it difficult to be able to ascertain before the experiment whether the receptor concentration or the K will have a D greater influence on the binding curve. Drake and Klakamp (2007) have developed a new four-parameter nonlinear equation and methodology based on the traditional multiple, independent binding site (MIBS) equation which takes into account the

118 A. W. Drake et al. effects of the receptor concentration on the titration curve and thus fits cell-based binding data much more rigorously than previous methods. As in any other chemical titration method that uses fluorescence detection as a spectroscopic handle for bound ligand (L ), the four-parameter model directly relates the fluorescence (F) B from a fluorescently labeled secondary polyclonal antibody to the amount of primary monoclonal antibody bound to the cell surface and is shown in Eq. 5.8 below. (K +L +nM )- (K +L +nM )2 -4(nM L ) F =P× D T C D T C C T +B (5.8) 2 The derivation of Eq. 5.8 from the MIBS equation, which assumes all independent binding sites possess identical equilibrium dissociation constants, is detailed by Drake and Klakamp (2007). Equation 5.8 relates fluorescence (F) to the concentration of bound ligand in terms of two known quantities, the total ligand concentration (L) and the molar cell concentration (M ), and four unknown parameters, the T C equilibrium dissociation constant (K ), the number of receptors of interest per cell D (n), background signal (B), and a proportionality constant (P) that relates arbitrary fluorescence units to bound ligand concentration, where P can be represented by (F max − B)/nM c where F max is the plateau signal of saturation in the titration curve and is an unknown floating parameter. When fitting a nonlinear binding titration curve, an estimate for K is obtained when L and M are hard-coded into the fitting D T C model while K, n, P (or F ), and B are floated freely in the nonlinear analysis. M D max C is calculated based on the number of cells and the final volume of each well in the titration. Each titration experiment normally includes a “blank” well that contains only cells, with L = 0, which are exposed to the same concentration of fluorescent T polyclonal labeling antibody as those wells containing the same number of cells titrated with serially diluted antibody. Any signal detected at L = 0 is considered T background signal, but simply subtracting this “blank” signal from all other data points compounds the errors inherently associated with the signals of the blank and non-blank wells (Miller and Miller 1988). Hence rather than a direct subtraction of background, the contribution from background to the total fluorescence signal in Eq. 5.8 is fit for with the unknown parameter B. It should be noted that the meaning of B changes in Eq. 5.8 if the measured fluorescence arises from directly labeled cell-bound ligand instead of bound unmodified primary ligand labeled with a secondary fluorescent marker. For a titration of unlabeled ligand into cells with subsequent fluorescent labeling by a pAb, B represents any instrument noise and nonspecific binding of the fluorescently labeled pAb to the cells and should be the same at all points since a large excess of labeling pAb is used in the procedure; therefore B should be a constant at each point in the titration if a significant amount of nonspecific binding (NSB) is not occurring with the unlabeled ligand. In contrast, with directly labeled ligand, the parameter B only describes the instrument noise, and background fluorescence may vary in direct relation with the labeled ligand concentration at each data point if significant NSB is present. In this case it is recommended that directly labeled ligand be titrated into a non-expressing parental cell line and an antigen-expressing cell line at the same M and identical ligand C

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 119 concentrations, so any NSB seen in the control titration can be properly subtracted from the titration of interest. This subtraction of control data from the titration of interest is, of course, only necessary if NSB influences adversely the titration curve describing the interaction of interest. 5.4.2.4 A Traditional Three-Parameter Model Compared to the Four- Parameter Model Because an extremely K -controlled titration contains very little information to esti- D mate any parameters related to the receptor concentration, Eq. 5.8 can be further simplified under K -controlled conditions by combining n, M, and P resulting in a D C proportionality constant P′ in a three-parameter fitting model shown below in Eq. 5.9: L F =P¢× T +B (5.9) K +L D T where free ligand (L F ) is known and assumed to equal L T. P′, B, and K D are the three unknown fitting parameters as described for Eq. 5.9. P′ can also be expressed as (F -B). Again, this simplification can only be done under extremely K -controlled max D conditions where it is correct to assume free ligand concentration equals total ligand concentration (Drake and Klakamp 2007). It should also be pointed out that the three-parameter equation originally published by Drake and Klakamp (2007) is not correct. However, the corrected Eq. 5.9 above yields results that are insignificantly different from those generated with the previously published erroneous three-parameter equation (Drake and Klakamp 2007). Because it is more difficult often to gauge the molar concentration of cell surface receptors, it is normally unknown if a cell-based titration experiment is either K -controlled or receptor-controlled, or is D influenced partially by both. Even after a visual inspection of the resulting binding curve, the shape of the curve will rarely show obvious characteristics of extreme examples of either case and will more than likely look like a curve that is a hybrid of K - and receptor-controlled conditions. Therefore, any cell-based titration exper- D iment is described more rigorously and accurately with Eq. 5.8 because the model not only takes into account the K influence on the shape of the binding curve, but D also the influence of the cell receptor concentration. It is, in fact, more reasonable to assume that most titrations contain both K - and receptor-controlled influences, D which makes an even stronger case that the four-parameter model of Eq. 5.8 is the correct nonlinear fitting model for cell-based equilibrium titration data. Actually, Eq. 5.8 can be used for any titration where a spectroscopic handle or other signal exists that is directly proportional to bound ligand. In other words, this equation holds not only for FACS titrations. Drake and Klakamp (2007) theoretically compared the effectiveness of the four-parameter model (Eq. 5.8) to a less rigorous three-parameter model which only assumed a K -controlled titration data set. In this theoretical example, a simple 1:1 D equilibrium expression (not Eq. 5.8) was used to generate simulated data sets by calculating L (total bound [mAb]) as a function of L (total [mAb]) with identical B T

120 A. W. Drake et al. antibody titrations into three data sets with equal numbers of cells but having three different levels of receptors/cell, or n values. The K of the antibody–receptor inter- D action was held constant for all three titrations and each value for L was converted B into an arbitrary fluorescent signal by choosing an arbitrary proportionality constant. By varying n, the molar concentration of receptors (R ) is varied, thus chang- T ing the ratio of the receptor concentration to the K in each case. The simulated data D sets were then fit with both the four-parameter model and the three-parameter model and the parameters returned with each model were compared to the theoretical parameters used to generate the data. To calculate the simulated data sets, the K D was assumed to be 50 pM, an arbitrary P value was chosen for each data set to convert L into an arbitrary fluorescence signal, and the molar cell concentration was B held constant at 1.107 fM (each plate well containing 200,000 cells in a 300 μL volume of buffer). B was also chosen arbitrarily in each case. The molecular mAb concentration range used for each simulated titration was 5 nM–5 pM, reflecting an ideal concentration titration range spanning 100-fold above the K and 100-fold D below the K. The first data set (Case 1) assumed n = 1.000 × 103 receptors per cell D which results in R = 1.107 pM. The second simulated data set (Case 2) assumed T n = 1.000 × 105 receptors/cell which results in R = 110.7 pM. The third data set T (Case 3) assumed n = 1.000 × 106 receptors per cell giving R = 1.107 nM. Case 1 T was a K -controlled simulation because R is almost 45-fold below the K. Case 3 D T D was a receptor-controlled data set where R is approximately 22-fold above the K. T D Because R was only twofold above the K, the titration in Case 2 was mostly sensi- T D tive to the changes in the K, but was also influenced somewhat by R. The nonlinear D T fits of each simulated data set with the four-parameter model (Eq. 5.8) and three-parameter model are shown in Fig. 5.11 and the resulting binding parameters returned from each fit are listed in Table 5.4 (Drake and Klakamp 2007). For Case 1, both the four-parameter and three-parameter models were fit to the data exceptionally well (Fig. 5.11a, b) and the fitted parameter values were almost identical to the actual parameters used to generate the data (Table 5.4). This was not unexpected since Case 1 was highly K -controlled and insensitive to n so either D model was able to accurately describe the titration curve. With R being only slightly T above K in Case 2 where the titration curve is sensitive to both K and n informa- D D tion, the four-parameter model again fit the data well (Fig. 5.11c) and returned parameter values almost identical to the theoretical values (Table 5.4) while the parameters returned with the three-parameter model began to deviate from their actual values (Table 5.4). In particular, the three-parameter model estimated a K D (105 pM) over twofold greater than the actual K (50 pM). Note also how the three- D parameter model slightly missed several theoretical data points shown for Case 2 in Fig. 5.11d. As expected the three-parameter model was unable to achieve a satisfactory fit to the highly receptor-controlled data set in Case 3 (Fig. 5.11f), hence the estimated binding parameters from the poor fit differed significantly from their actual values (Table 5.4). The best “fit” with the three-parameter model returned a K value (787.1 pM) almost 16-fold greater than the actual 50 pM affinity. D Alternatively, the four-parameter model fit the data well in Case 3 (Fig. 5.11e) and was able to correctly estimate all theoretical parameters (Table 5.4). Figure 5.11

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 123 shows the noticeable contrast in the shapes of nonlinear binding curves generated under K -controlled (Case 1) and receptor-controlled (Case 3) conditions. The bind- D ing isotherm in Case 1 shows a K -controlled titration curve maintains curvature to D saturation while the highly receptor-controlled data in Case 3 show a stoichiometric linear onset followed by only a small amount of curvature. These simulations demonstrated how the more rigorous four-parameter model can always correctly describe a cell-based titration curve whether the titration is K -controlled or D receptor-c ontrolled. The simulations also illustrate how the use of traditional fitting models which always assume a K -controlled titration and fail to consider the influ- D ence of receptor concentration can lead to erroneous cell-based affinity measurements. It should be emphasized that the values listed in Table 5.4 are the results of fitting noise- and error-free simulated data sets. Real binding data will most certainly contain noise and Eq. 5.8 will most likely not return a meaningful and accurate value for n when fitting a highly K -controlled data set. Conversely Eq. 5.8 D should not theoretically return a meaningful and accurate value for K when fitting D an extremely receptor-controlled data set. As discussed with KinExA data analysis, a simultaneous fit of both a K -controlled curve and a receptor-controlled curve D using Eq. 5.8 would be the most ideal approach to more accurately estimate K and D n. Here P and B would be fit locally to each curve while the values of K and n D would be calculated from a global fit of two or more titration curves run under different controlling conditions (Drake and Klakamp 2007). Unfortunately, in cases where the K is extremely tight and the cells have extremely high receptor expres- D sion levels, an unfeasibly low number of cells may be required to reduce the receptor concentration below the K of the interaction for a K -controlled experiment. D D Hence, it is always best whenever possible to use cell lines that have lower receptor expression levels. 5.4.2.5 Experimental Methods for FACS K Measurements D The four-parameter model in Eq. 5.8 will describe any titration experiment where a spectroscopic or radioactive signal is proportional to L as a function of L, which B T makes its application to FACS-based data an ideal method for measuring the affinities of antibodies binding to receptors expressed on a cell surface. The introduction of integrated 96-well plate-based readers in FACS instruments has made it possible to prepare and study multiple antibody titrations in a single experiment. The ideal concentration range in any titration is typically from 100-fold above the K to 100- D fold below the K. When serially diluting mAb into cells, at least one well should D serve as a “blank” well (no antibody added to cells) to help anchor the background parameter B in Eq. 5.8 during the nonlinear fit of the data. Ideally, titrations should be performed in duplicate or triplicate if possible. It may be more practical to perform single titrations when studying five or more antibodies in a single experiment. It is also imperative that the same number of cells is added to each well. Equilibration of the mAb-cells normally occurs while the 96-well plate (or plates) is gently shaken at 4 °C to minimize antibody internalization and to prolong the viability of the live cells. The brief post-equilibration and post-labeling washing steps in the FACS affinity protocol have caused some concern in the literature that a perturbation of

124 A. W. Drake et al. the equilibrium from dissociated antibody will affect the K measurement D (Rathanaswami et al. 2008). Kinetic simulations, however, show that any postwashing dissociation of mAb results in such a low free [mAb] that effects from any antibody rebinding are minimal (on the order of 5–10%). Moreover, because the k d is the same at all titration points since the same complex is being measured at each point, the shape of the titration curve is not affected by the mAb dissociation during the washing steps. It is important to realize that absolute bound concentrations of mAb are not being measured, but rather a signal proportional to the bound mAb is being measured, so as long as all the points relate to each other proportionally the curve describes correctly the binding reaction. Even with that said, the washing procedures should still be performed as quickly as possible to obtain the highest signal intensities for each data point. When recording the mean fluorescence intensity (MFI) using FACS instrumentation, at least 5000–10,000 “events” (fluorescence on a live cell) should be measured for each data point. GraphPad Prism software (GraphPad Software, Inc., La Jolla, CA) provides users with ample modeling flexibility and is highly recommended for the nonlinear analysis. Obviously, other software packages that allow the programming and fitting of Eq. 5.8 may also be used. 5.4.2.6 Interpretation of Cell-Based K Measurements D One of the most significant limitations of any cell-based monoclonal antibody affinity method is the interpretation of the measured K. Because the KinExA cell- D based technique estimates on-cell affinity indirectly via free antibody in solution as a function of the cell antigen (“antigen unknown”) concentration, it cannot differentiate between antibodies bound bivalently or univalently to the cell surface. In a standard solution-phase KinExA experiment, antibodies that have one or both binding sites free can bind to the detection resin, albeit the former with half the probability of the latter, where the resulting signal is directly related to the free antibody binding site concentration. In a KinExA cell-based experiment antibodies univalently bound to cells are centrifuged away from the free antibody concentration; hence the cell-based KinExA method can only measure the free antibody molecular concentration. Because of this, the only possible interpretation of the measured K is that it represents the stoichiometric or macroscopic K of the anti- D D body binding to the cell surface. In contrast to the KinExA method, the FACS affinity method detects antibody directly bound to the cell surface, but it is still impossible to discern whether the K measured is the site-binding or microscopic D affinity or a stoichiometric or macroscopic K. In the former case, it is more appro- D priate to use the antibody binding site concentration (twice the molecular concentration) for L when performing nonlinear analysis of binding data with Eq. 5.8 T while the whole molecular antibody concentration should be used in the latter situation. Extensive theoretical and experimental consideration has been given to the binding nature of multivalent ligand to cell surface receptors (Reynolds 1979; Dower et al. 1984; Kaufman and Jain 1992; Ong and Mattes 1993; Vanden Broek and Thompson 1996; Hlavacek et al. 1999; Yang et al. 2002; Jung et al. 2005, 2008; Mack et al. 2008, 2011, 2012; Jung et al. 2009). It may be helpful to consider that

5 Biophysical Considerations for Development of Antibody-Based Therapeutics 125 exact theoretical modeling in solution (not on a cell surface) has shown that in a titration where the concentration of the bivalent ligand is in significant excess over the univalent receptor that most of the bivalent ligand has only one bound receptor (Mack et al. 2008). Considering that in a FACS titration as outlined above, the bivalent mAb is in excess at almost all the titration points except for the first one or two points, it may be reasonable to assume the mAb is binding predominately in a monovalent manner although realizing that the theoretical treatment above relates to univalent receptor and bivalent ligand binding in solution rather than on a cell surface where receptors may be constrained spatially near monovalently bound mAb where they could more easily form a bivalent complex with mAb. In addition, Mack et al. (2012) reviewed the thermodynamic parameters of several binding studies of bivalent antibodies in the literature. Among these studies, it was noted the free energy change of the binding of the second arm of the antibody to a second surface receptor (referred to as the intramolecular free energy change, ΔG intra ) was always less than the free energy change of a bivalent antibody binding to a single receptor (referred to as the inter molecular free energy change, ΔG inter ). The authors, however, cautioned that analyses of more bivalent binding studies were needed before definitively concluding that ΔG intra will always be less than ΔG inter (Mack et al. 2012). Even though the above arguments for monovalent binding of mAb to the cell surface antigen can be made logically, the possibility of antibody binding bivalently to the cell surface at least partially cannot be discounted until further studies with FACS-based titrations are performed to discern how mAbs bind to antigens on cell surfaces. This monovalent or bivalent binding tendency may vary for every combination of mAb and cell- based receptor due to conformational constraints and flexibilities of the molecules, and the spatial arrangement and density of receptor on the cell surface. Normally few conclusions can be drawn by comparing Biacore or KinExA site-binding K measurements of purified antigen and antibody to cell-based measure- D ments primarily for two reasons: (1) the possibility of antibody binding avidly (bivalently) to a cell surface and (2) there is always the question as to whether purified antigen retains the same conformation and hence binding properties as native protein on a cell membrane. Additionally, most Biacore and KinExA experiments are performed at room temperature (KinExA is currently not equipped with temperature control) while FACS affinity titrations are usually equilibrated at 4 °C to avoid mAb internalization and to ensure cell viability. Measuring the affinity of a Fab fragment in a cell-based experiment could give insight to the intrinsic site-binding constant of the parent bivalent antibody, but there also may be some uncertainty as to whether even the Fab fragment retains all native binding properties of the full antibody. There is always the argument that it is irrelevant whether a cell-based experiment yields a therapeutic antibody’s intrinsic single site affinity or an avidity-influenced affinity because the K most likely represents the more biologi- D cally relevant “functional avidity” of the antibody binding to a cell surface. But this argument quickly breaks down if the antigen expression density of the cells used in an on-cell affinity experiment does not exactly match the in vivo expression levels of antigen in the models used in animal or human preclinical and clinical studies in

126 A. W. Drake et al. the latter stages of drug development. Even with cells having a perceived low antigen expression level, lipid rafts could still form concentrated microdomains of antigen on the cell surface and promote bivalent binding of antibody. Because of the numerous limitations inherent to the interpretation of cell-based antibody affinity data, cell-based measurements are by no means meant as a farreaching substitution for other biophysical methods such as Biacore and KinExA methods which are ideal for studying purified antigen binding to antibody either immobilized to a solution-like biosensor surface or in homogeneous solution, respectively (although, it should be noted, recent advances have been made in solubilizing stable GPCRs for Biacore studies) (Rich et al. 2009; Congreve et al. 2011; Bocquet et al. 2015). Instead, cell-based antibody affinity experiments using FACS or KinExA should only be performed when purified receptor is either unobtainable or known to lose native structure and/or function. 5.5 Concluding Remarks The surface-based (SPR), solution-based (KinExA), and cell-based (FACS, KinExA) technologies that can provide antibody–antigen affinity measurements are essentially designed for ease of use for investigators spanning several disciplines. Ease of use, of course, doesn’t always parallel the proper use of the instrumentation. Binding estimates measured may be meaningless if data were acquired incorrectly. This chapter was designed not only to outline the proper functionality of these techniques, but also to emphasize how experimental design, data processing, and data interpretation are essential for acquiring reliable kinetic and thermodynamic binding characteristics for any therapeutic monoclonal antibody discovery program. Experimental design for any method described here must stem strictly from chemical binding principles and adhere to the demands each instrument requires for reproducible and accurate data acquisition. Optimal data processing is necessary so that an observed signal truly represents an antibody– antigen binding event in solution. Theoretical fitting models of antibody–antigen binding data must make sense within the confines of bimolecular binding phenomena in the absence of additional structural information of the complexed binding partners. In addition, experiments should be designed to obtain the intrinsic site-binding equilibrium dissociation constants of antibody–antigen complexes, which is especially challenging for cell- based affinity assays. Additionally, because there are inherently fewer sources of artifacts when purified protein binding partners interact freely in homogeneous solution, KinExA or other solution-phase technologies should always complement Biacore platforms and should yield the more reliable binding measurements when discrepancies with Biacore do occur. In summary, solid-phase (SPR), solution- phase (KinExA), and cell-based (FACS, KinExA) biophysical methods provide rigorous and reliable kinetic and affinity data to aid in the progression of lead therapeutic antibody candidates into clinical development stages.
