Chapter 6: Novel Technologies for Generation of Bispecific Constructs Laurence Fayadat-Dilman

134 L. Fayadat-Dilman anti-CD19 × anti-CD3) are approved for therapeutic application in oncology. Both antibodies are unique in engaging the two targets in a trans configuration (i.e., expressed on different cells), such that engagement of both targets is required for the ensuing biological effect(s). The mode of action of both antibodies includes engaging cytotoxic effector T cells, ultimately mediating T-cell redirected cell lysis of the target cells. The slow progress in advancing bispecific modalities into the clinical arena has been mainly due to challenges associated with generating bispecific molecules in sufficient quality and quantity. However, due to advances in rapidly evolving technologies allowing for novel engineering, production, and development of recombinant protein scaffolds, development of novel bispecific modalities have witnessed an exponential growth. Today, a large number of unique bispecific formats suitable for the development of therapeutic proteins are available (Spiess et al. 2015; Brinkmann and Kontermann 2017). Combination therapies, where two or more biologics are combined as single agents, are desired in order to achieve the “synergistic” effects due to engagement of two or more pathways simultaneously. Using this approach, a flexible spatial configuration is possible as each construct can engage the intended target with conformational freedom. However, when “multi-specific” modalities are used, the ensuing target engagement may provide additional advantages due to lack of spatial freedom with combined single-agent therapies (discussed in Chap. 2). Hence, design of bispecific therapeutics should require careful attention with respect to the selection of target (expressed as cis- “same cell” or trans- “different cells”) and construct properties. In this chapter the current landscape for bi- and multi-specific modalities from the design, production, and develop ability aspects are discussed. 6.2 Bispecific Formats: Engineering and Develop ability Engineered proteins, such as bi- or multi-specific antibodies, capable of binding two or more antigens, can be generated using several methodologies: (a) cell fusion, (b) chemical conjugation, and (c) recombinant DNA techniques. Additionally, bispecific antibodies (bsAbs) can be divided into two major classes based on their format and composition: bearing an Fc domain or lacking an Fc domain. IgG-like bispecific molecules can recruit secondary immune functions via Fc-mediated effector functions, such as antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC), antibody-d ependent cellular phagocytosis (ADCP), and complement fixation (discussed in Chap. 2). In addition, antibodies that contain an Fc domain bind the neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) that is responsible for protection of the antibody and the long half-life in circulation. For two decades, a major obstacle in the development of bispecific antibodies has been the difficulty in producing the material in sufficient quality and quantity. The decreased stability of bispecific antibodies has often been the source of problems for downstream processes and posed develop ability challenges preventing the progression of these molecules into the later development stages (Carter and

6 Novel Technologies for Generation of Bispecific Constructs 135 Correctly assembled BsAb (12.5%) Possible mispaired antibody species

Fig. 6.1 Complexities and heterogeneity in the final product following co-transfection of HC (heavy chain) and LC (light chain) in the same cell due to random association of antibody species Ridgway 1995). Over the last 10 years, significant efforts have been focused on devising novel and improved engineering strategies to reduce the underlying complexities in generation of small- (research) and large-scale bispecific modalities. Despite considerable technological advances, generation of bispecific antibodies has not been easy and has faced considerable challenges. In many instances and depending on the construct sequence, additional process optimization steps have been required. Furthermore, development of novel analytical methods for proper characterization of bispecific modalities has been necessary. In theory, for a bispecific antibody that is composed of two heavy chains (HC) and two light chains (LC), the random association of four different chains can result in ten unique combinations (Fig. 6.1). This heterogeneity in the final product necessitates the development of rigorous analytical and chromatographic methods to identify and separate the bispecific antibody species with the desired purity and specificity. 6.2.1 Antibody-Like Format 6.2.1.1 Antibodies Derived from Quadroma Technology An older approach for generation of bispecific antibodies relied on expression of half-antibodies with subsequent cross-linking (Cook and Wood 1994) or employing quadroma technology (Milstein and Cuello 1984). This technology is based on the

136 L. Fayadat-Dilman somatic fusion of two different hybridoma cell lines expressing two murine monoclonal antibodies with the desired specificities of the bispecific antibody. Because of the random pairing of the heavy and light chains of the two different antibodies within the resulting hybrid-hybridoma (or quadroma) cell line, up to ten different heavy chain and light chain pairings could be generated (Fig. 6.1). Due to the presence of mismatched by-products, which significantly reduced production yields, sophisticated purification procedures are required (Morrison 2007). Trion (http:// www.trionresearch.com; Triomab®) used this approach to combine one-half of two full-length antibodies, i.e., a tumor-specific mouse antibody and a T-cell-specific rat antibody, into one molecule to produce the bispecific catumaxomab (Removab®) antibody that was approved in Europe for malignant ascites, a condition occurring in patients with metastasizing cancer (Shen and Zhu 2008). The combination of two different antibody species (rat IgG2b and mouse IgG2a) with different affinities to protein A enabled Trion to develop a purification method and overcome the production challenges encountered in generation of other bispecific antibodies and establish a commercially viable process for Removab®. 6.2.1.2 Asymmetric Heterodimeric IgG-Like Bispecifics The engineered heterodimeric Fc-format is an attractive scaffold for the design of bispecific antibodies because this format maintains the structural and many other favorable properties of monospecific antibodies. The heterodimeric Fc-format is also expected to exhibit favorable drug development attributes of monoclonal antibodies, including high expression levels and standardized CMC (chemistry, manufacturing, and control) development. However, development of molecules based on this approach has faced challenges due to suboptimal heterodimer (correctly assembled bispecific antibody) content after protein A chromatography and the presence of potential homodimer and half-antibody contaminants resulting from instability of the final molecule relative to the native Fc. 6.2.1.3 C 3 Mutations for Efficient Heavy Chain Pairing H Homodimerization of the two heavy chains in a monoclonal antibody is promoted by high-affinity interactions (K ~10 pM) between the C 3 domains in the Fc region. D H Additional interactions in the hinge region are involved in covalent association between the two heavy chains with a patch of relevant residues (T366, L368, F405, Y407, and K409), which are crucial for C 3–C 3 interactions (Dall’Acqua et al. H H 1998). The “knobs-into-holes” technology (Atwell et al. 1997; Ridgway et al. 1996) employs this approach and is a widely applied technique for the design and generation of bispecific modalities from monovalent antibodies. This technology platform involves engineering C 3 domains to create either a “knob” or a “hole” in each H heavy chain to promote heterodimerization. More specifically, sterically complementary C 3 domains create a “knob” (i.e., T366W) with bulky residues such as H tryptophan and tyrosine, while smaller amino acids (i.e., threonine, alanine) will form a “hole” (T366S, L368A, and Y407V), promoting the assembly of the two heavy chains. While these mutations are designed to force heterodimer and disfavor homo-dimerization, it is not uncommon to encounter an undesirable amount of homodimers or half-antibodies.

Solutions that involved the use of opposite electrostatic charges, i.e., two positively charged lysine residues in one chain (D399K, E356K) and two negatively charged residues (aspartate) in the other chain (K409D, K392D), were engineered recently (Gunasekaran et al. 2010). Other investigators have developed “quality-by-design” computational and structure-guided approaches to identify mutations in the C 3 domains (a combination of steric inter- H actions, charge-driven interactions, and hydrophobic interactions), which lead to high heterodimeric specificity with Fc-like biophysical properties that translate into a robust upstream stable cell line selection process (Von Kreudenstein et al. 2013). In another approach (Moore et al. 2011), a novel bispecific format, mAb-Fv, utilizes mutations in the C 3 domain (S364H, F405A/ Y349T, T394F or S364H/T394F/ H Y349T/F405A) of the Fc region with enhanced heterodimerization potential to enable monovalent co-engagement of two unique target antigens in a full-length antibody context (Table 6.1). Additionally, the knowledge gained from the naturally occurring process of bispecific formation of IgG4 in vivo has been employed in generation of bispecific antibodies. This natural process, known as the “Fab-arm exchange” phenomenon, involves the core hinge residue S228 and the C 3 residue H R409. The transfer of these mutations to an IgG1 isotype has been recently used for generation of bispecific antibodies after reduction and oxidation. Two solutions were developed to create bispecific antibodies that exploited the destabilized hinge

138 L. Fayadat-Dilman region or the C 3–C 3 dimer interface of IgG4 (Labrijn et al. 2013; Strop et al. H H 2012). Table 6.1 summarizes selected strategies and mutations to force heavy chain pairing. 6.2.1.4 Solving HC/LC Pairing Due to the manner in which full-length bispecific antibodies assemble during production, bispecific antibody generation was initially limited to pairing antibodies that share a common light chain. Subsequently, solutions were developed to generate bispecific antibodies from monoclonal sequences with two different heavy chains and two different light chains. Below is a summary of various approaches used for pairing of HC/LC: (a) CrossMAb: Roche invented the CrossMAb technology to produce bispecific antibodies. This technology relies on modifying one antibody’s light and heavy chains by swapping relevant domains required for their interaction, so that they could only pair with each other and not with the light and heavy chains from a second antibody (Klein et al. 2016). Using this approach, three possible bispecific CrossMAb variants against angiopoietin-2 (Ang-2) and VEGFA were generated with the goal of blocking these two antigen targets (Kienast et al. 2013; Kloepper et al. 2016). (b) HC/LC Mutations: Bispecific antibodies can be engineered to force the correct HC and LC pairing during production (Lewis et al. 2014; Liu et al. 2015). The mutations are engineered in the C 1-C kappa and C 1-C lambda interface. H H Electrostatic and hydrophobic steering mechanisms are used to engineer antibody LC-HC residues in the interface in such a way that each LC strongly favors its cognate HC when two different HCs and two different LCs are co-expressed in the same cell. Using this approach, the resulting bispecific antibody is shown to be functional, with binding to two different antigens with comparable selectivity and monovalent affinity to each target while maintaining an acceptable develop ability profile. While progress in engineering the HC/LC interface has been significant in the last 3–4 years and light chain pairing has become less of an issue, this methodology still poses additional analytical challenges for detection and quantification of the final bispecific antibody with mismatched HC/LC (i.e., LC paired with the wrong HC). (c) KLL-Body Asymmetric Technology: The Novimmune KL-body asymmetric technology platform employs a full-length IgG format, a common HC, and two different LCs (kappa and lambda, Fischer et al. 2015). Specificity is driven by the light chain. Purification involves a multistep purification scheme involving protein A, HiTrap MAbSelect SuRe, KappaSelect, and Lambda Fab Select from GE Healthcare (http://www.gelifesciences.com) to efficiently eliminate unwanted contaminating species. (d) Bispecific Antibody Production by In Vitro Assembly of IgG Heterodimers: Labrijn et al. (2013) developed controlled Fab-arm exchange, which is a straightforward method to generate bispecific human IgG1 antibodies. Two antibodies of interest are expressed with single matching point mutations in the C 3 domain (F405L/K409R) and mixed under permissive redox conditions H

6 Novel Technologies for Generation of Bispecific Constructs 139 in vitro to enable recombination of half-molecules. Bispecific antibodies with regular full-length antibody architecture are efficiently generated in a process which can be readily scaled-up. Other investigators combined the knob-in-holes technology with the separate expression of half-antibodies. The half-antibodies with either the knob or hole mutation were expressed with their corresponding light chains in two different E. coli cultures (Jackman et al. 2010). The half-antibodies were subsequently isolated from E. coli periplasm, and the bispecific antibody was formed via the two half-antibodies by in vitro assembly. This approach allows for the correct light chain association during expression of the half-antibody without the need for other approaches to correctly pair the appropriate LC to its correct HC. One obvious restriction of this approach is the subsequent lack of glycosylation due to expression in E. coli, so this approach cannot be applied in a fully generic manner, e.g., to IgG1 antibodies, when effector functions may be desired. Similarly, Strop et al. (2012) have combined mutations in the hinge region as well as in the C 3 domain (K409R and L368E) of human antibodies with IgG1 H and IgG2 backbones to form the desired full-length bispecific antibodies after reduction and oxidation under appropriate redox conditions. Specifically, these investigators expressed two different antibodies, directed against CD20 and CD3. The CD3 arm contains L368E mutation in the CH3 region as well as the hinge mutations D221E and P228E for IgG1 or C223E, E225E, and P228E for IgG2 (EEE), and the CD20 arm contains K409R mutation in the CH3 region and the hinge mutations D221R and P228R for IgG1 or C223R, E225R, and P228R for IgG2 (RRR). Purified antibodies carrying these mutations were mixed together under appropriate redox conditions, resulting in the formation of an anti-CD3/CD20 bispecific antibody. Basic physiochemical characterization of these molecules demonstrated high stability similar to monospecific IgG molecules (Strop et al. 2012). (e) Bispecific Heterodimeric IgG Antibodies Without Enforced Heavy Chain Heterodimerization: Scientists at Regeneron (Smith et al. 2015) identified residues derived from IgG3, which are known to lack binding to protein A, and inserted these into the IgG1 C 3 domains of one antibody to abrogate protein A H interactions. This approach allowed for separation of heterodimeric bispecific antibodies from their homodimeric contaminants in a simple purification method. An additional efficient way to avoid the HC/LC pairing problem without further engineering was to select two antibodies that utilize a common LC or to create hybrid bispecific antibodies where one arm was a Fab and the other arm a domain scaffold (scFv, single-domain antibody, or single protein domain scaffold), allowing only LC in the bispecific construct to associate with its native HC. 6.2.1.5 Appended IgGs Traditional monoclonal antibodies can be appended at either their N- or C-termini of both HC and LC with antigen-binding domains such as unpaired VH-VL, scFv, VHH, or other engineered protein domains (LaFleur et al. 2013). One type of appended IgG includes a symmetric IgG molecule with a C-terminal fusion of a

140 L. Fayadat-Dilman scFv to a full-length IgG (IgG-scFv). With this approach, an IgG-shaped trivalent molecule carries a third binding entity and one disulfide-stabilized Fv (dsFvs) without a linker between VH and VL that is tethered via connector peptides at the C terminus of the C 3 domains of the IgG. This approach was employed recently H (Metz et al. 2012) for generation of anti-BAFF/IL17 and anti-Met/EGFR constructs currently in Phase 1 clinical development. In addition to antibodies, appended IgG formats also can include scFv C-terminal fusions to dual-variable-domain immunoglobulin (DVD-Ig™) and Fabs-in-tandem immunoglobulin (FIT-Ig™) bispecific molecules. Some bispecific formats containing scFv have suffered historically from poor physiochemical properties such as low expression levels, a higher tendency for aggregation, and lower thermostability compared to their conventional antibody counterparts. Advances in stability engineering of scFvs using a combination of sequence-based statistical analyses and structure-based methods have made it possible to engineer stable scFvs to serve as bispecific antibodies (Miller et al. 2010). In the DVD-Ig format, each arm of the molecule contains two variable domains (VDs). The VDs within an arm are linked in tandem and can possess different binding specificities. The DVD-Ig format allows for bivalent binding to two targets. Several DVD-Igs are currently progressing in the clinic. ABT-981 binds IL-1a and IL-1b and is currently in Phase II for the treatment of osteoarthritis. ABT-122, raised against TNF and IL17A, is currently in Phase II for the treatment of rheumatoid arthritis (DiGiammarino et al. 2012). When combined with C 3 heterodimeric mutations and the CrossMAb, the H DVD-Ig format can create tetra-specificity with monovalent binding to four targets; the four-in-one antibodies were demonstrated to have superior inhibitory activity against EGFR, HER2, HER3, and VEGF through disruption of HER/MET cross talk. Interestingly, four-in-one antibodies were more active than the bispecific antibody variations in all in vitro and in vivo models (Hu et al. 2015). 6.2.1.6 Symmetric Bispecific Antibodies An additional approach for generation of symmetric bispecific antibodies is the Modular Antibody Technology™ platform which introduces a novel antigen-binding site into the constant (Fc) region to create a so-called Fcab™ (Fc domain with antigen binding). The resulting Fcab is then used as a building block for other drug formats. More specifically, an Fcab can be easily combined with the variable region (Fab) of an existing antibody to generate a full-length bispecific antibody or mAb2. The overall structural integrity of the Ig-fold is retained, and the concept bears similarity to the CDRs found in the IgG variable region. Each C 3 domain can H bind one antigen, thereby yielding bivalency (Leung et al. 2015). 6.3 Protein and Antibody Fragments Significant efforts have been invested in the generation and optimization of new small scaffolds (antibody-derived, such as BiTE®, DART®) (Rader 2011) or nonantibody-d erived constructs as potential therapeutics (Vazquez-Lombardi et al.

6 Novel Technologies for Generation of Bispecific Constructs 141 2015; Sloan et al. 2015). These formats may potentially provide an advantage over antibody formats due to their smaller size and modular nature. 6.3.1 Antibody-Derived Scaffolds Bispecific T-cell engagers (BiTE®) are fusion proteins consisting of two single-chain variable fragments (scFvs) of different antibodies linked by a peptide linker. The peptide is flexible and enables free rotation of the molecule’s two targeting arms. A BiTE molecule is about 55 kDa. One of the scFvs binds to T cells via the CD3 receptor and the other to a tumor cell via a tumor-associated antigen (e.g., CD19). Blinatumomab is the first bispecific molecule approved in the USA and is used as a second-line treatment for Philadelphia chromosome-negative relapsed or refractory acute lymphoblastic leukemia. Similarly, dual-affinity retargeting (DARTs) constructs are based on the diabody format originally developed by Holliger et al. (1993) with improved stability and optimal pairing of the VH and VL chains. DARTs (~50 kDa) consist of two polypeptide chains derived from the variable domains of two different antibodies that are covalently linked by a disulfide bridge. The first chain contains VH1 and VL2 and the second one VH2 and VL1. In each chain, the binding domains are connected by a short linker that, by impeding intra-chain pairing, facilitates heterodimerization of the two chains, promoting improved stability and manufacturability. DARTs can be formatted with an Fc if a longer half-life is desired. Six DART-derived constructs are currently in clinical trials. The DARTs in clinical trials are as follows: 1. MGD006 (flotetuzumab): a novel 58.9 kDa CD3 × CD123 DART 2. MGD007: a gpA33 × CD3 DART incorporating a modified Fc domain for enhanced PK 3. MGD011 (duvortuxizumab): a humanized CD19 × CD3 DART® with a modified Fc domain for enhanced pharmacokinetic properties 4. MGD010: a CD32B × CD79B DART for autoimmunity bearing a modified Fc domain to enhance PK 5. MGD014: a HIV × CD3 DART with a mutated Fc domain (for HIV latency) 6. MGD013: a PD-1 × LAG-3 DART bearing a mutated Fc domain (co-blockade of two immune checkpoint molecules co-expressed on T cells) 6.3.2 Single-Domain Antibodies (VHHs, Nanobodies) Nanobodies® are a novel class of proprietary therapeutic proteins based on single-domain antibody fragments that contain the unique structural and functional properties of naturally occurring heavy-chain-only antibodies. The nanobody technology was originally developed following the discovery and identification that Camelidae (e.g., camels and llamas) possess fully functional antibodies that consist of heavychain- only and therefore lack light chains. These heavy-chain-only antibodies

142 L. Fayadat-Dilman contain a single variable domain (VHH) and two constant domains (C 2, C 3). The H H cloned and isolated single variable domains have full antigen-binding ability with unique structural and functional properties. Due to their small size and unique structure, nanobodies are ideal building blocks for the generation of novel multi-specific and multivalent biologic drugs. Currently, two bispecific nanobodies (http://www. ablynx.com) are in clinical development: ALX-0761 (TNF/IL-17A) and BI836880 (VEGF/Ang2). Additionally, due to their small size and potential for increased tissue penetration, nanobodies have been viewed as the “magic bullet” for molecular imaging (Chakravarty et al. 2014; Tijink et al. 2008). 6.3.3 Non-antibody Protein Scaffolds Proteins have been evaluated as backbones to create molecules which can bind to relevant targets with affinity ranging from 1 to 100 nM (Vazquez-Lombardi et al. 2015). Through randomization of amino acid sequences at the binding surface, which often resembles the structure of antibody CDR loops, libraries of scaffolds are identified via display methods. To date, around 20 non-antibody scaffolds have been reported in the literature. Targets for such domains have been predominantly cytokines (e.g., IL-17, IL6, TNFα) or cell receptors (e.g., Her2, Her3, VEGF). Non- antibody domain scaffolds are typically of a molecular weight between 6 and 20 kDa. The majority of candidates derived from non-antibody scaffolds are currently in preclinical development. This section will focus on examples of the more advanced scaffolds in the clinic, which include Kunitz domain, DARPins, affibodies, and anticalins (Škrlec et al. 2015). Kalbitor® (ecallantide) is a Kunitz domain (that are stable as standalone peptides, are able to recognize specific protein structures, and also work as competitive protease inhibitors in their free form) approved by the FDA in 2009 for the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema for patients over the age of 16. Ecallantide is a 60-amino acid polypeptide and an inhibitor of the protein kallikrein that was developed from a Kunitz domain through phage display (Lehmann 2008). Additionally, a DARPin bispecific therapeutic targeting VEGF/HGF (MP0250) is currently in Phase I clinical studies for potential treatment of solid tumors and hematological malignancies. DARPins are designed using ankyrin repeat proteins which are naturally occurring. The scaffold is built by consensus design based on available sequences from a repeat library of ankyrin (Pluckthun 2015). The number of repeats varies, but the majority have four to five repeats yielding a MW of 14–18 kDa with pM affinities (<5–100 pM) reported for these molecules (Binz and Pluckthun 2005). Affibodies have generated a great deal of interest in the medical imaging arena. Due to their low molecular weight (~6 kDa), these molecules are rapidly cleared from the blood and have a favorable biodistribution for imaging applications (Ståhl et al. 2017). Affibody molecules are small, robust, and easily engineered proteins that bind to a large number of target proteins or peptides with high-affinity, mimicking monoclonal antibodies and are therefore a member of the family of antibody

6 Novel Technologies for Generation of Bispecific Constructs 143 mimetics. A low pM-affinity affibody against the human epidermal growth factor receptor (Her2)-neu has shown an increased tumor-to-organ accumulation ratio in comparison with the anti-Her2 monoclonal antibody trastuzumab (Ståhl et al. 2017). A second-generation affibody molecule, ABY-025, is currently being evaluated as an imaging agent for (Her2)-neu in Phase I/II clinical trials (Sandberg et al. 2017). In addition to affibodies, anticalins are another non-antibody scaffold that is currently in development targeting CTLA-4, hepatocyte growth factor receptor (HGFR; MeT), IL-4Rα, and IL-23:IL-17 (Skerra 2008; Schönfeld et al. 2009). Structurally, anticalins have a conserved beta-barrel backbone and four variable loops for antigen binding (Schönfeld et al. 2008). 6.3.4 Non-antibody Scaffolds: Opportunities and Challenges Owing to their relatively low molecular weight, unmodified scaffolds that are less than 50 kDa are at a disadvantage due to their short circulating half-life (rapid renal clearance). Antibody-like scaffolds smaller than 50 kDa typically have serum half- lives of less than 1 h. A number of approaches have been used to increase the serum half-life of the various scaffolds and include fusion to albumin or an Fc domain, the addition of half-life extension peptides, pegylation, or incorporation of a domain which binds to albumin. In general, most scaffold-based drug candidates require the use of protein engineering for fusion to half-life extension entities such as polyethylene glycol (PEG), albumin, albumin binders, or the Fc region of an antibody. Tissue penetration and short half-life, however, are advantages for application of these molecules for imaging (Löfblom et al. 2010; Vazquez-Lombardi et al. 2015). A successful example of this application is the development of radiolabeled affibody molecules for the imaging of human (HER2)-positive tumors. However, for therapeutic applications, a general advantage of non-antibody scaffolds is less apparent (Shah and Betts 2013). Furthermore, non-antibody scaffolds can present potentially a greater challenge when compared to antibody-based therapeutics, particularly for those derived from nonhuman or synthetic protein libraries, because of higher risks in develop ability and immunogenicity. Traditionally, phage display technology has been the preferred format for selection of these domain scaffolds. A second advantage of small scaffolds is the ability to combine several scaffold entities (with the same or different scaffold) together via a linker with a composition and length that can be optimized based on the biology and geometry of the targets. Another potentially unique feature of scaffold multimerization is the ability to generate potent agonists for targets that can be activated through simple di- or trimerization (discussed in Chap. 3). Examples of this biologic activity include the tumor necrosis factor (TNF) family of receptors and many G-protein-coupled receptors (GPCRs). However, scaffold multimerization is not without challenges. For instance, repeating identical epitopes within a molecule can trigger innate immunity, and protein multimerization can also initiate aggregation processes increasing the immunogenic potential of the therapeutics.

144 L. Fayadat-Dilman Fig. 6.2 Considerations for bispecific platform selection Valency, Construct properties Binding Site Affinity, Effector Function Orientation, Requirements Geometry Selection: Bispecific Format Exposure, Develop ability Half-Life (PK) & Pharmacodynamic (PD) 6.4 Selection Criteria The bsAb format is best chosen to match the proposed mechanism(s) of action and the specific clinical application. To increase the chances of success and to maximize the therapeutic effect, a bsAb should be designed during the discovery phase in several alternative formats. Selection of the final format should follow the construct evaluation both in vitro and in vivo. To select a well-suited platform, various factors should be taken into consideration. These factors can be summarized as (a) develop ability, (b) target-related considerations, (c) construct-related variables, and (d) effector function requirements (Fig. 6.2). (a) Develop ability: A major obstacle in the development of bispecific antibodies has been the difficulty in producing the material in sufficient quality and quantity. The decreased stability of bispecific antibodies has often been the source of problems for downstream processes and has posed develop ability challenges preventing the progression of these molecules into development. Over the last 10 years, significant efforts were focused on devising novel and improved engineering strategies to reduce complexities for generation of small- (research scale) and large-scale materials. Hence, as discussed (Sects. 6.2 and 6.3), the key consideration for platform selection will necessitate evaluation of suitability for large-scale manufacturing and process optimization steps that may be required. (b) Target-Related Considerations: Target and construct selection are critical considerations for successful design and application of multi-specific modalities. The capability of multi-specific modalities to engage targets will depend on target expression and density profiles in either cis (expressed on the same cell, i.e., T cells) or trans (expressed on different cells, i.e., T cells and antigen-presenting cells) configuration (discussed in Chap. 2). Potential anchoring

6 Novel Technologies for Generation of Bispecific Constructs 145 effects with multi-specific modalities may impact their pharmacological effect profiles by (1) stabilizing the receptor complex on the surface (cis configuration) and/or (2) cross-linking of receptor complex in a trans configuration, hence modulating the anticipated effector function profiles. Additionally, the potential impact on target internalization properties due to cross-linking effects (rosette formation) may impact the anticipated activity profiles (reversal of receptor activation to inhibition or vice versa). Hence, a thorough in vitro/in vivo evaluation of the multi-specific constructs’ activity profiles and side-byside comparisons with their single-agent counterparts will be necessary for successful modality design and target selection. Appropriate tools and relevant controls are necessary to evaluate multi-specific construct properties. (c) Construct-Related Variables: Clarity regarding the processes that govern antibody distribution, clearance, and interaction with Fc-related biological receptors (FcRn and Fc𝛄Rs) found on the surface of immune cells in multiple species has contributed tremendously to intelligent evaluation of the exposure-response data across species. The Fc domain links the antibody to immune effector pathways. The hinge region of IgG, as well as discrete locations further along the C 2 domain, contains critical residues that facilitate interactions with the Fc H gamma family of receptors (Fc𝛄Rs) and the complement pathway. Therefore, considerations regarding the construct-related factors that govern antibody activity such as Fc-effector interaction, the anticipated pharmacokinetic halflife, and the effect-profile properties (discussed in Chap. 7) are necessary for selection of a bispecific platform (Kasturirangan et al. 2017). 6.5 Bispecific Antibodies: Selected Examples Regardless of the bispecific technology utilized, the most attractive applications for bispecific modalities are the potential pharmacological effect properties that cannot be achieved by their monospecific counterparts as single agents. Novel bispecific molecules obviously need to outperform their first-generation monospecific counterparts (i.e., monoclonal antibodies and drug combinations). Obligatory bispecifics should enable a biology which can only be achieved by a bispecific design. Selected examples are summarized below. 6.5.1 CNS Penetration Recent novel approaches have been employed to deliver antibody-based therapeutics to effect compartments that are generally not accessible to this class of therapeutics. Using a bispecific antibody, investigators examined targeting transferrin receptor (TfR) to enhance penetration of an anti-BACE1 antibody across the blood-brain barrier as a potential treatment for Alzheimer’s disease (Yu et al. 2014; Kanodia et al. 2016). BACE1 (APP-cleaving enzyme 1 or beta-site amyloid precursor protein-cleaving enzyme 1) is an aspartyl protease that is believed to contribute

146 L. Fayadat-Dilman to the pathogenesis of Alzheimer’s disease. Hence, targeting BACE1 has been a sought-after strategy. The bsAb was engineered to bind TfR, a highly expressed protein on the surface of brain endothelium. Following distribution into relevant brain compartments, the second arm of the antibody was intended to interact with the β-site of BACE1 and to neutralize the activity of this protein (Fan et al. 2015). The considerations regarding the affinity balance for the interaction with TfR vs. BACE1 allowed the release of the bsAb from the endothelium and penetration into the brain compartment in order to target BACE1. 6.5.2 Factor VIII Mimetic Emicizumab (ACE910; RG6013) is a bispecific antibody created using antibody engineering technology from Chugai to mimic the function of blood coagulation factor VII to provide a novel concept for the treatment of hemophilia A. Hemophilia A is a disease presenting severe bleeding symptoms. In this disease, the blood coagulation reaction does not proceed normally due to the deficiency or the functional disorder of coagulation factor VIII. Emicizumab binds to both the activated coagulation factor IX and to factor X mediating the activation of the latter. This is normally the function of coagulation factor VIII, which is deficient in hemophilia A patients. Only a bispecific antibody, and neither monospecific antibodies (targeting either factor IX or factor X) nor a mixture of them, exhibited the therapeutic effect observed with emicizumab. The bispecific antibody requires simultaneous binding to the appropriate epitope of both FIX and FX to place these two factors into a spatially appropriate position and precisely bring the catalytic site of FIX close to the cleavage site of FX. Emicizumab was identified via high-throughput screening of ~40,000 different bispecific antibodies. Bispecific antibodies meeting the design criteria for FVIII cofactor activity were extremely rare (Sampei et al. 2013). 6.5.3 CD3-Mediated Redirected Killing A cocktail of two or more independent antibodies are incapable of mediating the intended effect of Blincyto®. Blinatumomab is an anti CD19/CD3 bispecific T-cell engaging (BiTE®) antibody approved for acute lymphoblastic leukemia in 2014 (Baeuerle and Reinhardt 2009). Blinatumomab is unique in engaging two targets, in “TRANS” configuration, such that engagement of both targets is required for the ensuing biological effect(s). The mode of action of blinatumomab includes engaging cytotoxic effector T cells to bind the CD19-expressing B cells, ultimately causing T-cell redirected cell lysis of the engaged B cells. It belongs to a class of constructed monoclonal antibodies, or bispecific T-cell engagers (BiTEs), which exert action selectively and direct the human immune system to act against tumor cells. As this drug works by activating the T cell to exert cytotoxic activity on the target cell, recruitment efficiency of effector T cells is critical. Blinatumomab is

6 Novel Technologies for Generation of Bispecific Constructs 147 used as a second-line treatment for Philadelphia chromosome-negative relapsed or refractory B-cell precursor acute lymphoblastic leukemia (Mølhøj et al. 2007). 6.5.4 Other Applications A review of various sources, i.e., published literature, company sources, and ongoing clinical studies (https://clinicaltrials.gov), highlights the extensive research undertaken in the design and development of bispecific modalities (Table 6.2). As reflected in Table 6.2, bispecific modalities are being developed to address underlying pathology in oncology and autoimmune, inflammatory, and infectious diseases, as well as HIV. Again, for all these applications, the most attractive feature for bispecific modalities has been the achievement of potential pharmacological effect properties that cannot be matched by their monospecific counterparts as single agents. (a) Oncology: For oncology applications, mostly redirected cell killing has been employed (discussed above). Bispecific approaches have also been used for induction of dual target internalization and degradation (Lee et al. 2016). As activation of the extensive cross talk among receptors (i.e., tyrosine kinase receptors, particularly the ErbB family-Met cross talk) has emerged as a likely source of drug resistance and a high cancer recurrence, dual inhibition of these pathways has emerged as an attractive strategy (Hu et al. 2015; Castoldi et al. 2016; Lu et al. 2015). Additionally, as it is expected that overcoming T-cell exhaustion will be a key component of any successful combination for application in immuno-oncology, targeted therapies using bispecific antibodies are being examined to overcome these challenges (e.g., MGD013, an anti- PD-1 × LAG-3 DART bispecific molecule that entered the clinic in September 2017). This molecule is intended to enable co-blocking of two immune checkpoint molecules co-expressed on T cells, namely, PD-1 and LAG-3, with a single agent. MGD013 is anticipated to be used for the treatment of a wide range of cancers, including both solid tumors and hematological malignancies (https:// www.macrogenics.com/mgd013-pd-1-x-lag-3/). (b) Infectious Disease: Because an increasing number of strains of bacteria have become antibiotic-resistant, alternative strategies have been explored to efficiently manage bacterial infections. A recent study evaluated the use of a bispecific antibody, BiS4αPa, that targets two epitopes on Pseudomonas aeruginosa and acts in synergy with existing antibiotics in mouse models of infection (DiGiandomenico et al. 2014). The two targeted epitopes were described as PcrV (epitope of P. aeruginosa involved in the secretion of virulence factors) and Psl (an extracellular polysaccharide epitope expressed by the pathogen that is involved in immune evasion and biofilm formation). The antibody BiS4αPa is currently in a Phase 1 clinical trial and could provide a valuable new option to treat P. aeruginosa infections, which are responsible for about 10% of all hospital-acquired infections in the USA (Kingwell 2015).

156 L. Fayadat-Dilman (c) HIV: Synergistic bispecific antibodies against HIV have exhibited high potency in neutralization of the virus with potential impact on both established infection and as a preventive therapy (Montefiori 2016). Using the CrossMAb technology, Huang et al. (2016) generated a library of bispecific antibodies from which antibodies with varying neutralizing activities against HIV-1 were isolated: 10E8V2.0/iMab and 10E8V1.1/P140 were selected due to high potency for neutralization of the majority of circulating HIV-1 strains tested in vitro. Furthermore, 10E8V2.0/iMab was advanced into proof-of-concept in vivo studies, demonstrating potent activity as a single agent in humanized mouse models of HIV-1 treatment and prevention. Additionally, bispecific antibodies targeting different epitopes on the HIV-1 envelope were shown to exhibit broad and potent neutralization (Asokan et al. 2015). These bispecific antibodies combine the HIV-1 inhibitory activity of ibalizumab (iMab), a humanized antibody directed against domain 2 of human CD4, with that of an anti-gp120 antibody (Pace et al. 2013). Conclusions Bispecific molecules have emerged as a successful class of therapeutic agents. With more than 50 bispecific molecules currently in clinical trials and two approved bispecific molecules, it is clear that bispecific antibody-based therapeutics continue to be of high interest. Regardless of the bispecific technology utilized, the most attractive applications for bispecific modalities are the potential pharmacological properties that cannot be achieved by their monospecific counterparts as single agents. Novel bispecific molecules obviously need to outperform their first-g eneration monospecific counterparts. Although progress has historically been slowed by technical constraints, there is little doubt that bispecific antibodies and other protein or antibody bispecific derivatives will continue to gain traction as a modality and will have a prime presence in the clinical space moving forward. Chapter 7: Stimulus-Response Mechanisms: An Overview Mohammad A. Tabrizi, Shraddha S. Sadekar, Isabel Figueroa, and Ayse

162 M. A. Tabrizi et al. macro molecules within the organism, resulting in alterations of functional and biochemical pathways (i.e., inhibition or stimulation of the receptors and the relevant signaling pathways). These biochemical or functional changes characterize the specific biological response to the administered drug. Exposure–response relationships can be elucidated by studying pharmacodynamics in conjunction with dosing and pharmacokinetic information. The concentration of drug in the proximity of the biological receptor (biophase) determines the magnitude of the observed response. In simple cases, the relationships between drug dose (or drug concentrations) and observed pharmacological effect(s) can be characterized by simple pharmacodynamic models. For some drugs, however, these simple models do not sufficiently capture the concentration– effect profiles as further complexities such as counterclockwise or clockwise hysteresis loops can be observed in concentration–effect relationships at non-steady-state serum or tissue concentrations (Holford and Sheiner 1981a, b). The underlying causes for these additional complexities in exposure–response relationships can be diverse, and related to factors such as active or inhibitory metabolites, development of tolerance, indirect response characteristics, or an equilibration delay in the distribution of drug to the biophase/effect compartment (Sheiner and Steimer 2000). Mechanistic pharmacodynamic models representing complex biological processes have been developed and have contributed to the understanding of the underlying biology and clinical activity for various marketed therapeutic products. The intelligent application of PKPD modeling techniques should continue to facilitate the development of novel small-molecule and biologics therapeutics (Mager et al. 2003; Danhof et al. 2008). This brief overview is provided to highlight the key concepts significant for evaluation of stimulus-response mechanisms. This topic was discussed in details in the first edition of this publication (Development of Antibody- Based Therapeutics: Translational Considerations, 2012). 7.2 Stimulus-Response Mechanisms A key feature in drug development, critical in both early preclinical development and later translational stages, is examination of the correlations between biological stimulus processes (receptor signaling and pathway amplifications) and desired pharmacological effect. The stimulus–response mechanisms that convert receptor occupancy into pharmacological activity are generally nonlinear and frequently disproportional (Kenakin 1993). Diversity in the relationships between receptor occupancy and response intensity could range from highly efficient to non-efficient responses (Fig. 7.1). For highly efficient responses, the binding curves denoting receptor occupancy (or binding) lie to the right of the concentration–response curves (K > EC50) owing to large amplification factors in signaling cascades and D biological network elements. By contrast, for the non-efficient responses, loss of signal in the cascade might contribute to the underlying system inefficiency and, hence, receptor occupancy curves might lie to the left of the concentration–response

163 Non-Efficient Response curves (K < EC50). Highly efficient pharmacological systems have been described D previously for production of glucose via glucagon or beta-adrenoceptor pathways (Kenakin 1993). For biologics, antibodies, or fragments, in which effector functions (ADCC, CDC, or effector cell recruitment) contribute significantly to the underlying mechanisms of action, highly efficient pharmacological responses are observed. One such class of biologics producing highly efficient response(s) is the bispecific T-cell engager, or BiTE® constructs (Wolf et al. 2005). BiTE® is approximately a 55 kDa flexibly linked single-chain construct. A flexible peptide linker connects the heavy and light chain of the variable region of each fragment and then the two constructs are fused together by a shorter linker (of about five amino acids). One single-chain variable fragment (scFv) is specific for a tumor cell antigen and the other is directed against the CD3 subunit of the T-cell receptor complex (Wolf et al. 2005). BiTE® constructs transiently connect T cells to the intended target cells for potent redirected cell lysis with application in oncology (Baeuerle et al. 2009). Due to their flexible design as well as their ability to engage CD3, BiTE® constructs cause concomitant activation, serial lysis, and expansion of T cells in vivo (Baeuerle et al. 2009). In vitro observations indicated that these constructs can mediate their pharmacological effect at relatively low effector-to-target ratio combinations (Dreier et al. 2002; Hoffmann et al. 2005; Torisu-Itakura et al. 2011). Hence, as an exception to the classical receptor theory, BiTE® constructs show maximum pharmacological effect at low receptor occupancies, demonstrating a highly efficient response (PD) profile (K D ≫ EC 50 ). Close investigation of the receptor occupancy-response for blinatumomab in B-cell lymphoma cell lines highlights this important property (Fig. 7.2a). Comparison of model-predicted CD19 occupancy and experimentally observed in vitro pharmacological response have shown greater than a 1000-fold difference in their reported K and EC (the in vitro response potency) values D 50 (Fig. 7.2b). Additionally, the high in vitro response-efficiency observed with these modalities has been translated into their in vivo activity profiles. Highly efficient BiTE® constructs are clinically administered at about a 1000-fold lower dose than their traditional counterparts—i.e., monoclonal antibodies targeting the specific tumor cell antigens in the absence of CD3 engagement (Table 7.1). ro tceffE ycnapuccO rotpeceR ro tceffE ycnapuccO rotpeceR ro tceffE ycnapuccO rotpeceR 7 Stimulus-Response Mechanisms: An Overview Equi-Efficient Response High-Efficient Response Binding Effect Effect Binding Effect Receptor Binding Concentration Concentration Concentration The response to a drug is less than proportional The response to a drug is proportional The response to a drug is more than proportional to the number of receptors occupied. to the number of receptors occupied. to the number of receptors occupied. The signature pattern in the temporal changes for the affected response variable (R) is generally observed for drugs which can alter the production (k ) or dissipation (k ) process normally controlling endogenous levels of response in out (R). Drugs can inhibit or stimulate any of these processes (Fig. 7.3). The four basic indirect response (IDR) models include a zero-order production rate constant and a first-order elimination or removal rate constant to describe the turnover of the pharmacodynamic variable(s) and drugs that can inhibit or stimulate either of these processes. The general characteristics of IDR models have been discussed in depth previously and will not be captured here (Jusko and Ko 1994; Sharma and Jusko 1998). This class of pharmacodynamic models account for the PKPD of numerous therapeutics which inhibit or stimulate production or loss of the response variables. The basic models can also be extended to deal with various complexities (i.e., the extended indirect response model; Mager and Jusko 2001; Mager et al. 2003).

166 M. A. Tabrizi et al. 7.4 Application in Drug Discovery and Development Indirect response models have been widely used to describe the changes in the time course of biomarkers and pharmacological effect profiles following therapeutic modulation with various anti-inflammatory drugs (Lon et al. 2013). In general, this approach offers a semi-mechanistic understanding of the anti-inflammatory modulation and the ensuing effect profile(s). In this section, we briefly review selected published examples both in preclinical and clinical settings in order to elucidate the application of this approach in drug discovery and development. 7.4.1 Semi-mechanistic Modeling in Treatment of Psoriasis The IDR approach was successfully used in evaluating the effect profiles for ustekinumab in patients with severe psoriasis (Zhu et al. 2010). Ustekinumab is a human IgG1κ antibody that binds to human IL-12 and IL-23 and has demonstrated efficacy in patients with psoriasis and psoriatic arthritis (Rozenblit and Lebwohl 2009). Clinically, the psoriasis severity at baseline and following therapeutic modulation with anti-inflammatory treatments is measured using the Psoriasis Area and Severity Index (PASI). A PASI score is measured on selected skin regions where intensity of redness, thickness, and scaling of psoriasis is assessed and hence is a composite index reflecting the severity of the disease (Ashcroft et al. 1999). To quantitatively evaluate ustekinumab effects in patients with psoriasis, a previously established population PK model was used to describe the time course of changes in the drug exposure (Hu and Zhou 2008). As placebo administration resulted in a small but significant effect on PASI score, the authors attempted to capture the time course of the placebo effect, along with that observed following administration of ustekinumab, using the IDR Model I (Zhu et al. 2010). An equation describing the effect of ustekinumab (and placebo) on PASI score is shown below: d(PASI(t))  E ⋅Cγ  =k ⋅1− max p −k ⋅plb⋅PASI(tt) dt in   IC 5 γ 0 +C p γ   out The placebo effect (plb) was described as a function of maximum placebo effect and the rate constant to reach the plateau in PASI score. The complexity of the model emerged from the inclusion of the empirical baseline function. Additionally, with application of the PKPD modeling approach employed in this study, the authors were able to stratify patients based on their response to the administered drug. In the responder population, the model-estimated potency (IC ) reflected a 30-fold higher 50 sensitivity relative to the partial responder population. This finding suggested that the partial responders were not as sensitive to ustekinumab’s effects in comparison to the responder population, highlighting that nonresponders may require higher doses or more frequent administration of ustekinumab in order to elicit comparable efficacy.

7 Stimulus-Response Mechanisms: An Overview 167 A similar modeling approach was used to characterize the PKPD of brodalumab, an antibody targeting the IL-17 receptor used to treat psoriasis patients. In this analysis, PK properties of brodalumab were described using a two-compartment model with both linear and nonlinear (Michelis-Menten) elimination pathways. The traditional effect-compartment modeling (Sheiner and Steimer 2000) was used to link PK to changes in PASI score with an indirect response model. This model was superior in comparison to the alternative PKPD modeling approaches employed (i.e., direct modulation of psoriatic plaque turnover by drug in central compartment and the traditional effect-site modeling). The placebo effect was modeled similar to the previous ustekinumab example, though the contribution of the placebo was described by its impact on the degradation rate constant (Zhu et al. 2010). An equation describing the effect of brodalumab (and placebo) on PASI score is shown below: d(PASI(t))  E ⋅Cγ  =k ⋅1− max p −k ⋅plb⋅PASI(tt) dt in   IC 5 γ 0 +C p γ   out Complete inhibition of the PASI score was not observed in the study; however the maximum inhibition (E ) of PASI formation was estimated to be approximax mately 1, suggesting that theoretically at higher doses a full inhibition of PASI can be achieved. The estimated parameter describing formation of psoriasis plaque (k ) was found to be similar to the estimated parameter in the ustekinumab syn study, highlighting the similarities in the underlying disease pathology and dynamics. 7.4.2 RANK Ligand Modulation Denosumab is a fully human Xenomouse®-derived IgG2 antibody directed against human RANKL that is used for the treatment of osteoporosis, treatment-induced bone loss, bone metastases, multiple myeloma, and giant cell tumor of bone (Bekker et al. 2004; Lewiecki et al. 2007). Denosumab is designed to inhibit RANKL (RANK ligand), a protein that acts as the primary signal for bone removal with its receptor RANK. In many bone loss conditions, RANKL overwhelms the body’s natural defenses against bone destruction. Pharmacokinetic properties of denosumab are highly influenced by target (RANKL) expression and density (Tabrizi et al. 2010). A strong correlation has been observed between the target saturation and other mechanistic bone resorption markers such as N- and C-telopeptides (NTX and CTX, respectively) in preclinical models and human patients. These biomarkers additionally have shown a strong correlation with other proof-of-concept markers such as bone mineral density, a critical endpoint for evaluation of denosumab therapeutic efficacy (Tabrizi et al. 2010). Recently, the in vivo exposure-time profiles for denosumab and ibandronate (an orally administered drug used for treating osteoporosis) were linked to the relevant bone resorption biomarkers (NTX and CTX) as well as changes in bone mineral

168 M. A. Tabrizi et al. density in postmenopausal women with bone loss (Marathe et al. 2011). Combining the PK models for denosumab (Marathe et al. 2008) and ibandronate (Pillai et al. 2004) with an integrated bone homeostasis model, as published previously (Lemaire et al. 2004), resulted in development of a mechanistic PKPD model for describing the bone resorption biomarkers as well as the changes in bone mineral density using both (1) indirect response models and (2) a fully integrated bone homeostasis approach. Consistent with the known mechanism of action for both drugs, in the first modeling approach, bone resorption biomarkers were described by IDR Model I while changes in the bone mineral density were described by the IDR Model IV. Based on this approach, it was assumed that denosumab and ibandronate had inhibitory actions on the production rate of NTX and CTX, respectively, and additionally denosumab had an inhibitory action on the degradation rate of bone mineral density. In the second approach, the changes in the osteoblasts (active and responding) and the active osteoclasts were described by zero-order production and first-order elimination processes, respectively. Changes in the active osteoclasts and osteoblasts were then correlated with RANK and TGF occupancies, respectively (Marathe et al. 2008). Ibandronate concentration in bone was used to inhibit the production of active osteoclasts. Resorption biomarkers were described as a function of the changes in active osteoclasts relative to the basal concentrations of the biomarkers (NTX and CTX). The changes in the bone mineral density were described via processes whereby active osteoblasts and osteoclasts stimulated the zero-order production rate and the first-order degradation rate of bone marrow density, respectively. The predictions from the two modeling approaches employed were comparable. While the fully integrated bone homeostasis model benefited from including essential characteristics of bone biology, IDR modeling was able to successfully describe the drug effects on the biomarkers and the clinical outcome. Additionally, the fully integrated PKPD approach provided a mechanistic platform for prediction of the long-term clinical endpoint and the dynamics in the underlying pharmacology following therapeutic modulation. Conclusions A key feature in drug development is examination of the correlations between biological stimulus processes and desired pharmacological effect(s). Diversity in the relationships between receptor occupancy and response intensity could benefit from application of quantitative pharmacology and PKPD principles. The intelligent application of PKPD modeling techniques has been critical in development of novel small-molecule and biologics therapeutics. Mechanistic pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic modeling approaches such as IDR models have contributed to this success. Indirect response models have been widely and successfully used to describe the changes in the time course of biomarkers and pharmacological effect profiles for various therapeutic modalities and have significantly enhanced the understanding of the disease pathology and various complexities in the underlying biological processes.

Chapter 8: Evaluation of Tumor Growth Inhibition in Preclinical Tumor Models: A Quantitative Approach Shraddha Sadekar

172 S. Sadekar et al. article(s) and inhibition of tumor growth is evaluated with respect to the control test article by measurements of tumor volume (or size). In general, tumor growth models have a degree of intrinsic variability, so studies must be powered with an adequate number of replicates to enable differentiation of the anticipated and desired effects. Best practices for animal care and welfare, as set forth in ethical guidelines, prescribe that if the tumor size in an animal exceeds the established “cutoff” value or large losses in body weight are observed, the animal must be euthanized. Because of this, animals that are in the untreated groups or groups receiving less efficacious treatments are frequently followed for shorter times as compared to the groups that receive more efficacious treatments. As a result, the experimental data may have a treatment-dependent bias. This limitation must be considered when quantitative approaches are employed for evaluation of preclinical data. Appropriate statistical analysis of tumor growth data is necessary for proper interpretation of preclinical studies. A myriad of tumor models have been used with the goal of mimicking human disease; however, the translatability of these models is limited. Interpretation of the findings and implications for human disease obtained from preclinical models is rarely straightforward and is limited by various factors such as tumor type, choice of species, strain background, and MOA (i.e., construct-related factors: cytotoxic vs. cytostatic properties, immune modulation requirements, tumor penetration, effect-site localization and biodistribution, pharmacodynamics efficiency across species). As an example, immuno-oncology drugs require functional immune systems and hence relevant animal models (i.e., syngeneic tumor models or human reconstituted mouse models) are used. In these instances, differences in immune function/activation across species are a critical consideration. Additionally, target expression profiles, density, construct affinity, and epitope recognition across species are other critical factors (First Edition). Here, we describe some of the quantitative methods used in the drug development process to evaluate antitumor activity in preclinical models. Depending on their merit and limitations, some quantitative approaches may be more suitable for the study objectives and the expected application and use of the experimental outcomes. 8.2 Single-Agent Therapy 8.2.1 Comparison of Tumor Size at a Single Time Point One of the simplest, but most limited, approaches for comparing drug effect on tumor growth is evaluation of the tumor size for treated and untreated groups at a specific time point, frequently, at the end of the study. While this methodology may be used to qualitatively describe the data, selection of the time point will inevitably impact the conclusions (see the case study in Sect. 8.3). Although a dose-dependent response may be observed, estimation of the salient construct-related properties (i.e., potency and maximum effect) is highly sensitive to the selected time point at which the analysis is performed. Additionally, comparison of dose potency for

8 Quantitative Evaluation of Tumor Growth Inhibition in Tumor Models 173 multiple constructs, addressing the same target at the screening stage, is highly limited by the absence of pharmacokinetic (PK) data. This approach, in general, suffers from key limitations that could result in erroneous conclusions (Sect. 8.3). 8.2.2 AUEC Versus Dose Area under the effect curve (AUEC) is obtained based on evaluation of the complete tumor growth curve and is compared to an untreated control group. This approach benefits from ease of implementation and inclusion of the whole growth curve but is limited and must be used with caution. First, this approach is highly limited by the absence of exposure data; hence, it ignores the factors that regulate the effective concentrations that drive the pharmacodynamic effect(s). Additionally, due to potential differences in tumor growth rates between control groups and treated animals and considerations regarding the animal care and welfare (as stated above), duration of the study may be different in the control versus treated groups. Therefore, for fast-growing tumors that are responsive to treatment, large differences in the duration of the study for various groups may be observed. Without inclusion of appropriate statistical methods, this approach can be biased. 8.2.3 Evaluation of Tumor Doubling Time The tumor doubling time (TDT) is defined as the time that a tumor takes to reach twice its previous volume (i.e., exponential growth half-life (Tubiana 2009)). Typically, initial tumor growth kinetics follows an exponential rate and as the tumor grows in volume its rate of growth declines. In cases where the study duration falls within the exponential growth rate, the tumor volume versus time profile can be characterized by the tumor doubling time parameter; TDT can be described using the following equation: æ 0.693 ö TV=T expç- time÷ Vo è TDT ø In this equation, TV reflects the tumor volume (and can be replaced by tumor size), and T is the tumor volume at baseline (when treatment is initiated). This Vo equation can be used to describe the experimental data to obtain a value of TDT for individual animals. Group statistics can be computed for comparison among treatments. Longer tumor doubling times indicate that tumor growth has been slowed down and suggest higher antitumor activity. One of the main advantages of this methodology is that the TDT parameter has a physiological meaning. It is commonly used to evaluate single-dose administration, but other regimens can be used as long as the kinetics follows exponential growth. Note that in other cases where there is a delay in the drug action or the observed growth kinetics is not exponential (e.g., exponential linear growth), this approach is not well suited to describe the

174 S. Sadekar et al. drug effect. Additionally, the corresponding kinetic growth constant, k, can be growth estimated from TDT as a measure of tumor activity (Sect. 8.3). The kinetic growth constant is related to TDT by the following mathematical relationship: k =0.693/TDT growth The kinetic growth constant (unlike TDT) is capable of representing tumor growth conditions, as a continuum variable, and the estimates can represent tumor growth progression (Confrey et al. 1994): (a) k > 0 for increases in tumor size growth (b) k ~ 0 for tumor stasis growth (c) k < 0 for tumor shrinkage growth 8.2.4 Minimum Efficacious Dose The minimum efficacious dose (MED) has been used to define the dose required to achieve a certain level of efficacy in a given tumor model (Fink et al. 2011; Yu et al. 2007). For example, the MED is chosen as the dose required for achieving tumor stasis or a specific tumor size at a certain time point. As MEDs may be tumor type dependent, this parameter is used when construct performance is compared in the same tumor model. It is often used in conjunction with the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) for estimation of the experimental therapeutic index. Again, this approach is limited by lack of inclusion of the exposure data in the analysis. 8.2.5 Unperturbed Growth Rate In general, tumor growth rates may have widely different kinetic properties. For practical reasons, faster growing tumors are often preferred to their slower growing counterparts. Faster growing tumors may offer clear differentiation earlier following therapeutic intervention for shorter study durations. In general, tumor growth rates can often be represented by an initial exponential growth stage followed by a second phase described by first-order kinetics. In this case, the following equation can be applied: dTV k k TV Unperturbed growth rate= = o 1 dt k +k TV 1 o This approach can represent the unperturbed growth rate (UGR), where k is the o exponential growth parameter and k represents the linear growth rate (Simeoni 1 et al. 2013). Note that when the tumor volume is small (k 1 + k o TV ≈ k 1 ) the described growth rate is approximately exponential and, when the tumor volumes are large, the rate of growth is approximately linear (k 1 + k o TV ≈ k o TV).

8 Quantitative Evaluation of Tumor Growth Inhibition in Tumor Models 175 8.2.6 Quantitative Exposure-Response Approaches Exposure-response (ER) models are used to relate the plasma concentration time profiles to the time course of the effect(s), i.e., tumor growth inhibition. This approach makes use of the salient features observed in both exposure and effect data collected during the study and can generate systems and construct-related parameters (Sheiner and Steimer 2000). Moreover, inter- and intraindividual variability can be addressed by relevant statistical analysis of the experimental data. ER models provide relevant insight for comparisons between test articles and allow inclusion of differences in various experimental designs. One of the key advantages of this approach is the ability to uncouple the exposure and response properties of a given drug candidate and to allow evaluation of potential time dependencies in both exposure (due to up- or downregulation of enzymatic processes, protein binding, tissue saturation, etc.) and response processes (tolerance or sensitization). With inclusion of relevant assumptions and considerations with respect to the selected species, the relevant exposure-effect properties for the lead candidate may be translated across species. For evaluation of tumor growth data using this approach, the net tumor growth can be described by a differential equation that includes both the unperturbed growth rate and the construct-dependent growth impact as used in previous work (Sukumaran et al. 2015): dTV k k TV = o 1 – f(TV, C,Kmax, IIC50,¼) dt k +k TV1o Treatment Dependent Unperturbed KillingRate GrowthRate In this equation, the first term represents the net growth of the untreated group and the second term represents the construct-related impact on tumor growth. The killing function, f(TV, C, k, KC ), can take diverse forms and it is typically max 50 selected to describe the killing kinetics of the treatments being studied. An example of such a function is the sigmoidal model shown below: KmaxC Killing rate= f(TV, C, Kmax, IC50,¼)= TV IC50+C This approach includes both construct-related properties (C: plasma concentration; K: killing potency; and K: maximum killing efficiency), and system factors 50 max that regulate tumor growth or tumor volume (TV). Here, the estimated potency values obtained via this approach correspond to the plasma concentrations that drive the observed activity in the tumor tissue. This approach was recently used to model the tumor growth inhibition of two antibody therapeutics: trastuzumab and Anti- Steap1 TDC (Thiomab Drug Conjugate) (Sukumaran et al. 2015). A mathematical function was used to capture the complex pharmacokinetics of the different drug species present in the system, the unperturbed tumor growth was modeled using a first-order growth function, and the antitumor activity of these agents was

176 S. Sadekar et al. represented by a sigmoidal function that was employed to account for the effect of all pharmacologically active species. An additional efficacy parameter, the tumor static concentration (TSC), may also be derived from this approach. The TSC is defined as the plasma concentration at which tumor stasis is achieved. In many cases, delays between the plasma or blood concentrations and the observed tumor growth effect are observed; this dissociation between exposure and effect time course should be considered during analysis to describe effectively the drug properties. The approach by Simeoni et al. (2013) assumed that the observed overall tumor volume is the result of the contribution of various cell populations with distinct biological properties: (a) proliferating cells, (b) damaged cells, and (c) failing cells. Based on this approach, drug treatment can perturb growth by driving tumor cells from one state to another. An alternate approach used was to capture the delayed antitumor effect based on pharmacodynamic transduction models. An example of this application is shown in the work of Yang et al. (2010), where the authors developed a semi-mechanistic model using several transduction compartments to describe the exposure-response relationships for various chemotherapeutic agents. These investigators established that both the transduction model and the cell cycle function can be used for evaluation of tumor growth behavior. Lobo and Balthazar also employed an approach using “transit” compartments to describe drug effects in vivo and in vitro (Lobo and Balthasar 2002). Additionally, multi-scale approaches have been used to capture exposure-response relationships for antibody-drug conjugates (Shah et al. 2012; Haddish-Berhane et al. 2013). These approaches included mechanistic models that attempted to address various processes such as drug distribution from the systemic circulation into tumor tissues, drug binding to the target, target internalization, and efflux of the drug and release of the payload. One of the advantages of this approach is that the model parameters can be potentially associated with relevant physiological and biological processes determined by in vitro experimental approaches. 8.3 Antibody Drug Conjugate Kadcyla® (ado-trastuzumab emtansine,1 T-DM1; http://www.kadcyla.com/hcp/) is an antibody-drug conjugate consisting of the monoclonal antibody trastuzumab (Herceptin®) linked to the cytotoxic agent maytansinoid (DM1) (Jumbe et al. 2010). Trastuzumab alone stops growth of cancer cells by binding to the HER2/neu receptor, whereas DM1 enters cells and destroys them by binding to tubulin. Preclinical efficacy studies for this construct consisted of a series of preclinical tumor models where the efficacy of T-DM1 was evaluated. Here, application of various empirical methods as described in Sect. 8.2 (doseresponse evaluation only, as no drug concentration data was available) is demonstrated: 1 Emtansine is a combination of DM1 and the stable linker.

8 Quantitative Evaluation of Tumor Growth Inhibition in Tumor Models 177 (a) Evaluation of potency using a single time point (Sect. 8.2.1): Using previously published data (Jumbe et al. 2010), potency of T-DM1 was evaluated at various “single” time points (Fig. 8.1). Because of the significant differences observed in response at various study days, the single-time-point analysis was only applied to data obtained on study days 7 and 14. As discussed in Sect. 8.2.1, selection of the time point inevitably impacted the study conclusions regarding the dose potency (Fig. 8.1; Table 8.1). Analysis of data obtained on day 7 relative to day 14 resulted in an ~3-fold difference in the estimated potency (3 vs. 9 mg/kg; Table 8.1). (b) AUEC versus dose (Sect. 8.2.2): The AUEC method was applied to estimate drug potency (Fig. 8.1b). First, a truncated AUEC was used only to day 14 because the control animal group could not be carried throughout the study past day 14 (Jumbe et al. 2010). For the expanded approach using the full study time period, it was assumed that the tumor volume at the remaining study days for the control group was similar to that observed on study day 14 (the study cutoff for the control group). A comparable ED50 value was observed using either of these approaches (~9–10 mg/kg; Table 8.1). (c) Evaluation of potency using TDT (Sect. 8.2.3): Nonlinear regression analysis was used to estimate the growth rates corresponding to each treatment using data reported in Jumbe, 2010. Tumor growth data were analyzed for individual animals as longitudinal samples of the tumor volume when available (data not shown).
