This case study clearly shows the merit and limitations of the methods applied as discussed in Sect. 8.2. Certainly, selection of a quantitative approach for evaluation of tumor growth data will inherently carry limitations that need to be considered. For the same data set, variability in the estimated parameters via different approaches demonstrates that quantitative results obtained must be used with caution. Depending on their merits and limitations, some quantitative approaches may be more suitable depending on the study objectives and on the expected application and use of the experimental outcomes. 8.4 Combination Therapy 8.4.1 Immunotherapy Combinations To achieve a more effective therapeutic benefit, combination therapies have been a common practice in cancer therapy. In immuno-oncology (IO) therapy, antigen-specific activation of a patient’s immune response is employed for achieving therapeutic benefits. In light of the promise that this novel therapy has bestowed to cancer therapy, combination strategies have become even more critical for achieving the most effective result, namely enhancing the favorable outcome in a broader cancer indication with concomitant decrease in potential adverse events (Drake 2012; Morrissey et al. 2016). Mechanisms that potentiate the immune response via different stages in the cancer immunity cycle may be critical to overcome resistance and restore functional immune surveillance in the tumor microenvironment (Chen and Mellman 2013). Examples of combination therapies that have proved promising are vaccines combined with cytokines: gp100 peptide vaccine + IL2, critical immune checkpoint

8 Quantitative Evaluation of Tumor Growth Inhibition in Tumor Models 179 inhibitors (PD1 + CTLA4), and immune-stimulatory agents (PDL1 + CTLA4 + IL2) targeting different stages of the cancer immunity cycle, as well as cytokines in combination with anti-angiogenic targets (IFN-α + VEGF) (Yu et al. 2010; Schwartzentruber et al. 2011; Curran et al. 2010). Combination immunotherapy treatment may be useful to inhibit multiple checkpoint inhibitors when scientific evidence favors modulation of other inhibitory receptors that contribute to escape from immune surveillance, as seen in nonresponders induced by the first therapy. Immunotherapy combinations with standard-of-care radiation therapy have also been employed (Gulley et al. 2005). Radiation therapy in combination with immunotherapy may enhance tumor antigen(s) presentation and potentially result in an abscopal effect, which is important for prevention of tumor metastasis (Demaria et al. 2004). Chemotherapeutics combined with vaccines as well as T-cell modulators have also shown promise (North 1982). Immune-potentiating mechanisms of chemotherapeutics such as elimination of immunosuppressive cells, activation of immune effector cells, and sensitization of tumor cells to lysis, as well as their ability to access intracellular targets, have been harnessed in combination with IO agents (Morrissey et al. 2016). There are few effective IO drugs approved for clinical use with many more currently in preclinical evaluation. As the field of single-agent IO therapies evolves, optimization of combination approaches at the preclinical stage is critical for future advancements in the field. In the next section, established quantitative approaches to evaluate drug combinations and interactions are summarized. 8.4.2 Quantitative Methods to Evaluate Drug Combination/ Interaction Both semi-mechanistic and mechanistic approaches have been applied to evaluate drug interactions (Danhof et al. 2007; Foucquier and Guedj 2015; Jonker et al. 2005). In semi-mechanistic approaches, drug combinations are classified as additive, synergistic, or antagonistic using effect-based or dose-effect-based approaches (Foucquier and Guedj 2015). Effect-based approaches are a quick and easy way to evaluate combination effects when a dose-response evaluation has not been implemented. Response additivity or Bliss independence methods may be used to classify the nature of drug combination effects (Foucquier and Guedj 2015). Dose-effectbased approaches evaluate combination properties over a wide dose range and are more data intensive. They typically use the Loewe additivity method to classify the nature of the drug interaction by isobologram analysis. Isobologram analysis with a linear additivity requires dose-effect curves with constant potency ratios and equal maximal effects. For drugs that do not have a constant potency ratio, curvilinear isoboles may be observed (Foucquier and Guedj 2015). To evaluate drug interactions over a range of drug concentrations, drug doses may be selected using a multiple-ray design or a full factorial design (Foucquier and

180 S. Sadekar et al. Guedj 2015). A multiple-ray design maintains fixed dose ratios while simultaneously varying both drug concentrations. It is typically best suited for a response surface analysis which provides insight into differences in drug interactions spanning concentration ranges for each drug (Foucquier and Guedj 2015). A full factorial design varies the drug concentrations of one drug while fixing concentrations of the second drug at various levels (Foucquier and Guedj 2015). Such a design is expected to suit a curve-shift analysis as illustrated in the case study in Sect. 8.5. The operational model of agonism has been utilized to understand mechanisms of drug interactions in the literature (Jonker et al. 2005). This model distinguishes between drug and system-dependent properties by viewing the drug–receptor interaction as a three-step sequential process of drug–receptor binding, receptor activation, and signal transduction (Fig. 8.2). Single-pathway models describe drug interactions on a receptor-binding level (Fig. 8.2a) (Jonker et al. 2005). Competitive drug interactions compete for the same receptor site while allosteric interactions compete for different sites on the same receptor. Single-pathway interactions are known to alter potency while not impacting maximal efficacy (Fig. 8.2b) (Jonker et al. 2005). Dual-pathway models describe drug interactions on the transduction level where both potency and maximal efficacy are expected to be impacted (Fig. 8.2a, b) (Jonker et al. 2005). In dual-pathway interactions, the nature of pre-amplification of the pharmacological systems determines the range and degree of synergy as well as concentration ranges in which synergy occurs (see also Chap. 7). The highest synergistic effect is observed when two pathways converge late in the transduction cascade and in systems with a low degree of amplification/efficiency of signal transduction (Jonker et al. 2005). 8.4.3 Evaluation of Drug Interactions for Tumor Growth Inhibition Dose-response approaches in evaluation of tumor growth inhibition for single-agent drugs have been discussed in Sect. 8.2. Evaluation of drug interaction in vivo is a resource-intensive endeavor and difficult to implement. Literature examples for tumor inhibition effects following drug combination therapies have been previously reported (Goteti et al. 2010; Harrold et al. 2012; Koch et al. 2009; Parra-Guillen et al. 2013; Terranova et al. 2013; Wilson et al. 2015; Wu 2013). Koch et al. reported evaluation of interactions for anticancer drugs tested in tumor-bearing mice (Koch et al. 2009). In an effect-response model characterized by a smooth nonlinear tumor growth curve, a transit compartment model, and a first-order tumor kill rate model, the interaction term for the tumor kill rate was used to quantify the extent of interaction. The impact of the first drug on tumor growth (i.e., kill rate) was fixed at the single-agent therapy concentration; the addition of the second drug was assumed to impact the kill rate of the first drug in combination. A positive parameter estimate for the interaction term was viewed as a synergistic interaction.

First the effect of each drug on tumor growth was evaluated in syngeneic tumor models as single agents. A dose-ranging evaluation of tumor growth inhibition was conducted to estimate the in vivo potency and maximal efficacy of each drug. Each drug construct was administered via intraperitoneal injection for a total of five doses (doses were administered every 4 days). Tumor growth inhibition for each drug was characterized by a semi-mechanistic exposure-response model incorporating exponential tumor growth. The structural components of the model are shown in Fig. 8.3. Drug input was fixed based on the previously estimated pharmacokinetic parameters describing time profiles for each drug at various concentrations. In vivo drug potency and tumor kill efficiency were estimated by fitting tumor volume data to the model shown in Fig. 8.3. Tumor growth inhibition for each drug as a single agent was well characterized. Both drugs as single agents had comparable in vivo potencies and kill capacities (Table 8.2). 8.5.2 Combination Therapy Evaluation of tumor growth inhibition for combinations of drugs A and B: For combination evaluation, drugs A and B were administered at varying doses, selected based on an understanding of the single-agent dose-response of each drug (study design outlined in Table 8.3). Combination tumor growth effects were characterized by a semi-mechanistic exposure-response model shown in Fig. 8.4. 184 7000 6000 5000 4000 3000 8.6 Conclusions In preclinical drug development, it is critical to gain a quantitative understanding of tumor growth inhibition in response to therapeutics. Such an understanding can help gain insight into dose-response and facilitate dose decisions. Quantitative approaches employed to evaluate tumor growth inhibition can impact assessment of dose-response for both single-agent and combination therapies. The study design, mechanism of action of drug(s), as well as objective of the quantitative evaluation should be considered in choosing the most appropriate analysis method. Empirical evaluation approaches such as the “area under the effect curve” approach may be more appropriate when a quick evaluation is desired with no intent of extrapolation to other treatment regimens or species. Other methods such as the “tumor growth rate” approach that parameterize the tumor growth and inhibition with fundamental system parameters are more desirable for extrapolation to other treatment regimens and for semi-mechanistic or mechanistic translation across species. For quantitative evaluation of combination drugs for tumor growth inhibition, semi-mechanistic as well as mechanistic PKPD modeling can help provide insight into the mechanism of drug interaction, combination dose selection, and dose projections. Chapter 9: Application of Proof-of-Mechanism Biomarkers in Design and Development of Biologics Modalities Glareh Azadi

188 G. Azadi et al. measurable endpoints that allow prediction of desirable or deleterious outcomes, i.e., proof of concept, POC (Young 2009; Lee et al. 2007; Chau et al. 2008). Similarly, evaluation of comparable biomarkers in early preclinical development during the screening phase and at later stages of preclinical development should allow for the design of successful translational strategies from discovery to the clinic while identifying potential risks to humans and establishing safe first-inhuman (FIH) dosing strategies (Tabrizi et al. 2009; Tabrizi and Roskos 2007). Evaluation of relevant biomarkers in appropriate animal models can greatly enhance translation of exposure–response relationships across species (Tabrizi et al. 2009; Tabrizi and Roskos 2007; Buckley et al. 2008). When appropriate immunoassay methodologies are available, relationships between antibody pharmacokinetics (PK) and ensuing effects on POM or POP biomarkers can be effectively examined (Young 2009; Lee et al. 2007; Chau et al. 2008; Tabrizi and Roskos 2006; Tabrizi et al. 2006). Evaluation of PK–PD relationships in vivo can provide invaluable information with respect to antibody potency (EC ), and the maximum pharmaco-50 dynamic response efficiency (E ), thereby facilitating determination of the clinical max dose and dose escalation plans (Tabrizi et al. 2006, 2009; Tabrizi and Roskos 2006; US Department of Health and Human Services 2005). Many of the ABTs raised against soluble antigens, such as circulating TNF, VEGF, IgE, and various cytokines (e.g., IL-8, IL-5), have undergone extensive research both in animal models and clinical studies. For this class of ABTs, application of mechanistic exposure–response models using POM biomarkers can prove highly effective for the design of successful translational strategies throughout various development stages. Following administration of therapeutic doses of an ABT, it is anticipated that the free concentrations of the antigen are suppressed. However, in general the elimination rate of small antigens is reduced upon binding to the ABT causing a simultaneous increase in the ABT-antigen complex concentration. The magnitude of the in vivo increase in ABT-antigen complex concentration is governed by the turnover rate of the antigen (i.e., antigen synthesis and clearance rates) relative to the elimination rate of the complex and the strength of the interaction (affinity). In many instances, due to detection limit requirements for the bioanalytical methodologies used for quantification of free antigen concentrations, experimental evaluation of the free antigen concentration post-ABT dosing is not feasible. However, as a decrease in free antigen concentration is correlated with an increase in the bound antibody-antigen complex, application of PK–PD modeling approaches should allow for extrapolation of the free antigen concentrations when the bound or total antigen complex is experimentally measured. In this chapter, we discuss the use of POM biomarkers, i.e., in vivo evaluation of the antibody-antigen interaction, during early preclinical development. 9.2 Translational Approach A unique feature of antibody-based therapeutics is the high specificity conferred by the antibody interaction with a specific region on the targeted antigen. Hence, it is not surprising that efficacy and safety of this class of therapeutic are

9 Application of Proof-of-Mechanism Biomarkers (POM) in Design 189

generally correlated with the extent of their interactions with the intended target antigen(s). Monoclonal antibodies are generally safe and well tolerated, and the toxicities observed with this class of biologics are usually due to an exaggerated pharmacology (Tabrizi and Roskos 2007). The antigen-related toxicities are often predictable from preclinical studies if the antibody cross-reacts with the target antigen in the relevant animal models. The primary requirement for preclinical safety studies is that the antibody cross-reacts with and functionally modulates the target antigen in the species of interest in the same manner as the human antigen. This can be evaluated by analyzing the cross-species sequence homology, critical residues in the epitope, affinity, and functional activity of the antigen in a given animal model. When cross-reactivity of a lead antibody across species is limited, antibody development programs require development of novel approaches for evaluation of antibody-antigen interactions during the screening stage in order to assess the desirable lead antibody properties such as affinity and correlation with dose requirements (Tabrizi et al. 2009). As the efficacy and safety of this class of therapeutics are generally correlated with the extent of their interaction with their intended target antigen, screening studies as described below can generate meaningful information with respect to dose ranges where dose-dependent binding of target antigen can be achieved. Here, we have described an animal model whereby the preliminary evaluation of the antibody-antigen interactions can be addressed at early stages of preclinical development (Fig. 9.1 and Sect. 9.3). The screening data can provide supporting information for the design and conduct of exposure–response studies and appropriate dose selection in relevant species, generally nonhuman primates, and translation to the target patient population (Fig. 9.1). To this end, we have developed a conscious rat-infusion model, as described here, where recombinant human antigen(s) of interest can be infused continuously. Generally, the available information regarding the steady-state antigen(s) concentrations in relevant animal models (safety and pharmacology models) and the target patient population can be obtained from the available literature. Inclusion of the antigen(s) turnover kinetics will allow determination of infusion rates where the intended steady-state target antigen concentrations can be achieved (Fig. 9.2a). Once the steady-state antigen concentrations are achieved, various doses of the lead antibody (or a biologics construct) can be administered (Fig.  9.2a). Relationships between the antibody serum concentrations

(exposure) and the antigen concentrations (bound or total antigen, PD response) can then be quantitatively examined (Fig. 9.2b). The inverse relationships between the theoretical changes in free and bound antigen concentrations at steady state are shown in Fig. 9.3 and have been discussed previously (First Edition). This inverse relationship highlights the translational value of POM such as total or bound antigen concentrations where experimental evaluation of free antigen is not achievable (i.e., due to detection limit requirements for the analytical methodologies). Figure 9.4 shows the experimental results following simultaneous infusion of two antigens in rats. For each antigen, target antigen concentrations were selected based on the reported range for serum concentrations in the target patient population and nonhuman primates. Based on the molecular weight of each antigen, anticipated serum clearance was determined. In general, for antibodies against soluble targets, a “quasi-steady-state equilibrium” condition can be assumed to evaluate the potency of interaction. This model has been described previously and allows for evaluation of free and total (or bound) antigen levels following inclusion of the antibody elimination kinetics and the antibody-antigen interaction binding potency. 9.5 Case Study Considerations with respect to differences in antigen concentrations in serum versus those in the effect compartments such as tumor, skin, lungs, joint, and brain are important for design of dosing regimens in various settings. As the concentration of drug at the proximity of the biological receptor determines the magnitude of the observed pharmacological responses, a significant issue for effective therapeutic application of monoclonal antibodies is a thorough understanding of the processes that regulate antibody biodistribution. Similar to other modalities, distribution of monoclonal antibodies to various tissues is affected by factors such as molecular weight, blood flow, tissue and tumor heterogeneity as well as structure and porosity, target antigen density, turnover rate, and target antigen expression profiles (First Edition). As discussed in Chap. 3, the potency ceiling for affinity is highly dependent on antigen concentrations. In general, when antigen concentration is lower than antibody affinity ([Ag] ≪ K D ), higher affinity antibodies could be more efficacious. However, when [Ag] ≫ K D, binding is stoichiometric and antigen dependent; in these instances, improvements in affinity will not impact the dose requirements. In the case study discussed here, the fact that there are differences in antigen concentrations in serum versus those in lungs appeared to be a key variable

As efficacy and safety of ABTs are generally correlated with the extent of their interactions with the intended target antigen(s), the antigen-related toxicities are often predictable from preclinical studies if the antibody cross-reacts with the target antigen in relevant animal model(s). When cross-reactivity of a lead antibody across species is limited, antibody development programs require development of novel approaches. Screening studies in rodents can generate supportive information for the design and conduct of exposure–response studies and appropriate dose selection in relevant species, generally nonhuman primates, for translation to the target patient population.

Chapter 10: Antibody Drug Conjugates: Translational Considerations Mohammad A. Tabrizi, Isabel Figueroa, Wendy Blumenschein, and Jeff

198 M. A. Tabrizi et al. chemistry has resulted in a highly streamlined process, thereby eliminating conjugation heterogeneity as well as enhancing linker design, stability, and ADC manufacturability (Sievers and Senter 2013; Perez et al. 2014; Panowski et al. 2014; McCombs and Owen 2015; Shah et al. 2012). Monoclonal antibodies have become a key therapeutic modality for treatment of a broad range of ailments. The therapeutic prospect for monoclonal antibodies is derived from their exquisite specificity and high affinity binding to their antigen target (Tabrizi et al. 2012). A deep understanding of both the biology and the pathology of disease has facilitated target identification and validation. This knowledge has enabled the rational design of antibody therapeutic attributes including the mechanism of action, specificity, potency, isotype subclass, epitope, affinity, and half-life (Sadekar et al. 2015; Tabrizi et al. 2012; Bornstein 2015). Consideration and utilization of the appropriate animal models have enabled optimal translation of preclinical data in support of clinical trial design (Tabrizi et al. 2012). Evaluation of appropriate translational factors in the intended patient population(s) such as antigen expression and distribution, epitope, antibody affinity, and effector function efficiency has facilitated translation of exposure–response relationships across various species for monoclonal antibodies (Tabrizi et al. 2012; Bornstein 2015; Masson- Hinrichs and Dixit 2015). An important consideration for successful therapeutic application of an ADC is selection of the appropriate payload. The ultimate payload delivery to relevant compartments will depend on the target expression profile, density, turnover rate, and epitope selection; hence, translational evaluation of the key factors such as mode of action and potency, metabolism, protein binding, and elimination mechanisms for the payload across species is essential. Historically, clinical application of ADCs has been mainly focused on the targeted delivery of highly potent chemotherapeutic agents (e.g., microtubule inhibitors, DNA-damaging agents) with a cell-killing mode of action (Sievers and Senter 2013; Shah et al. 2012; Bornstein 2015; Drachman and Senter 2013; Adair et al. 2012). However, with the successful application of these pioneering therapies in oncology, now attention has been refocused on the potential targeted use of drugs with a narrow therapeutic index with application in immunology and inflammatory diseases (Sadekar et al. 2015). Technological advances along with recent successes of ADC modalities in the clinical arena have made this class of therapeutics highly desirable. Although numerous publications have addressed recent advances in the field (Perez et al. 2014; McCombs and Owen 2015; Drachman and Senter 2013), translational considerations critical for successful development of ADCs have not been fully appreciated (Tabrizi et al. 2012; Bornstein 2015). Current emphasis on preclinical lead selection appears to be mainly reliant on in vitro methodologies. However, it is not clear if mere application of in vitro approaches will be sufficient for successful design and selection and early development of this class of therapeutics. Here, we have attempted to address some of the key translational considerations important for early development of ADCs in an integrated fashion. As anticipated, successful application in a clinical setting will be highly dependent on effective translation of critical attributes governing exposure–response relationships for antibody drug conjugates across species.

10 Antibody Drug Conjugates: Translational Considerations 199 The discussion outlined in this chapter is mainly focused on translational aspects and further builds on various topics that have been addressed previously (Tabrizi et al. 2012, 2009; Bornstein 2015; Masson-Hinrichs and Dixit 2015). 10.2 Translational Considerations Effective translational strategies for development of ADCs should deliver superior and safe therapeutic candidates for clinical development of the intended patient population(s). Successful strategies for the design, selection, and development of ADCs will need to be established from the earliest days of drug discovery. Considerations regarding target selection and modality design have been discussed previously and are extremely important for application of ADCs in preclinical and clinical studies (Tabrizi et al. 2012; Bornstein 2015; Masson-Hinrichs and Dixit 2015). Quantitative approaches can establish an effective framework for evaluation of the interplay between drug- and system-dependent properties with regard to cellular and physiological properties (Sadekar et al. 2015; Tabrizi et al. 2012, 2009; Tabrizi and Funelas 2010). Effective strategies for the development of the lead candidate, therefore, will require comparative investigations and integration of knowledge with respect to target- and modality-related considerations across species. The most critical driver underlying the efficacy profile for an ADC is the successful delivery of the payload (drug) into the intended target cells, and hence evaluation of the intracellular trafficking post-ADC binding and internalization. Delivery of potent payloads to the effect site is a result of the interplay between target- and modality-related properties, and therefore it is not surprising that these same parameters are critical for translation of preclinical results into clinical settings. Key considerations for successful translation of information across species for ADCs are highlighted in Fig. 10.1. 10.2.1 Evaluation of Relevant Species One of the greatest challenges in the development of novel antibody-based therapeutics is identification of the most pharmacologically relevant species (Tabrizi et al. 2012; Bornstein 2015; Masson-Hinrichs and Dixit 2015). For ADCs, it is essential to understand that “relevant” signifies not only evaluation of those factors that are related to antibody and antigen interactions but a further examination of the translatability of the payload’s absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) and PD properties across species. Hence, successful strategies for the development of effective ADCs will require comparative investigations and integration of knowledge with respect to target antigen properties (epitope, expression, internalization, and density), antibody design criteria (such as affinity, isotype selection, and Fc domain interaction), payload PK, effect-time profile, and metabolism as well as evaluation of the factors that impact ADC exposure–response relationships across species (Fig. 10.1).

10.2.2 Target and Modality Related 10.2.2.1 Target Determination of target capacity (TC) across species is a key translational variable that requires not only relevant knowledge with respect to the target antigen expression within various tissues and organs in a given species but also an understanding of the target internalization rate. In many instances, target expression and density profiles (mRNA and protein levels) may drastically differ across species. Figure 10.2a, b) shows mRNA expression profiles across various tissues for a cell membrane-associated antigen in mouse, monkey, and human. Though direct quantitative comparison of the data across species may not be appropriate due to differences in the efficiency of the primers used for mRNA profiling, conclusions regarding gross differences in target expression profiles can be highly informative for predictions of the magnitude of the target sink in humans based on preclinical data (Figs. 10.2 and 10.3). Total target capacity has been previously described and utilized as a valuable parameter for the quantitation of drug-receptor interactions and induction of the downstream effects in an in vivo system (Sadekar et al. 2015; Tabrizi et al. 2012; Tabrizi and Funelas 2010). It is important to note that the total target pool capacity reflects both receptor turnover rates and total receptor numbers [V = total receptor max (R ) × target turnover rate (k ); Fig. 10.3]. Target capacity can directly impact TOT int antibody clearance in a dose-dependent fashion (Sadekar et al. 2015; Tabrizi et al. 2012, 2009; Tabrizi and Funelas 2010). The dose-dependent saturation of the nonlinear clearance pathway can be used not only as an effective proof-of-mechanism

As delivery of the payload via an ADC to the target cells will be limited by the target pool capacity (as defined above), this evaluation will be critical for determination of payload potency and payload residence time in a particular cellular compartment (Sadekar et al. 2015) and in predictions related to the target capacity in the intended patient population(s). Figure 10.3a demonstrates the impact of the target capacity (as determined by the intrinsic clearance: CL ) for an antibody against a cell-assoint ciated antigen undergoing target-mediated clearance in mouse and monkey. An approximate 12-fold difference in the target capacity (CL int ) was observed between the two species (Fig. 10.3b). The impact of the target capacity on the antibody serum exposure-t ime profiles in mouse and monkey is shown in Fig. 10.3a following administration of similar doses. It is interesting to note that the target density profiles in mouse appeared much more pronounced as compared to the monkey tissues (Fig. 10.2). Additionally, comparative evaluation of the target expression and

202 M. A. Tabrizi et al. density between human and monkey highlights the close similarity between the two, and thus can be employed as an important translational bridge for early predictions of the target capacity in support of first-in-human (FIH) clinical studies. The value of this translational tool is discussed in Sect. 10.3 (Case Study). 10.2.2.2 Antibody Design of an effective ADC, in addition to the target-related considerations, requires understanding of factors related to the antibody. Among the key antibody-related attributes that require careful attention are epitope identification, affinity, and isotype design specifications (antibody engineering requirements (Tabrizi et al. 2012)). Target internalization and turnover rates are among the key components for effective delivery of payloads via ADCs (see above). Slow internalization rates will limit the delivery of the payload while faster target turnover rates may allow for a higher delivery capacity per unit time when proper trafficking to relevant intracellular compartments occurs (Sadekar et al. 2015; Shah et al. 2012). Epitope selection can facilitate identification of specific residues on the target that may impact internalization rates. Ligand-induced internalization is a pivotal mechanism regulating availability and signaling of cell-associated receptors (i.e., EGF receptor). While interaction of some ligands (i.e., EGF) may result in receptor downregulation and lysosomal degradation, other ligands (i.e., TGF-α) may accelerate receptor recycling (Roepstorff et al. 2009; Puddicombe et al. 1996; Johns et al. 2004; Henriksen et al. 2013; Modjtahedi et al. 1998; Heukers et al. 2013; Perera et al. 2007). Different mechanisms have been identified that may contribute to differences in recycling profiles observed with these ligands (Roepstorff et al. 2009; Puddicombe et al. 1996). The diversity in internalization profiles and downstream activities observed with EGF receptor and various ligands has been attributed to the particular residues (epitopes) that are involved in interaction with ligands or various antibodies (Johns et al. 2004; Henriksen et al. 2013). In addition to epitope, the influence of affinity on antigen internalization rate has been addressed previously (Puddicombe et al. 1996; Johns et al. 2004; Henriksen et al. 2013; Rhoden et al. 2016). In general, higher affinity antibodies against the same epitope resulted in a higher degree of internalization. The impact of affinity on internalization can be directly correlated to the rate of dissociation (k ) of the antibody from its target. Hence, a thorough off translation of the preclinical data as relevant to delivery of the payload requires a rigorous comparison of the epitope, downstream trafficking consequences, as well as antibody affinity across various species. 10.2.2.3 Payload Of particular importance for successful application of ADCs is the selection of the payload with the appropriate potency. In general, delivery of the payload to the targeted cells will be limited by the target-related factors discussed above; hence in the face of potentially limited delivery of the payload, the potency evaluation is a critical parameter that requires the utmost attention during the early stages of ADC design. Appropriate measures will need to be put in place to potentially evaluate the potency differences across various species. Other factors such as protein binding,

10 Antibody Drug Conjugates: Translational Considerations 203 metabolism, clearance pathways, and payload residence time in the effect compartment need to be evaluated. These factors are particularly critical for therapeutic application of ADCs in non-oncology diseases and have been discussed previously (Sadekar et al. 2015). 10.3 Case Study Lymphocyte function-associated antigen 1 (LFA-1) is a human protein that is involved in cellular adhesion and costimulatory signaling. Efalizumab (Raptiva®) is a humanized IgG1 antibody that binds to CD11a, the alpha-subunit of LFA-1, and acts as an immunosuppressant by inhibiting lymphocyte activation and cell migration out of blood vessels into tissues. Efalizumab was associated with fatal brain infections and was voluntarily withdrawn from the market in 2009 by Genentech (https://www.fda.gov/Drugs). In humans, clearance of efalizumab was highly influenced by antigen expression. The target-mediated clearance (CL ) for this antibody int was evaluated in both human and chimpanzee as shown in Fig. 10.4 (Bauer et al. 1999). An approximately 50-fold decrease in the antibody clearance was observed with a ~100-fold increase in the dose (Fig. 10.4, panels B and D). Using the listed parameters given in Fig. 10.5 and the model shown in Fig. 10.4e, a theoretical framework was built to illustrate the impact of target capacity on payload delivery into a hypothetical effect compartment assuming: (a) complete efficiency in payload release and availability; (b) payload activity after release; and (c) lack of time dependency in any of the underlying processes (i.e., receptor shedding, up- or downregulation, or changes in clearance processes for both the antibody (AB) and the smallmolecule (SM) payload). For these simulations, it was assumed that each mole of the antibody could carry two moles of the small-molecule payload (SM) to the effect site with the stated payload potency (EC50; Fig. 10.5a–d). Predicted plasma concentration-time profiles after intravenous administration of the ADC are shown in

10 Antibody Drug Conjugates: Translational Considerations 205 Fig. 10.5a. Doses were selected so that different levels of target saturation were achieved (see simulations in Fig. 10.4c). According to these simulations, predicted payload concentrations and delivery rates into the hypothetical effect compartment were estimated (Fig. 10.5b). It is important to note that the rate of payload delivery (in the absence of any other rate-limiting steps as outlined above) is only limited by the target capacity, which is a function of total receptor and internalization or turnover rate. The relationship between target internalization rates and target capacity (CL at int a fixed receptor number) is shown in Fig. 10.5c. When k < < k (antibody’s dissociaint off tion from the cell is faster than the internalization rate), target-dependent delivery (via a CL estimate) should increase with a more rapid internalization rate (Fig. 10.5d). int However, when internalization rate is comparable or faster than the antibody-target dissociation rate (k int ≥ k off ), target-d ependent clearance becomes less sensitive to the value of k. Additionally, the impact of internalization rate on payload concentrations int (and hence the required payload potency) at the effect compartment is shown in Fig. 10.5d. As evident at fixed receptor concentrations (R), more stringent consideration for payload potency is required for targets with slower internalization rates. Conclusions Antibody drug conjugates combine the exquisite selectivity of targeted antibodies and the high potency of small-molecule drugs with the aim of achieving durable responses in patients. The most critical driver underlying the efficacy profile for an ADC is the successful delivery of the payload (drug) into the intended target cells. Delivery of potent payloads to the effect site is a result of the interplay between target- and modality-related properties, and therefore it is not surprising that these same parameters are critical for translation of preclinical results into clinical settings. Hence, the ultimate payload delivery to relevant compartments will depend on the target expression profile, density, turnover rate, and epitope selection. Chapter 11: Application of PK-PD Modeling and Simulation Approaches for Immuno- Oncology Drugs Xiaochen Zhao, Xiaoning

208 X. Zhao et al. dosing regimens, and design of rational immuno-oncology (I-O)-based combination therapies. Unlike traditional chemotherapeutic agents, I-O drugs bring a novel approach in cancer therapy where antitumor responses are triggered through modulating the immune system (Chen and Mellman 2013). Since the first immune checkpoint inhibitor Yervoy® (ipilimumab) was introduced in 2011, there has been considerable interest in evaluating the key regulatory mechanisms for I-O therapies, understanding their exposure-response (E-R) relationship, and identifying the potential sources of variability in clinical responses to I-O therapies (Stroh et al. 2015). Pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic (PK-PD) modeling and simulation (M&S) has served as an important tool for defining the E-R relationships for I-O therapies and predicting their outcomes in clinical practice. Model-informed drug discovery and development have been implemented in the development of I-O therapies and have also shown promise in accelerating the development cycle and decision-m aking processes (Milligan et al. 2013). This chapter focuses on the application and potential impact of quantitative approaches in this evolving field. 11.2 Preclinical Application Application of quantitative approaches during preclinical drug development is an inherent component of the drug development process. Quantitative evaluations typically involve data obtained from in vitro experiments and in vivo animal studies. These studies are generally performed to address drug mechanism of action, evaluate the response of pharmacological systems, triage different drug candidates, and establish E-R (exposure-response) relationships for lead drug molecules. While this approach has been implemented in the discovery and development of many I-O drugs, it is rather challenging to make clinically relevant decisions based on results obtained from these preclinical studies. It is evident that recapitulation of the entire immune function following I-O therapy (the main underlying pharmacology for I-O drugs) in animal models does not exactly parallel that observed in the intended target patient population in the clinical setting. Additionally, preclinical results obtained from in vivo studies are often limited, owing to the use of surrogate molecules (due to lack of cross-reactivity of the lead molecule in rodents) or surrogate animal models (i.e., use of reconstituted or humanized mouse models). Therefore, it is imperative that limitations of these preclinical studies be established early on as the experimental outcomes may have limited translational value. Noting these challenges, quantitative methods, i.e., PK-PD and M&S (modeling and simulation) approaches, can serve as important tools to enhance knowledge from preclinical experiments. If quantitative methods are implemented effectively they can serve as a valuable tool for integration of knowledge obtained from various preclinical experiments and should facilitate information flow from early stages of development to later phases. Using appropriate quantitative methods, integration of relevant preclinical data (both content from in vitro and in vivo studies) can be simultaneously achieved to establish robust mechanistic approaches necessary for lead selection and proof-of-mechanism and -concept (POM and POC) assessments

11 Application of PK-PD Modeling and Simulation Approaches 209 (discussed in Chaps. 7 and 9). Additionally, mechanism-based PK-PD models can incorporate intelligent approaches based on established knowledge regarding the human population, i.e., inclusion of critical parameters relevant to the human immune system, for effective translation of preclinical data. Reconstituted mouse models can be used to capture salient features of target biology and modulation (Zitvogel et al. 2016). 11.3 Case Studies: Preclinical Application Dual-Affinity Retargeting Molecule: An example of how in vitro data can be used to inform I-O drug development is provided by Chen et al. (2016). These authors investigated an extended half-life dual-affinity retargeting (LP-DART) molecule with the ability to target CD3 receptors on T cells and the P-cadherin receptor on cancer cells, simultaneously. The goal of this investigation was to integrate the preclinical data generated using the T-cell-engaging bispecific molecule to project “The Minimum Anticipated Biological Effect Level, MABEL” dose (http://www. ema.europa.eu/docs/en_GB/document_library/Scientific_guideline/2009/09/ WC500002989.pdf) for this biologic construct. First, a series of in vitro studies were conducted. In these experiments, changes in concentrations of the LP-DART (drug), T-cell numbers (effector), and relative number of target cancer cells (i.e., effector-to-target ratio; E:T) were evaluated. The number of effector cells (T cells) and target cells (cancer cells) was measured following incubation with LP-DART for up to 4 days; changes in both cell numbers obtained from all the treatment groups were characterized simultaneously using a quantitative model as shown in Fig. 11.1a. The triple-molecular complex, formed via interaction among the T cell, cancer cells, and bispecific molecule, was assumed to be important for killing of the cancer cells and drove the efficiency of the LP-DART for cancer-killing effects (Chen et al. 2016). The final model parameters were used to estimate the triple-molecular complex concentrations leading to 20% killing of cancer cells in vitro (i.e., a measure of activity). These authors also evaluated effective concentrations that were anticipated to demonstrate cytokine release-related toxicity using relevant in vitro assays. The LP-DART concentration that resulted in 20% of cytokine release levels (i.e., a potential safety endpoint) was selected as a threshold concentration for safety. As a follow-up experiment, in vivo studies were conducted and a quantitative PK-PD model for LP-DART was developed (Fig. 11.1b). The model characterized the PK of LP-DART using a two-compartmental model and accounted for the presence of the shed/soluble cancer target (i.e., P-cadherin) and CD3-expressing T cells in the systemic circulation. The bispecific molecule was then allowed to distribute to and from the tumor compartment using relevant diffusional pathways. Within the tumor environment the interaction between the T cell, cancer cell, and LP-DART followed the same processes as shown in Fig. 11.1a. The human PK for LP-DART was predicted based on allometric scaling of the data obtained from studies in monkeys. The parameters for drug diffusion to the tumor were included as reported previously (Schmidt and

210 X. Zhao et al. Wittrup 2009). Additionally, other parameters related to receptor expression in human tumors, drug binding, and antigen shedding were obtained from the published literature (Chen et al. 2016). The in vivo PK-PD model was used to predict the dose that provided steady-state tumor concentrations of functional complex at which 20% of tumor-killing efficiency was anticipated (i.e., predicted dose of 1.9 ng/kg/week). Alternatively, the same model was used to predict the equivalent LP-DART concentrations that resulted in 20% of cytokine release levels in the proposed clinical study (i.e., predicted dose ~1.5 ng/kg/week). Following this approach, higher doses were predicted to result in 10% occupancy of the cancer target (P-cadherin; 360 ng/kg/week) and CD3 cells (~8300 ng/kg/week), respectively. Since the biological effect of LP-DART is driven by the functional complex (i.e., synapse) formation, and the PK-driven approach assumes that only the drug concentration alone drives the pharmacology, in the end the investigators decided to use the outcome of the PK-PD modeling approach to choose the first-in-human (FIH) starting dose. The dose of 1.5 ng/kg/week was proposed as the FIH dose, to ensure that the LP-DART exposure would lead to minimal target binding while still maintaining safety and efficiency of the drug in clinical studies. Application of a Surrogate Antibody: A recent report highlights the use of a surrogate antibody in support of dose selection with an I-O application. Lindauer et al. (2017) reported a quantitative approach for evaluation of anti-PD-1 efficacy (using a surrogate molecule, DX400) in support of the lowest effective dosing regimen for the lead drug Keytruda®. The PK of the surrogate anti-PD-1 antibody (DX400) was evaluated in the MC38 colon adenocarcinoma-bearing C57BL/6 mice, and the antibody efficacy (tumor growth inhibition) at multiple dose levels was measured. In addition, to support the development of a mechanism-based PK-PD model, the occupancy of the PD-1 receptor in mouse blood and tumor was determined using a fluorescence-based assay. The final model structure consisted of (1) a two-compartment PK model to characterize plasma PK of an anti-PD-1 surrogate antibody; (2) physiologically based PK (PBPK) components to characterize tumor exposure (Shah and Betts 2012); (3) a receptor-binding model with a feedback mechanism that increased the expression of PD-1 upon upregulation of T cell; and

212 X. Zhao et al. (4) a tumor growth- and PD-1 receptor occupancy-driven tumor-kill model to characterize the in vivo efficacy of the anti-PD-1 antibody. Using this approach, the preclinical PK-PD model was employed to characterize plasma PK of the antibody, PD-1 receptor occupancy in the tumor, and tumor growth inhibition data closely (Chen et al. 2016). A translational approach was then employed to predict the human PK and the observed clinical efficacy in patients. In order to facilitate clinical translation, the PK-PD model was built based on (a) changing tumor exposure parameters from mouse to human (Shah and Betts 2012); (b) binding parameters between pembrolizumab and PD-1 obtained from the literature; (c) parameters related to degradation of the pembrolizumab-PD-1 complex that were allometrically scaled from mouse; and (d) parameters related to clinically relevant tumor size and tumor growth rate that were obtained from the literature (Liu et al. 2006; Tejera-V aquerizo et al. 2012). Data from the PK-PD studies in the reconstituted mouse model were then used to simulate the efficacy (i.e., percentage change from baseline tumor diameter) of pembrolizumab in melanoma patients using various dosing scenarios. This approach revealed that the lowest clinical dose with a high probability of reducing tumor size by >30% (i.e., attainment of partial response) was 2 mg/kg pembrolizumab given every 3 weeks (Q3W). Reducing the dosing interval to every 2 weeks (Q2W) and increasing the dose to 10 mg/kg showed a minimal improvement in the efficacy. This retrospective analysis was in agreement with the findings from various clinical studies demonstrating the activity of a 2 mg/ kg dose of pembrolizumab following a Q3W regimen. Additionally, it has been demonstrated that doses of 2 and 10 mg/kg in clinical settings provide equivalent clinical efficacy with respect to their ability to reduce tumor size. This retrospective analysis by Lindauer et al. (2017) highlights the potential application of PK-PD M&S for establishing a relevant preclinical E-R relationship for I-O drugs with potential application for translation into clinical settings. Quantitative methods may also be employed at the preclinical stage for understanding of the complex interactions between the immune system and tumor compartment, which may further allow evaluation of the therapeutic potential of different I-O approaches. In this regard, Peng et al. (2016) used a systems pharmacology approach to predict the efficacy of various I-O therapies in a prostate cancer setting. Initially, a PK-PD model was employed (Fig. 11.2) to address the involvement of relevant immune-related compartments that had an impact on the final therapeutic outcome. This model included a tumor compartment (i.e., prostate compartment) and a lymphoid compartment (representing the prostate-draining lymph nodes), with relevant communication channels accounted for between these two compartments (Fig. 11.2). The prostate compartment was assumed to contain two types of cancer cells, androgen-dependent/castration-sensitive prostate cancer cells (CSPC) and androgen-independent/castration-resistant prostate cancer cells (CRPCs) (Schmidt and Wittrup 2009). The androgen was assumed to function as a stimulus for the growth of CSPC and deprivation of androgen was assumed to transform CSPC into CRPCs. Both prostate and lymphoid compartments were assumed to contain dendritic cells, cytotoxic T-lymphocytes (CTLs) as effector immune cells, as well as Tregs and IL-2 as the inhibitory immune components.

11 Application of PK-PD Modeling and Simulation Approaches 213 Prostate Lymphoid Mature DC Functional DC Regulatory DC CTL Treg CTL Treg IL2 IL2 Androgen TUMOR TUMOR CSPC CRPC Fig. 11.2 A PK-PD model was established to capture complex immune interactions between the immune system and tumor compartment. This model was used to predict efficacy profiles following various I-O treatments in prostate cancer. The figure shows the interaction between tumor cells (in prostate) and immune microenvironment (in prostate and lymph tissue). The prostate tumor includes castration-sensitive prostate cancer (CSPC) and castration-resistant prostate cancer (CRPC) cells. The tumor-related immune system consists of dendritic cells (DCs), cytotoxic T cells (CTLs), and T regulatory cells (Tregs). The arrow and hammer represent promotion and inhibition in the interaction, respectively (reproduced from Peng et al. (2016)) The model was first trained using efficacy data generated in a prostate-specific P prostate cancer mouse model according to the following treatments: ten−/− 1. Castration (CX) or androgen deprivation therapy (ADT) 2. CX + IL-2 neutralization (AI) 3. CX + Treg depletion (AR) 4. UV-8101-RE sarcoma cell vaccination (V) 5. CX + V 6. CX + V + AR UV-8101-RE sarcoma cells (Dubey et al. 1997) work similarly to the FDAapproved prostate cancer vaccine Sipuleucel-T by programming the host immune system to seek out cancer cells. Subsequently, the final model was used to simulate the efficacy of 16 possible combination regimens of the 4 treatment conditions (i.e., CX, AI, AR, and V), to

214 X. Zhao et al. find out the most promising I-O treatment regimen for prostate cancer. It was found that Treg depletion and/or IL-2 neutralization both can effectively improve the treatment efficacy of ADT and vaccination, and Treg depletion was found to have a higher synergistic effect than IL-2 neutralization. Thus, the investigation by Peng et al. (2016) concluded that Treg depletion is a promising treatment approach for effective reduction of the tumor burden in castration-resistant prostate cancer. More importantly, this work provided an example of how a systems-based modeling approach can be developed using preclinical data for studying tumor-related immune responses and predicting effective I-O treatment approaches. Several other preclinical investigations highlight the application of quantitative approaches in support of I-O therapy (dePillis et al. 2014); (Serre et al. 2016; Klinke 2015). Klinke (2015) employed a quantitative systems pharmacology approach describing the IL-12 effects in the tumor microenvironment using in vitro data. It was shown that the model can be used to stratify patients based on the prevalence of specific IL-12 suppression mechanisms present within the tumor, and the model was used to guide selection of patient-specific therapies. Serre et al. (2016) developed a PK-PD model to demonstrate the utility of the simulation approaches in designing combination therapies for I-O drugs. Their model was not only able to simulate the preclinical PD effect of radiotherapy in combination with anti-PD-L1 and anti-CTLA-4 antibodies, but also able to offer an explanation for the reported biphasic relationship between the size of a tumor and tumor immunogenicity (as measured by the abscopal effect). In addition, the quantitative work conducted by Serre et al. (2016) has established an in silico design and optimization method to evaluate synergistic combination effects of radiotherapy and I-O therapies. 11.4 Clinical Application Once the drug development program moves forward into the clinical stage, early and precise characterization of drug disposition in patients and their association with clinical benefit and risk are extremely important in selecting the right dose for the targeted population, which in turn is essential for the development of a successful therapeutic product (Venkatakrishnan et al. 2015; Huang et al. 2013). Model- based predictions for anticancer agents can inform exposure-response relationships, covariate identification, and optimization of benefit-risk profiles (Buil-Bruna et al. 2016). With the breakthrough and maturation of I-O therapies, our knowledge regarding PK characteristics, E-R relationships, and quantitative systems approaches is evolving in parallel. We outline below selected examples of modeling and simulation approaches that have proven beneficial in clinical development of I-O therapeutics. For I-O therapy, dose selection in clinical studies usually occurs early on in clinical development and is typically based on tumor responses (via “Response Evaluation Criteria In Solid Tumors” or RECIST assessment). However, unlike cytotoxic chemotherapeutic agents, overall survival (OS) benefits may be observed following I-O therapy even though the magnitude of the tumor effect may not be as apparent (Agrawal et al. 2016). This lack of association between early clinical efficacy endpoints (i.e., following RECIST assessment) and long-term efficacy benefits

215 (OS) in pivotal trials has introduced a unique challenge for dose selection. In addition, by targeting the immune system regulatory pathways, I-O agents may also provide a unique opportunity to support a uniform dose and dosing schedule selection paradigm across different tumor types (Agrawal et al. 2016). In light of these differences, a model-based quantitative approach for dose selection during latestage clinical studies (phase 2/3) has been used widely in I-O clinical drug development (Agrawal et al. 2016; de Greef et al. 2017; Stroh et al. 2017; Wang et al. 2017). Nivolumab (Opdivo®) is an anti-PD-1 (programmed death-1 inhibitor) immune checkpoint inhibitor that blocks an inhibitory signal that prevents activated T cells from attacking cancer cells, thus allowing the immune system to clear the cancer (Jurado et al. 2008). In clinical studies addressing diverse tumor types, i.e., advanced melanoma (MEL), renal cell cancer (RCC), and squamous (SQ) and non-squamous (NSQ) non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC), a uniform dosing selection of nivolumab monotherapy has been reported (Agrawal et al. 2016). The integrated results from dose- and exposure-response for tumor dynamics, PD-1 receptor occupancy, clinical efficacy (objective response), as well as clinical safety (adverse events) in a dose-ranging phase 1 study were used to facilitate dose selection of nivolumab. The E-R for the objective response showed no further advantage in observed response at doses ≥1 mg/kg for melanoma and RCC and ≥3 mg/kg for NSCLC. Interestingly, the trend in the E-R relationship for objective response at each dose level was not consistent with the overall E-R, whereas nivolumab clearance appeared to be associated with the probability of response (Fig. 11.3). Overall RO fo ytilibaborP 0.1 8.0 6.0 4.0 2.0 0.0 11 Application of PK-PD Modeling and Simulation Approaches 1 mg / kg 3 mg / kg 10 mg / kg 5 10 15 20 25 30 35 CL [mL/h] Fig. 11.3 Exposure-response analysis conducted to characterize probability of objective response (OR) versus nivolumab clearance (CL) by dose level (1 mg/kg, 3 mg/kg, 10 mg/kg, and overall) in melanoma (reproduced from Agrawal et al. (2016))

216 X. Zhao et al. Collectively, an optimal monotherapy dose of 3 mg/kg Q2W was selected across the clinical development program, and subsequently demonstrated the survival benefits of nivolumab in patients with melanoma, NSCLC, and RCC in confirmatory phase 3 studies (Agrawal et al. 2016). This confounding effect of clearance on the E-R for efficacy was later identified in a subsequent nivolumab E-R analysis in both melanoma and NSCLC (Wang et al. 2017; Feng et al. 2017a; Bajaj et al. 2017a). The relationship between OS and nivolumab exposure (Cavg1: time-averaged concentration after the first dose) was characterized using the semi-parametric Cox proportional hazards model. Wang et al. (2017) showed that baseline clearance was a significant covariate of OS, and OS improved with decreasing baseline clearance in advanced melanoma. The possible hypothesis for the mechanism underlying this relationship is that a patient’s ability to clear nivolumab may reflect the patient’s overall disease state; therefore, baseline clearance serves as a surrogate biomarker for long-term survival benefit. Clearance is shown to be higher in patients with more severe disease (as indicated by ECOG status, serum albumin, and lactate dehydrogenase). The clearance of anti-bodies is also likely to be elevated in cancer patients with severe disease who are experiencing cachexia-related weight loss, which is characterized by high whole-body protein turnover (Porporato 2016; Meibohm 2016). Similar results were reported by Feng et al. (2017a) following E-R analysis of both SQ and NSQ NSCLC. After accounting for the baseline clearance effect, exposure was not a significant covariate for OS in MEL and NSCLC treated with nivolumab monotherapy. The authors also conducted a sensitivity analysis to show that without including the baseline clearance effect (Fig. 11.4a, b), the model underand overpredicted the observed OS data at 1 and 10 mg/kg dose in NSQ NSCLC. The precise characterization of the flat E-R relationships provided evidence for the recent FDA approval of a flat dosing regimen for nivolumab in melanoma, NSCLC, and RCC (Modification of the dosage regimen for nivolumab 2017). The early tumor response (e.g., tumor shrinkage at week 8) appears to be a significant predictor for the long-term survival benefit as shown in the tumor growth dynamic (TGD)-survival model in subjects treated with cytotoxic and targeted chemotherapeutic agents (Wang et al. 2009). Besides the E-R analysis of best overall response (BOR) used in phase 3 dose selection, the previously reported TGD model was also used to further characterize the relationship between nivolumab exposure and longitudinal tumor size data (sum of the longest diameter of index lesions). Although tumor growth dynamics appear to be different across tumor types, there were no apparent relationships between tumor shrinkage rates and exposure. The tumor progression rate decreased with increasing exposure for melanoma, NSCLC, and RCC. However, doses ≥3 mg/kg Q2W provided the maximum decrease in the tumor progression rate across all tumor types. TGD modeling has also been utilized to characterize pembrolizumab exposure and tumor dynamics (Chatterjee et al. 2017). The effect occurs at the plateau of maximal response at the exposure generated at 2 mg/kg and 10 mg/kg Q3W doses of pembrolizumab. Different types of E-R safety models have been utilized with immune checkpoint inhibitors. The logistic regression model was used to characterize the

Fig. 11.4 Kaplan-Meier of observed survival (red solid line), predicted median survival (blue dashed line), and 90% prediction interval (PI, blue shaded area) for nivolumab at 1 mg/kg, 3 mg/ kg, and 10 mg/kg Q2W in subjects with NSQ NSCLC, using (a) a full E-R model including nivolumab clearance effect and (b) a sensitivity E-R analysis without nivolumab clearance effect (reproduced from Feng et al. (2017b)) relationship between exposure and immune-related adverse events (irAE) to support approval of the first checkpoint inhibitor, ipilimumab. The E-R results showed that the probability of irAE increases with ipilimumab exposure. A limitation of the logistic regression model is that the model ignores the onset of adverse events. For example, severe events that occur after the first dose or at the end of an induction period (fourth dose) are treated the same. To account for the time of onset AE, the Cox proportional hazards model was used to support development of a second checkpoint inhibitor, nivolumab. The nivolumab E-R efficacy analysis was tumor type specific; however, the E-R safety analysis involved data from patients across different tumor types. In this analysis, AEs, leading to discontinuation or death (AE-DC/D), were selected as E-R safety endpoints, since they reflected a broad range of event categories that may have prevented patients from receiving

218 X. Zhao et al. treatments. The risk of AE-DC/D was expected to be similar regardless of tumor histology based on an earlier analysis showing the nature, frequency, and severity of treatment-related AE to be similar across multiple dose levels and tumor types (Agrawal et al. 2016). The E-R of AE-DC/D appeared to be flat, indicating that a safety risk did not increase with exposure within the examined dose range (1–10 mg/kg). As highlighted, though logistic regression and Cox proportional hazards model generally are used for exposure-response analysis, they have limitations with respect to addressing the intensity and onset of the AEs. However, Markov model has been shown to address these limitations. Feng et al. (2012) have utilized a Markov model to characterize the ipilimumab E-R relationships in regard to the occurrence, severity, and resolution of irAE, with incorporation of the modulating effects of steroids that may have been administered to manage severe irAE. The Markov model enabled characterization of the time course of irAE while taking into account the onset and duration of irAE, as well as evaluation of the drug concentration effect on irAE. This model suggested that the risk of irAE is near maximal by the end of the induction phase (12 weeks), and that extending the induction dosing regimen (every 3 weeks) beyond 12 weeks does not markedly increase the risk of irAE. The well-established statistical methods that are commonly used to characterize E-R relationships in drug development have recently been applied to I-O field. For example, case-control analysis was used to support the dose selection for elotuzumab, a CD-319 immune-stimulatory antibody, used in relapsed multiple myeloma (Clinical pharmacology and biopharmaceutics review(s) of elotuzumab 2017). The active control group was matched to the patients in the lowest-to-highest quartile of elotuzumab exposure with respect to multiple risk factors. The analysis showed that there was no difference in median PFS for patients in the lowest quartile of elotuzumab exposure as compared to patients on active control, while patients with the higher quartiles of elotuzumab exposure exhibited a treatment benefit. Additionally, multiple analysis methods can be integrated in light of the unique features of I-O agents. For example, interesting findings were made regarding the time-dependent clearance for various I-O drugs. Liu et al. (2017) investigated the association of such phenomena with disease dynamics using nivolumab. These authors combined the time-varying PK characteristics with case-control analysis and tumor growth dynamics modeling to suggest that the exposure metrics from the first dose are most appropriate for identifying a true E-R relationship. Cancer vaccines and oncolytic viruses represent an emerging class of I-O agents that promote antitumor responses through selective tumor cell killing and induction of systemic antitumor immunity (Kaufman et al. 2015). Although there is no clinical application of modeling and simulation reported to date, computational modeling approaches have been applied at the preclinical stage (Pappalardo et al. 2013). Piccoli et al. combined a mathematical model, consisting of a system of ordinary differential equations describing the cancer-immune system with a theory of optimal control, to determine the timing and the extent of immune system stimulation by means of immunotherapeutic agents (Castiglione and Piccoli 2007). A multi-scale mechanistic model developed by Wang et al. (2015) captures many features of

11 Application of PK-PD Modeling and Simulation Approaches 219 the immune system following adenovirus-mediated vaccination, including the activation and differentiation of naive CD8 + T cells in the lymph node, the trafficking of the resulting cytotoxic T cells into blood and the tumor microenvironment, and interactions between tumor cells, T cells, and cytokines that control tumor growth. Such in silico work has proven useful in screening for the optimal therapeutic dosage and for the dosing regimen in cancer treatment. Another mathematical model was used to gain insight into the cooperative interaction between anti-TGF-β and vaccine treatments (Wilson and Levy 2012). The model followed the dynamics of the tumor size, TGF concentration, activated cytotoxic effector cells, and regulatory T cells, and was capable of capturing the observed in vitro experimental results, and hence can be potentially used in designing future experiments involving this approach to immunotherapy. 11.5 Optimization Therapeutic monoclonal antibodies and chemotherapies are often dosed based on body weight (or body surface area), with a perception that this reduces interpatient variability in drug exposure, hence providing relatively consistent benefit-risk profiles across all patients. This approach has been applied to many I-O antibodies such as nivolumab and pembrolizumab. With the growing understanding of antibody PK and its impact on efficacy and safety, the weight-based dosing was reassessed for anti-PD1 antibodies with application of modeling and simulation approaches. The initial approved dose for nivolumab in melanoma, NSCLC, and RCC was 3 mg/kg every 2 weeks (Q2W); this dose was employed in the pivotal trials and demonstrated benefit over the standard of care with a favorable safety profile (OPDIVO (nivolumab) 2017). Further work was done to characterize PK variability and to establish E-R relationships of nivolumab across tumor types (Feng et al. 2017a; Bajaj et al. 2017a, b). Based on the extensive clinical data and evaluation of the E-R profiles, Zhao et al. (2017a) addressed the feasibility of switching the nivolumab dosage to a fixed-dosing regimen in multiple tumor types. The key findings from this analysis demonstrated that nivolumab safety and efficacy were similar over the exposure range observed following either a 3 mg/kg Q2W or 240 mg Q2W dosing regimens. More importantly, this quantitative analysis led to the regulatory approval of dose modification for nivolumab in multiple tumor types (Modification of the dosage regimen for nivolumab 2017). A similar example for pembrolizumab is presented by Freshwater et al. (2017). These authors compared the variability of pembrolizumab exposure following a 2 mg/kg Q3W and 200 mg Q3W dosing paradigms and demonstrated the similarity between these two regimens providing basis for the decision to move to fixed dosing regimens in clinical studies. Additionally, dosing frequency has been a subject of further optimization for I-O therapeutics. A case study was presented by Zhao et al. (2017b) leveraging robust clinical datasets with a quantitative framework to assess nivolumab dosing frequency in various tumor types (i.e., 480 mg Q4W vs. 3 mg/kg Q2W). By comparing the model-predicted variation in target concentrations and impact on the clinical

220 X. Zhao et al. safety (e.g., AE-DC/D, Grade 3+ AE) and efficacy (e.g., overall survival and objective response), these authors suggested a similar risk-benefit profiles for nivolumab for either regimens. In addition, determination of an optimized risk-benefit profile for combination therapies is significantly more challenging than for single-agent trials. A reliable estimate of the dose- and exposure-response relationship in combination settings requires robust dataset from a range of doses and sensitive measures for evaluation of clinical activity and toxicity. Recently, an E-R analysis of tumor growth dynamics and AE-DC/D was performed from a multi-cohort phase 1 study to determine risk-benefit profiles following combination of nivolumab and ipilimumab in first-line NSCLC patients (Zhao et al. 2017c). Analyzing the exposure, efficacy, and safety data in various cohorts, including nivolumab monotherapy and six different combinations, allowed identification of unique trends in E-R profiles following combination therapy and suggested the optimal regimens that could be further evaluated in a follow-up confirmatory registration trial. 11.6 Summary Recent strides in the field of immuno-oncology are generating the most exciting advances in cancer therapy with remarkable responses achieved in some patients. Understanding the clinical variables and rational design of I-O combinations remains challenging (Harris et al. 2016). The importance of PK-PD modeling in the entire drug development process from preclinical to clinical to life-cycle management is highlighted above. With advanced research to further identify novel targets as well as complex combination regimens, the utility of a quantitative approach is now extending beyond traditional population PK and exposure-response analyses. State-of-the-art modeling with emphasis on immunology, integrative pharmacology, and pharmacodynamics can maximize the potential for bench-to-bedside translation and improve the effectiveness of I-O drug development.
