Chapter 12: Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development of Combination Therapies Selvakumar Sukumar and Niña

224 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan FIH First-in-human dose GEP Gene expression profile GLP Good laboratory practice H&E Hematoxylin and eosin HED Human equivalent dose HLA Human leukocyte antigen IHC Immunohistochemistry test IO Immuno-oncology irAEs Immune-related adverse events MABEL Minimally anticipated biological effect level MHC Major histocompatibility complex MMR DNA mismatch repair MoA Mechanism of action NHP Nonhuman primates NOAEL No observed adverse effect level NSCLC Non-small cell lung cancer PBMC Peripheral blood mononuclear cells PD Pharmacodynamic PFS Progression-free survival PoC Proof of concept PoM Proof of mechanism RO Receptor occupancy TCR T-cell receptor TPS Tumor proportion score TRAE Treatment-related adverse events VEGF Vascular endothelial growth factor 12.1 Introduction The overarching goal of cancer therapy is to effectively eradicate cancer (maximize benefit) in a manner that is tolerable and safe in the targeted patient population (minimize risk). Maturation of antibody technology and development of antibody therapeutics present a much more targeted treatment option than chemotherapeutics. With chemotherapy, the attack is systemic and the therapeutic index relies on drugs being more toxic at a faster rate to cancer cells than normal cells. However, for solid tumors, accumulation of chemotherapeutics is inefficient, thereby limiting their efficacy (van der Veldt et al. 2010; Bosslet et al. 1998). The approval of Herceptin® for treatment of HER2-positive breast cancer was a watershed moment in targeted therapeutics. A critical component of this approval was the co-development of the biomarker assay for HER2 to properly select the patients who would benefit from treatment. Identification of patients who would respond to Herceptin based on the HER2 immunohistochemistry (IHC) test drastically increased the probability of survival and circumvented the use of costly therapies

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 225 that would have been ineffective. Of all the biomarkers approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) for cancer, the HER2 IHC test might have the highest probability of changing the treatment decision and presents the largest savings in treatment cost (Davis et al. 2009). This underscores the importance of the discovery and implementation of predictive biomarkers for clinical success. The development of appropriate pharmacodynamic and safety markers early in drug development can further facilitate a higher probability of success for new drug candidates. According to an analysis of drug failure rates from 2013 to 2015, lack of efficacy (52%) and unfavorable safety profile (24%), including a low therapeutic index, are the primary reasons for experimental drug failures (Harrison 2016). In addition to judicious implementation of biomarker strategies, combination therapies for the treatment of cancer have emerged as an effective way to overcome cancer heterogeneity and resistance. As early as 1961, combination therapies with two chemotherapeutic agents, prednisone and vincristine, were shown to elicit 90% complete remission of acute lymphocytic leukemia, compared to 21–57% for a single-agent therapy (Frei III and Freireich 1965). Historically, combination agents were selected based on different toxicities and mechanisms of action and then empirically tested in patients. Today, a vast array of combination therapy options are available. Agents have expanded from chemotherapeutic drugs to treatments that modulate the different facets that fuel cancer growth, i.e., oncogenic drivers, mutations, angiogenesis, and growth factor signaling. Recently, the emergence of cancer immunotherapy has increased the tools at our disposal. Instead of affecting cancer growth, IO modalities target the ways in which cancer evades detection and attack from the body’s immunological defenses. Currently, hundreds of clinical trials for combination of traditional oncology drugs and immune checkpoint blockade are ongoing. Synergy in curative potential is expected and discussions regarding cancer cure are moving into the realm of possibility. For example, targeted therapies are effective in the majority of patients with mutations in the targeted molecule, but clinical responses are short-lived. On the other hand, immune checkpoint blockade can lead to durable responses (Sharma and Allison 2015). Just as cancer can deploy various ways of evasion, survival, and proliferation to ensure its longevity, we are beginning to learn how to launch multipronged attacks to better defeat cancer. Currently, there are over 10,000 ongoing clinical trials in the United States alone investigating the benefits of various combination therapies for cancer (Rationalizing combination therapies 2017). Of those, over 800 include an immunotherapeutic agent. In spite of the high number of combination therapies currently tested in clinical trials, the theoretical therapeutic combinatorial possibilities based on approved and emerging single agents are quite vast, especially when combining more than two agents. A recent editorial article (Rationalizing combination therapies 2017) elaborates on the challenges in testing these combinatorial therapies both clinically and preclinically and the need for rationally combining these agents in order to maximize patient benefits. For example, although empirically determined initially, the mechanistic underpinnings of rationally combining PD-1 and CTLA-4 blockade are being increasingly understood (Buchbinder and Desai 2016). However, the

226 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan combination benefit is not apparent with all drugs that block the same pathway.1 It is possible that biomarkers could help decipher the differences between the pharmacodynamic responses observed with different investigational drug candidates addressing a similar underlying pharmacology. Similar to gains made in treating patients with combinations of anti-infective agents or chemotherapeutics, superior understanding of the modes of action of single agents, mechanisms by which resistance to treatment is generated, and delineation of the redundant and complementary pathways that regulate response and resistance will enable a higher probability of success for these combinatorial IO therapies. Biomarkers are critical for answering these unknowns. The discussions in this chapter are limited to the use of current and emergent biomarkers in the design and development of treatment combinations for cancer, with a special emphasis on emerging IO therapies. 12.2 Combination Studies in Cancer Immunotherapy Early clinical treatment of cancer has shown that combination of two or more chemotherapeutic agents can better eliminate cancer cells than any one of the agents alone. Decades later, while we have developed a better understanding of the complexity, adaptability, and persistence of cancer, we are also acutely aware of the necessity for additional treatment options. Although the diversity in therapeutic choices has grown considerably, it is not practical to test every possible combination. Instead, expansion of the mechanistic knowledge and predictive strategies should allow development of an intelligent approach for strategic design and implementation of promising combination therapies. Much excitement on the trans formative impact of IO therapies has given way to hundreds of combination therapies in various clinical trials. These combination studies span the spectrum of immunotherapy combinations with neoadjuvant treatment, chemotherapy, radiotherapy, targeted therapies, and other IO drugs. Synergy is predicated on the reduction of tumor burden, immunogenic cell death, presentation of neo-antigens, increased lymphocytic infiltration in the tumor, and/or distinct mechanisms of action. The hope is that these combination approaches should enhance immune responses, reduce (and eliminate) tumor burden, and/or mitigate toxicity. Yet, it is apparent that adverse events can become multiplicative or compounded with drug combinations. The lack of understanding in this compounded combination drug effect highlights the importance of developing not only predictive biomarkers but safety biomarkers as well. Currently, little is known in terms of which biomarkers can best predict efficacy and safety. Even predictive biomarkers, such as PD-L1 expression, that worked well in monotherapy may not have prognostic or predictive value when combination approaches are explored. In this chapter, a selection of combination therapies that may be instructive for the development of biomarkers is presented. 1 http://www.ascopost.com/News/57874

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 227 12.3 Case Studies The combination of nivolumab and ipilimumab for the treatment of advanced melanoma reported by Wolchok et al. is the first successful checkpoint inhibitor combination (Wolchok et al. 2013a). Objective response was observed for 53% of the patients at the concurrent dose combination of 1 mg/kg of nivolumab and 3 mg/kg of ipilimumab. In line with what has been found following single-agent therapies, responses were durable, with 2-year survival rates of 79%. Incidences of grade 3 or 4 adverse events (AEs), such as gastrointestinal effects and pathological inflammation of the gut, also increased. These were within the range of previously encountered AEs with monotherapy and consistent with prior toxicology studies in nonhuman primates (Melero et al. 2015). The AEs were manageable following administration of combination drugs. The authors also evaluated the benefit of sequenced treatment. Both objective response rate (20%) and grade 3 or 4 AEs (18%) were lower compared to concurrent combination therapy. A subsequent randomized Phase II study focused on patients with previously untreated BRAF wild-t ype melanoma (Postow et al. 2015a). Confirmed objective response occurred for 61% of the patients with BRAF wild-type tumors treated with the ipilimumab and nivolumab combination versus 11% in the ipilimumab and placebo group. Similar to the Phase I study, 54% of the nivolumab and ipilimumab combination group experienced grade 3 or 4 drug-related AEs compared to 24% for the placebo group. This combination was also found to be better in a randomized, double-blind Phase III clinical trial that compared the combination to either drug alone (Larkin et al. 2015). In both the Phase II and Phase III studies, the responses were independent of tumor PD-L1 expression, BRAF mutation status, or metastasis. PD-L1-positive patients had longer progression-free survival rates on nivolumab monotherapy or ipilimumab/nivolumab combination therapy compared to ipilimumab monotherapy. Incidence of adverse events was highest in the combination therapy arm and least with nivolumab monotherapy. In 2015, the FDA granted approval for a nivolumab/ ipilimumab combination in BRAF wild-type melanoma—the first approval for an immunotherapeutic combination. Clinical trials that test this combination for metastatic renal cell carcinoma and non-small cell lung cancer are ongoing. Combination therapy of BRAF and MEK inhibitors for the treatment of BRAF V600E-positive melanoma patients showed initial responses but the duration of response was less than 1 year (Larkin et al. 2014). BRAF V600E inhibitors, such as vemurafenib, have been shown to increase lymphocytic infiltration and to enhance T-cell function. The enhanced T-cell infiltration has made it a suitable candidate for combination with immunotherapy, which may prolong the duration of response (Sharma and Allison 2015). A Phase I study for the combination of vemurafenib with ipilimumab, however, was terminated due to a high incidence of hepatotoxicity (grade 3 elevation of aminotransferase levels) (Ribas et al. 2013). High-grade liver toxicities were not observed in the combination of ipilimumab with dabrafenib or with dabrafenib and trametinib. Dabrafenib and trametinib are also used to treat BRAF V600E-positive melanoma patients. The triple combination was stopped due to increased risk of colon perforation (Puzanov 2015). Further assessment of

228 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan dabrafenib and ipilimumab combination is ongoing. Given the better safety profile of anti-PD-1 antibodies compared to anti-CTLA-4 antibodies, a combination of BRAF inhibitors with PD-1 therapeutics is also being heavily investigated. Moreover, Frederick et al. have shown that BRAF inhibition can improve antigen presentation and major histocompatibility complex (MHC) expression, and can promote an overall favorable tumor microenvironment for PD-1 blockade (Frederick et al. 2013). This combination could potentially become more amenable to application of biomarkers, such as PD-1 expression and neo-antigen analyses. PD-1 and CTLA-4 blockades have also been combined with chemotherapy to achieve better response rates and overall survival. In the KEYNOTE-021 Phase II trial in patients with non-small cell lung cancer, pembrolizumab in combination with carboplatin and pemetrexed was compared with chemotherapy alone. The combination arm had more rapid response to therapy (1.5 vs. 2.7 months) and showed an 80% response rate for patients with tumors that had ≥50% PD-L1 expression, compared to a 29% response rate for chemotherapy alone (Langer et al. 2016). The frequency of grade 3/4 AEs was also higher in the combination arm (39%) versus the chemotherapy-only arm (26%). This combination is further explored in an ongoing Phase III clinical trial. Combinations of anti-CTLA-4 with chemotherapeutics have also been reported. Combination of ipilimumab with temozolomide had a median overall survival of 24.5 months. Notably, AEs were milder, such as nausea and constipation, compared to diarrhea and colitis encountered in other combination therapies (Patel et al. 2017). Serum lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) levels were used as a biomarker for patient selection. Although the scope of the trial was limited and the primary objective is 6-month progression-free survival (PFS), these results warrant further investigation of this combination. The vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) can suppress the immune system, making the anti-VEGF treatment with bevacizumab an attractive prospect for combination with checkpoint blockade. Bevacizumab has been found to improve the capability of circulating DCs to present antigens in treated patients with colorectal cancer (Osada et al. 2008). In metastatic melanoma, ipilimumab plus bevacizumab compared to ipilimumab alone has been investigated in a small Phase I study with 46 patients (Hodi et al. 2014a). Endothelial cell activation, permissive to the observed infiltration by CD8+ T cells and CD163+ dendritic macrophages into the tumors and a marked increase in circulating memory cell phenotypes, was attributed to the rationale for adding bevacizumab to ipilimumab treatment. Grade 3 or 4 proteinuria and hepatotoxicity, as well as immune-related AEs (irAEs) of arteritis, dermatitis, and eosinophilic hepatitis, were seen in some patients, but these AEs were tolerable. Median overall survival rate (OS) was notable at 25.1 months. The efficacy and apparent synergy of bevacizumab and ipilimumab justify trial expansion and bevacizumab addition to other immunotherapeutics, especially anti-PD-1. In the recent Phase III IMmotion 151 study, the combination of an anti-PD-L1 antibody, atezolizumab, with bevacizumab resulted in statistically significant improvement in PFS compared to sunitinib alone when used as a first-line treatment for patients with advanced or metastatic renal cell carcinoma (RCC). Patients receiving the combination therapy were selected based on a ≥1% expression of PD-L1 on

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 229 immune cells. Results of this study echo the results of the Phase III IMpower 150 study in patients with non-squamous non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC). This study also demonstrated improved PFS for patients treated with a combination of atezolizumab and bevacizumab versus bevacizumab plus chemotherapy.2 The cytokine granulocyte-macrophage colony-stimulating factor (GM-CSF) was combined initially with ipilimumab for the treatment of prostate cancer. Expected T-cell cross-priming by dendritic cells activated by the systemic administration of GM-CSF (sargramostim) was expected to potentiate patient response (Fong et al. 2009). Although therapeutic synergy was observed in preclinical models, these did not translate into clinical benefit for patients. A Phase II clinical trial with 245 patients with stage III or IV unresectable melanoma was conducted with this combination (Hodi et al. 2014b). Median OS was 17.5 months for the combination versus 12.7 months for ipilimumab alone, but no difference was observed in the PFS (3.1 months). Interestingly, fewer incidences of grades 3–5 AEs were observed for the combination (44.9%) versus ipilimumab alone (55.8%) as described below. Given the improved safety profile, a follow-up randomized phase 2/3 clinical trial for stage III or IV unresectable melanoma was initiated to compare the combination of sargramostim with ipilimumab or nivolumab (NCT02339571). This combination offers the unique potential of mitigating the treatment-related toxicities associated with IO therapies. 12.4 Pharmacodynamic (PD) Markers One of the key challenges in early clinical development is to understand if a drug candidate has the intended activity in humans. Demonstration of significant therapeutic benefit (efficacy) in proposed indications can only be achieved late in development after large proof-of-concept (PoC) studies or pivotal studies have been completed. This requires significant investment of resources and time. Hence, it is desirable to understand the activity of the drug candidate early in development to justify continued resource allocation and exposing high numbers of patients to the experimental therapy. Pharmacodynamic (PD) markers help understand and interpret drug activity in preclinical studies and early human trials. PD markers also facilitate establishing PK/PD relationships (concentration/response curves) early in clinical development, which supports the basis for human dose justification during the course of drug development. Accurate development of these models using physiologically relevant PD markers can decrease development times drastically by effective design of dose ranging/finding studies. Pharmacodynamic markers can be proximal or distal based on what they report. Proximal PD markers report on the immediate and direct effects of the drug and hence are less likely to be influenced by related biological pathways and processes (i.e., target-engagement markers). They are primarily useful in accurately quantifying target 2 https://www.gene.com/media/press-releases/14701/2017-12-10/phase-iii-immotion151study-showed-genen

230 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan engagement and are thus valuable in addressing if saturation of target is achievable at a relevant dose range. Proximal PD markers are not always linked to the proposed mechanism of action (MoA) of drug activity and may report on effects of target engagement unrelated to the disease intervention. Proximal markers help build PK/PD models that are more robust but may not necessarily be predictive of the anticipated efficacy. Nevertheless, they may guide the evaluation of safety and tolerability of the drug candidate upon sufficient target engagement. A classic example of a proximal PD marker is the estimation of receptor occupancy (RO) (Liang et al. 2016; Stewart et al. 2016). Receptor occupancy accurately reports on target engagement and is typically estimated by flow cytometry for immune cells in peripheral blood for membrane-associated targets. For soluble targets, an ELISA-based approach or other bioanalytical assays are employed (Lee et al. 2011). Receptor occupancy has not played a major role in the development of anti-PD-1 (Brahmer et al. 2010) and CTLA-4 antibodies due to the binding characteristics or receptor expression patterns, but has been extensively used to derive the first-in-human (FIH) dose for a variety of IO therapeutics in clinical development (Saber et al. 2016). Calculations of FIH doses help find a dose that is considered safe based on non-clinical toxicological studies. This entails identifying a human equivalent dose (HED) of the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) seen in a relevant toxicological species. However, in recent years, sponsors of clinical trials and regulatory agencies have advocated for a more conservative approach in determining a safe FIH dose. This was spurred by the Tegenero incident (Suntharalingam et al. 2006), wherein the first six healthy volunteers dosed developed severe cytokine release syndrome and subsequent multi-organ failure. Thus, an alternative approach frequently taken uses pharmacodynamic markers to calculate the minimally anticipated biological effect level (MABEL) (Muller et al. 2009; Agoram 2009). Receptor occupancy can easily be measured and, in several instances, is the only in vivo pharmacodynamic marker available to calculate MABEL. A recent review by the FDA highlights the importance and utility of RO in a MABEL-based approach to calculate FIH dose (Saber et al. 2016). Based on the known pharmacology of the drug candidate and prior knowledge of similar agents, determination of RO can also help increase the starting dose by providing a more precise estimate of target engagement, consequently shortening development timelines. A MABELbased approach used to predict the FIH dose and dose ranges is dependent on the availability of robust PD markers applicable for use in pharmacologically relevant species (Suh et al. 2016). Distal PD markers report effects that are phenotypically downstream from target engagement and hence reflect the overall effect of the drug in patients, inclusive of any counteractive responses. These markers, if aligned with the proposed MoA, are more predictive of safety and efficacy outcomes than proximal PD markers. Additionally, PD markers have the potential to accelerate and de-risk the development of drug combinations by aiding co-development of these agents. As we better understand the immunosuppressive mechanisms in cancer, it is becoming increasingly clear that some agents will be most beneficial if co-administered and might not have single-agent activity due to redundant or compensatory immunosuppressive

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 231 mechanisms. Accelerated co-development is typically justified for difficult-t o-t reat diseases like cancer, where risks and uncertainties are associated with the lack of sufficient data from individual development paths (Woodcock et al. 2011). In particular PD markers can help to understand the MoA of individual agents and their independent contribution to the safety and efficacy of the combination therapy. 12.5 Safety Markers Immunotherapy is a breakthrough therapy not only because of the significant and durable responses obtained in clinical trials but also due to the relatively favorable toxicity profile of these agents, especially compared to chemotherapeutic agents. Nevertheless, several AEs have been reported for approved checkpoint inhibitors and other immunotherapeutic agents. Our understanding of their incidence, severity, pathogenesis, management, and prevention has considerably increased since first approval. As expected, a vast majority of these AEs are immune related and are mainly due to a general increase in immune activation status and the breakdown of tolerance towards self-antigens. Most of the published information is on anti- CTLA- 4 and anti-PD-1/L1 antibodies (Champiat et al. 2016; Boutros et al. 2016), but adverse events for other approved agents have been reported as well. Anti-CTLA-4 is hypothesized to work by enhancing activation of T cells and by reducing the suppressive effects of regulatory T cells. It is reported to have a higher incidence of immune-related adverse events (irAEs) compared to anti-PD-1/L1 antibodies, which presumably act by preventing and reversing exhaustion of chronically stimulated T cells (Granier et al. 2017; Wolchok et al. 2013b; Topalian et al. 2012). Importantly, similar to the enhancement of efficacy, the incidence of adverse events is significantly increased when these agents are combined. For example, in a Phase III trial involving patients with advanced melanoma, treatment-related adverse events (TRAE) of grade 3 or 4 are similar in frequency for the two monotherapy arms but are doubled for the dual-combination arm. Specifically, 28% of the patients in the ipilimumab (anti-CTLA-4) group, 21% of those in the nivolumab (anti-PD-1) group, and 59% of those in the nivolumab-plus-ipilimumab group had severe AEs (Wolchok et al. 2017). In another Phase III trial in patients with untreated metastatic melanoma, TRAEs of grade 3 or 4 occurred in 16.3% of the patients in the nivolumab group and 27.3% of those in the ipilimumab group, but increased to 55.0% of those in the nivolumab-plus-ipilimumab group (Larkin et al. 2015). Since irAEs are associated with pro-inflammatory and general immune activation effects, it is expected that the incidence and severity of irAEs will increase when IO drugs are combined with each other or with other targeted therapies (Ribas et al. 2017a, b; Sullivan et al. 2017). When a combination of vemurafenib (BRAF inhibitor) and ipilimumab was used to treat patients with advanced melanoma in a Phase I study, there appeared to be an unacceptable risk of hepatotoxicity and further enrollment was stopped (Ribas et al. 2013). Combining GM-CSF and ipilimumab (10 mg/kg) was reported to prolong survival and decrease treatment-related irAEs in patients with advanced melanoma (Hodi et al. 2013), presumably due to differences in the

232 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan underlying pharmacology. Translational medicine approaches that help better understand the pharmacology and toxicology of these drugs will be vital for not only preventing and minimizing irAEs but also allowing intelligent combinations that improve efficacy without a significant increase in the incidence and severity of adverse events. Several studies have attempted to define biomarkers that can predict the propensity to develop irAEs (Manson et al. 2016). Fong and colleagues have done pioneering clinical work associating early diversification of T-cell receptor (TCR) repertoire after ipilimumab treatment as an indicator of both improved efficacy and increased risk of irAEs (Fong et al. 2016; Oh et al. 2017). Also, the expansion of T-cell clones in the periphery seems to precede the development of toxicities to anti-CTLA4 treatment and might be predictive of AEs (Subudhi et al. 2016). The effect of combining ipilimumab with anti-PD-1 on TCR diversification is currently being assessed. Higher levels of circulating IL-17 were found in a preliminary study to correlate with increased gastrointestinal toxicity of ipilimumab (Tarhini et al. 2015). Higher levels of eosinophils prior to treatment and a rise in eosinophil counts early in treatment have been reported to be associated with increased incidence of irAEs (Schindler et al. 2017). Although CTLA-4 expression is not documented on neutrophils, increased expression of CD177 and CEACAM1 genes on circulating neutrophils (probably indicating increased neutrophil activation) (Shahabi et al. 2013) and higher levels of neutrophil infiltration and activation in the gut mucosa (Berman et al. 2010) have been reported to be associated with increased occurrence of gastrointestinal adverse events. Predictive markers for irAEs observed in anti-PD-1/L1 therapies have not been studied extensively and the utility of the above-mentioned biomarkers in predicting irAEs in anti-CTLA-4 and anti-PD-1/L1 combinations is also not understood. Although the incidence of severe and life-threatening AEs has been relatively low (<10%) (Champiat et al. 2016), they are expected to rise when current treatments are combined with novel agents. Arguably our ability to select patients who are at a lower risk of developing irAEs is critical for identifying the optimal drug combinations for future development. A clear advantage for currently approved drugs is the availability of adequate information on the toxicity profile of single agents, which may not be available for newer combinations of drugs in early development. Unique challenges are posed when these combinations are developed as multi-specific modalities, since the toxicity profile of single agents or those attributable to each target cannot be deconvoluted experimentally. Similarly, tolerability data can be challenging to establish with adoptive T-cell therapies, cancer vaccines, and gene therapy approaches when combined integrally with checkpoint inhibitors in a single entity. Innovative and creative biomarker-focused translational medicine approaches are required to safely develop these modalities and combinatorial approaches. 12.6 Predictive or Patient Stratification Biomarkers Predictive biomarkers are used to rationally select the most likely responders to the specific therapy, to stratify them according to expected degree of benefit, and/or to exclude nonresponders or those who are likely to have adverse effects. A predictive

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 233 biomarker is distinct from a prognostic biomarker. The former is indicative of treatment efficacy whereas the latter predicts disease outcome, regardless of treatment (Ballman 2015). The Biotechnology Innovation Organization (BIO) found that implementation of a biomarker program for patient enrollment into clinical studies increased the likelihood of approval (LoA) for a drug at Phase I by threefold.3 Phase transition success rates were also significantly higher when patient selection criteria were included. Predictive biomarkers may be developed as companion diagnostics (CD). CD are developed in parallel with the drug program and are independently approved by the FDA in conjunction with the drug candidate. Appropriate predictive biomarkers can help accelerate drug approval, as in the case of the HER2+ CD assay for the drug Herceptin. Aside from accelerating the approval and thus speed to market, predictive biomarkers can help decrease the sample size in clinical trials while increasing response rates, and thus decreasing development costs. In the case of the CD for HER2 expression levels used by Genentech in the development of Herceptin, the Phase 3 trial consisted of 470 breast cancer patients and a markernegative control arm. Without the CD, the expected trial size would have been 2200 patients4 (Davis et al. 2009). From a financial perspective, predictive biomarkers can help reduce payers’ cost by helping select treatments to which the patient is most likely to respond. Implementation of predictive biomarkers in clinical trials can be done in a number of ways. If the scientific evidence is sufficient, they can be used from the Phase I trials to aid in drug dosage and patient stratification. Alternatively, several predictive biomarkers may be selected for concurrent development in Phase II of clinical trials for a specific drug, and a retrospective analysis of patients is performed afterward. Retrospective analysis of randomized controlled Phase III trials can also be done (Mandrekar and Sargent 2009). 12.7 Predictive Biomarkers in Cancer Immunotherapy Predictive biomarkers for cancer immunotherapy measure either the patients’ immune competence or activation prior to and in response to therapy or the genomic landscape within the tumors. Several criteria may be taken into account in the development of predictive biomarkers: (1) prevalence of biomarker; (2) heterogeneity of target expression; (3) detection agent or assay used; (4) how sample collection affects the biomarker; and (5) effect of prior/concurrent treatment on biomarkers. Biomarker prevalence may be bimodal or continuous. If a biomarker is binary, its presence or absence is tested and used in clinical decision-making. An example of this is testing for KRAS mutation status to predict the efficacy of anti-EGFR anti-bodies such as cetuximab and panitumumab in advanced colorectal cancer. For a continuous biomarker, the relative amount, such as expression levels, of the biomarker present is considered. Selection of the appropriate thresholds will be 3 https://www.bio.org/press-release/bio-releases-largest-study-ever-clinical-developmentsuccess-rates 4 Arthur Levinson: Presentation in October 2003.

234 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan necessary to optimize the benefit-to-risk ratio to patients. The need to set threshold levels makes this type of assessment more complex. Biomarker selection criteria are of critical importance. The intricacies and complexities of the selection criteria for a predictive biomarker are well explored in the example of PD-L1 expression. Currently, this is the most prominent predictive biomarker used in cancer immunotherapy. However, the complexity of the spatial, temporal, and cellular conditions that leads to PD-L1 expression calls for judicious use of PD-L1 expression to predict response to anti-PD-1/PD-L1 monotherapy and combination therapy. First, mere detection of PD-L1 is not indicative of response to anti-PD-1 therapies. Further knowledge of the mechanistic drivers of PD-L1 expression is necessary to fully optimize its use as a predictive biomarker. True adaptive immune resistance occurs when cancer cells express PD-L1 in response to T-cell infiltration, antigenic activation, and release of interferons (Taube et al. 2012). In turn, this serves as a stop signal to the activated T cells. Independent of response to immune activity, oncogenic processes can also drive cancer cells to constitutively express PD-L1 (Pardoll 2012). It is uncertain whether this type of PD-L1 expression is responsive to PD1/L1 blockade (Ribas and Tumeh 2014). Finally, dysfunctional, inactive T cells with no TCR specificity to tumor antigen may interact with tumors and may not produce interferons that lead to PD1/L1 expression. The three FDA-approved biomarker tests for PD-L1 expression are IHC 223 pharmDx (Keytruda, NSCLC), IHC 28-8 pharmDx (Opdivo, non-squamous NSCLC and melanoma), and SP142 (Tecentriq, metastatic urothelial bladder cancer) (Masucci et al. 2016). Of these three, only IHC 223 pharmDx is an approved companion diagnostic (Masucci et al. 2016). Keytruda™ is approved for first-line treatment of patients with metastatic non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) whose tumors have high PD-L1 expression [tumor proportion score (TPS) ≥50%] (first line) and for the treatment of patients with metastatic NSCLC whose tumors express PD-L1 (TPS ≥1%) as determined by an FDA-approved test, with disease progression on or after platinum-containing chemotherapy (second line).5 The other two are complementary tests that were tested retrospectively and can inform on the risk-to-benefit ratio. Unfortunately, independent development of these assays makes it impossible to compare clinical trial results using the different PD-L1 IHC assays. The Blueprint Working Group, an initiative formed among various pharmaceutical companies, was created to analyze the disparities among the various PD-L1 assays. Additional complications exist for the use of PD-L1 as a predictive biomarker. Differentiating between PD-L1 expression on tumor cells and tumor-infiltrating immune cells is challenging and the relevance is not well understood (Topalian et al. 2016). At a cellular level, PD-L1 is functional only when it is expressed on the membrane. However, the assays do not distinguish between membrane and cytosolic PD-L1 expression. Also, limitations on the binding sites possible for PD-L1 limit the utility of fixed patient samples that are often used for these types of diagnostics. Other points of subjectivity include the various conditions for antibody labeling in the IHC assay, the different algorithms used for scoring and determination of the 5 https://www.keytruda.com/static/pdf/keytruda-pd-l1-expression-testing-guide.pdf

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 235 thresholds, and the pathologists’ own judgment. Timing of when the sample was collected is also critical. PD-L1 status can change in response to previous or concurrent treatment. Therefore, whenever possible, tumor biopsies should be taken prior to proceeding with treatment to better assess the next steps. Aside from PD-L1 expression, other immunological markers have been proposed. Interestingly, PD-1 expression does not have an absolute correlation to the efficacy of anti-PD1 treatment. Higher levels of tumor-infiltrating lymphocytes (TILs), especially CD8+ T cells, have been shown to be predictive of positive outcome in anti-PD-1 and anti-CTLA-4 therapies (Hamid et al. 2011; Hodi et al. 2008). Detection of T-cell activation markers, such as 41BB, LAG3, and OX40, is thought to be an indirect measure of tumor specificity (Ribas and Tumeh 2014). Specific T-cell activation at the tumor site can be detected by cytokine release, especially of IFN-γ (Ribas and Tumeh 2014). Using an unbiased screen of gene expression profiling and an iterative process of “learn and confirm,” Ayers et al. developed a tumor baseline gene expression profile (GEP) consisting of 18 IFN-γ-related gene signatures with a score that predicts response to pembrolizumab (anti-PD 1) therapy (Ayers et al. 2017). They found strong concordance between the predictive value of the GEP and that of PD-L1 IHC for patient response to pembrolizumab therapy. The clinical grade GEP exhibited an impressive negative predictive value of >95% in several tumor types. Furthermore, statistical validation of this GEP (containing several IFN-γ-responsive genes) was much stronger when applied across various tumor types compared to single-gene assays. Elevated expression of IFN-γ and associated genes was also observed in melanoma patients treated with the anti-PD-L1 therapeutic atezolizumab (Herbst et al. 2014). Expression levels of shared tumor-associated antigens (TAA), such as Melan-A, tyrosinase, prostate-specific membrane antigen (PSMA), MAGE, and NY-ESO-1, have been correlated with response to ipilimumab (Topalian et al. 2016). NY-ESO-1 has been studied extensively as a biomarker to predict response to ipilimumab treatment, but the reported predictive value is not consistent among the studies (Callahan and Wolchok 2013). TAAs are in fact self-antigens, and an increasing body of evidence suggests that neo-antigens, which are mutated and therefore identifiable as nonself, may be better predictors of immunotherapeutic response. The aggregate genetic mutations, rearrangements, insertions, and deletions within a tumor are collectively called mutational load or mutational burden. The mutational load of a tumor can be estimated by next-generation sequencing (NGS) to identify nonsynonymous mutations and by comparison to normal cells (Snyder et al. 2014; Rizvi et al. 2015; Allen et al. 2015). A significant correlation between high mutational load and response to immunotherapy has been reported. Patients with cancers that have the highest mutational load, such as melanoma and NSCLC in smokers, have been observed to have high response rates to checkpoint inhibition (Snyder et al. 2014; Allen et al. 2015; Garon et al. 2015). Higher mutational load was reported to be associated with improved objective response, durable clinical benefit, and progression- free survival in two independent cohorts of non-small cell lung cancer patients treated with anti PD-1 (Rizvi et al. 2015). Similarly, a higher mutational load was also associated with clinical benefit in melanoma patients treated with

236 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan anti-CTLA-4 (Snyder et al. 2015). Supporting this, colorectal cancer patients with deficiency of mismatch repair proteins showed a better immune-related objective response and immune-related PFS (Le et al. 2015). Furthermore it was demonstrated that patients with advanced DNA mismatch repair (MMR)-deficient cancers across 12 different tumor types had significantly better responses (Le et al. 2017). Based on this and other studies that demonstrated efficacy in a total of 15 cancer types, the FDA approved anti-PD1 therapy for tumors positive for the biomarker referred to as microsatellite instability-high (MSI-H) or MMR-deficient, even if anti-PD1 antibody is not indicated for the tumor type.6 12.8 Emerging Technologies in Biomarker (BMX) Discovery Our ever-increasing capability for the acquisition of big data (genomics, transcriptomics, proteomics, epigenomics, metabolomics, etc.) coupled with advancements in the field of bioinformatics and computational biology to support data analysis has yielded a wealth of knowledge in understanding cancer. For example, the significantly decreased cost of next-generation sequencing technologies and the ability to use minimal sample volumes have further spurred the drive towards molecularly targeted cancer therapeutics and personalized medicine, wherein genomic data acquired on individual patients inform clinical decisions. Similarly, advances in fluorochrome chemistry, microfluidics, mass spectrometry, imaging techniques, and single-cell analytics have all greatly improved our ability to accurately detect and quantify biological processes. In this section, we detail the application of these new and emerging technologies to identify biomarkers that can illuminate the mechanism of action of immuno-oncology drugs, assess pharmacodynamic/pharmacokinetic effects, and better predict safety and efficacy in patients. 12.8.1 Evaluation of T-Cell Repertoire Given that the approved checkpoint inhibitors are designed to harness or reactivate the adaptive immune system, their effect on T-cell density, clonality, and localization can illuminate the effects of these therapies. For example, the type, density, and location of T cells within colorectal tumors are among the best predictors of disease outcome (Galon 2006). Estimation of T cell receptor (TCR) repertoire and clonal expansion can be achieved by multiplexed PCR amplification and deep, high-throughput sequencing of the complementarity-determining region 3 (CDR3) of the TCRβ chain. The clonal diversity of an individual’s T-cell repertoire stems from gene rearrangements and nucleotide addition/deletion at the rearrangement junctions during thymic development. Depending on the checkpoint inhibitor used, immune sequencing has revealed that T cells undergo either clonal expansion of 6 https://www.fda.gov/newsevents/newsroom/pressannouncements/ucm560167.htm

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 237 tumor-dominant clones resulting in increased clonality or an increase in TCR diversity and epitope spreading. Clonal expansion has been observed in anti-PD-1 therapy, wherein T-cell clones present prior to treatment are amplified posttreatment and overall T-cell density is increased (Tumeh et al. 2014). Epitope spreading, in which the T-cell population becomes more polyclonal, has been observed for CTLA-4 inhibition (Cha et al. 2014). A post hoc analysis of 12 patients with metastatic melanoma found that higher clonal diversity of circulating T cells prior to treatment with ipilimumab corresponded with better response rates in patients (Postow et al. 2015b). Melanoma patients whose tumor-infiltrating lymphocytes (TILs) show restricted TCRβ usage, indicating a more “clonal” nature of the immune response, and who show increased clonality of TILs upon treatment are more likely to respond to anti-PD1 therapy (Tumeh et al. 2014). Similarly in patients with urothelial cancer, lower peripheral TCR clonality was associated with increased survival, and clonality in the tumor and peripheral expansion of tumorresident clones after treatment were associated with a better response to anti-PD- L1 treatment (Funt et al. 2017). Increased clonality has also been demonstrated in melanoma patients treated with a BRAF inhibitor, and the existence of preexisting dominant T-cell clones was associated with better response (Cooper et al. 2013). Robert et al. reported that anti-CTLA-4 treatment increases peripheral TCR repertoire diversity (T-cell richness) in patients with melanoma (Robert et al. 2014). Similar results were demonstrated by other groups in advanced melanoma and prostate cancer (Cha et al. 2014). The relevance of TCR clonotypes to response has not been reported and thus TCR diversification can be a pharmacodynamic effect of CTLA-4 blockade. Since CTL-mediated antitumor effects are key to response, TCR repertoire analysis is fast becoming a standard and universal tool to dissect T-cell responses to immunotherapy (McNeel 2016). A three-arm study of combination therapy with cryoablation and ipilimumab compared with either therapy alone demonstrated the utility of this immunesequencing technique. The stability of DNA against mechanical disruption by cryoablation makes DNA sequencing a more viable method of analysis versus the gold standard of TIL analysis via hematoxylin and eosin (H&E) staining. Whereas cryoablation alone decreased T-cell density in the tumor, ipilimumab alone increased this factor. Combination therapy led to decreased T-cell density but diversified and remodeled the intra-tumoral T-cell clonal repertoire (Page et al. 2016). Compared to these changes in the tumor microenvironment, the clonal expansion of T cells in peripheral blood was not as notable. This may be attributed to the dilution of the intra-tumorally derived expanded T-cell clones within the abundant repertoire that exists in the peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMC). Regardless, TILs are thought to be the main effectors of an adaptive immune response and clonal expansion can be indicative of a response to tumor-associated antigens. Although this retrospective study does not assess neo-antigen production, cryoablation is known to release an abundance of tumor antigens from the resulting necrotic cells (Waitz et al. 2012). Tumor antigens, whether specific to the tumor type or to the individual patient, are also targets for biomarker discovery and immunotherapy as discussed below.

238 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan 12.8.2 Neo-Antigen Discovery The aberrant biology of tumors is marked by somatic mutations and genetic alterations that can lead to a higher mutational burden in cancerous cells versus normal cells. The correlation between high mutational load and response to IO therapies was discussed previously. These mutations, if nonsynonymous and present in the coding regions, encode for neo-antigens that in turn can serve as a biomarker of a tumor’s sensitivity (mutation load or frequency of such mutations) to checkpoint inhibition or even be targeted itself as a means of immunotherapeutic intervention. Bioinformatics comparison of whole-exome sequencing data from cancer tissues and healthy tissues provides a mutational landscape of the patient-specific tumor. To activate T cells, these neo-antigens must first be processed into shorter peptides, loaded onto major histocompatibility complex (MHC) class I or class II molecules, and the MHCneopeptide complex presented to the T cells for engagement. Since not all mutations result in generation of neo-antigens, a variety of considerations and computational algorithms are used to identify potential neo-antigens. Well-established computational methods are used to predict the probability that the mutant peptides will be presented on MHC alleles, taking into account the likelihood of proteasomal processing, transport into the endoplasmic reticulum, and affinity for the relevant MHC alleles. Confirmation that the identified neo-antigens are indeed presented is only possible by the use of mass spectrometry-based detection of peptides in the MHCpeptide complex. Indeed, several groups have taken this approach to successfully identify and confirm neo-a ntigens (Gubin et al. 2014; Yadav et al. 2014). Efforts to identify T-cell-activating neo-epitopes have yielded a handful of effective neo-epitopes from a large initial sample set (Robbins et al. 2013; van Rooij et al. 2013). Topalian et al. suggest that neo-antigenicity should not determine patient exclusion for treatment with checkpoint inhibitors, given that it is not always predictive of response (Topalian et al. 2016). Correlations have been observed between overall mutational load and responsiveness to either drug, especially in patients with melanoma and smokers with NSCLC, two groups with a high, carcinogen-i nduced mutational burden (Snyder et al. 2014; Rizvi et al. 2015; Allen et al. 2015). Instead, they suggest that mutational load and neo-antigenicity be employed as surrogate markers in the absence of a predictive biomarker for the administration of ipilimumab and/or pembrolizumab (Topalian et al. 2016). Enumeration and characterization of the T-cell repertoire, (discussed above) and multiparameter cytometry of peptide-MHC multimers (discussed below) can improve decision-making in patient selection. Identification and characterization of neo-antigens help understand the nature of immune response to immunotherapy and confirm that at least part of the response is mediated by T cells specific to neo-antigens. Nevertheless, it does not exclude the potential contribution of T cells reactive to self-antigens or viral antigens. Therapeutic uses of neo-antigen discovery include design of cancer vaccines, CAR- T, and other adoptive T-cell transfer strategies. An exciting new development is the use of patient-specific neo-antigen information in the design and use of personalized vaccines (Ott et al. 2017; Sahin et al. 2017). Although much work to date has focused on this process for human leukocyte antigen

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 239 (HLA) class I antigen presentation, engagement of HLA class II-neo-peptide complex by CD4+ T cells may also be important for the antitumor response. To better explore this requires more advanced computer algorithms given that peptide processing and loading differ between the two MHC classes. Indeed, clinical response of metastatic cholangiocarcinoma to infusion of CD4+ T cells highly enriched for reactivity against an MHC class II-restricted neo-antigen was demonstrated in a case report (Tran et al. 2014). 12.8.3 Multiparameter Flow Cytometry and Mass Cytometry Advancements in the excitation and detection capabilities of flow cytometers and the growing arsenal of fluorochromes available have greatly improved the ability to perform multicolor flow cytometry. Furthermore, simultaneously labeling cells in a single sample with up to 18 colors allows the generation of a significant amount of cell-specific data with limited amount of tissues. Flow cytometry is often used in immune monitoring of patients treated with immunotherapeutic agents. A clever application of this is the combinatorial encoding of peptide-MHC multimers in order to detect antigen specificities of CD8+ T cells from PBMCs in patients with melanoma (Hadrup et al. 2009). In this method, peptide-loaded MHC (pMHC) complexes are multimerized with streptavidin-fluorochrome conjugates. Eight different fluorochromes are used and two-color combinations of these pMHC multimers are created to yield 28 different dual-color codes. This configuration gives the highest sensitivity with the least amount of patient sample used to test for T-cell specificities. Mapping of the T-cell epitope and readout of CD8+ T-cell activation during checkpoint blockade are possible with this method. In addition, the immune competence of a patient, based on various cytokine/chemokine/degranulation events such as IFN-γ, MIP-1β, and TNF-α, can be determined by multiparameter flow cytometry. An orthogonal method to perform similar studies is with mass cytometry, specifically cytometry by time of flight (CyTOF), wherein the probes are labeled with heavy metal ions. This allows for the contemporaneous detection of up to 40 parameters per cell (Chang et al. 2014). Parallel advancements in data analysis and data mining are helping decipher the vast amounts of data generated due to these technological advances. A new field of computational flow cytometry is rapidly evolving to develop clustering and dimensionality reduction tools in order to analyze the large amount of high-dimensional data (Saeys et al. 2016; Chester and Maecker 2015). 12.8.4 Multicolor Immunohistochemistry The advantage of histopathological techniques is the generation of spatially and microanatomically relevant information using the most commonly available formalin-fi xed paraformaldehyde-embedded (FFPE) tumor specimens. Advances in multiplexing technology allow detection of up to eight individual antigens, which again spares precious tumor samples for other analyses. A mass spectrometry-based scanning technology is being developed using heavy metal ion-labeled reagents

240 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan which might potentially allow for detection of up to 40 antigens (Angelo et al. 2014; Giesen et al. 2014). Higher throughput is increasingly made possible by advances in automated slide processing and automated image analysis technologies. 12.8.5 Immune Cell Epigenomics with Quantitative Real-Time PCR-Assisted Cell Counting Epigenetic imprinting is considered to be one of the strongest correlators of cellular function and identity. The epigenetic process of demethylation to activate a gene is used as an enrichment marker for cells that are activated due to an immune response during checkpoint blockade. Thus, cell type-specific DNA methylation patterns can help quickly enumerate cell types in samples by established quantitative real-time PCR-based methods. DNA stability allows for the use of various fixed samples with less concern for degradation. This method has successfully been used in a Phase II study (SELECT trial) for monitoring the characteristics of regulatory and conventional T cells after CD25 blockade in multiple sclerosis patients (Huss et al. 2015). 12.8.6 Circulating Tumor DNA Fragments of DNA from both healthy and cancer tissues are released from cells for various reasons and these fragments of cell-free DNA can be found circulating in the blood. The fraction of circulating cell-free DNA that is released from primary tumors or metastasis is called circulating tumor DNA (ctDNA). Since these fragments contain the same genetic alterations seen in tumor cells, they can be used as an easily accessible sample source for developing diagnostic, prognostic, or predictive biomarkers. They have been primarily used for quantifying tumor burden (cancer screening and longitudinal disease monitoring) and to identify mutations in tumor cells. Since ctDNA is released mainly from necrotic and apoptotic tumor cells, response to immunotherapy results in an early spike in ctDNA followed by a significant decrease (Cabel et al. 2017). This has been used as a predictive marker for response (Goldberg et al. 2018; Iijima et al. 2017; Xi et al. 2016). Additionally, ctDNA levels also help differentiate true progression from pseudo-progression, which commonly occurs after immunotherapy (Guibert et al. 2017). Thus, by predicting response early in treatment, ctDNA-based biomarkers can enable physicians to make timely treatment decisions. 12.9 Translational and Experimental Medicine In the broadest sense of the term, translational research or translational medicine encompasses all activities that bring the benefits of scientific discoveries made at the lab bench to the patient bedside and to the community. A key element of this transition is conducting experimental medicine studies. Experimental medicine

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 241 usually refers to “investigation undertaken in humans, relating where appropriate to model systems, in order to identify mechanisms of pathophysiology or disease, or to demonstrate proof-of-concept evidence of the validity and importance of new discoveries or treatments.”7 Experimental medicine studies are not intended to obtain confirmatory data on safety and efficacy suitable for registration of medicinal products, but should be designed to demonstrate proof of mechanism (POM) or early proof of concept (POC). These studies typically precede and inform the design of late-phase or pivotal studies and, more importantly, should be designed to help make early Go/No-Go decisions. These studies must be focused, have clear objectives, enroll a limited number of subjects, be cost effective, and rely heavily on biomarkers for interpretation. The overall goal of these efforts must be to both build knowledge and expedite decision-making by using biomarker-driven POM and exploratory POC studies as an experimental medicine paradigm of “fail-fast” systems (Khanna et al. 2016). These translational approaches form the core of intelligent, efficient, and accelerated drug discovery and development. This is one of the top priorities for the pharmaceutical industry, government agencies, and patient advocacy groups. Experimental medicine studies can be designed to achieve any of the following objectives: • Understand the physiology of the target • Validate the relevance of the target in disease pathogenesis or disease mitigation • Discover and develop biomarkers • Identify optimal efficacious doses or explore different dosing regimens • Demonstrate POM and validate proposed MOA of the drug candidate • Identify clinical endpoints or surrogate efficacy readouts • Determine predictive markers of response or resistance and patient selection cri- teria for pivotal studies • Explore additional indications • Identify potentially beneficial combinations that can be explored in later phase studies A majority of the early-phase clinical trials that test drug combinations for the treatment of cancer have elements of experimental study design and are most efficient in selecting drug combinations for later stage clinical development. 12.10 Biomarker Strategy and Clinical Trials As expected, a key step in the success of any biomarker strategy is its implementation in clinical trials. Adequate attention must be paid to the technical and logistical aspects of implementing that plan. Furthermore, the regulatory and commercial 7 https://www.mrc.ac.uk/research/initiatives/experimental-medicine

242 S. Sukumar and N. G. Caculitan strategy should also be articulated at the outset to avoid regulatory hurdles and nonalignment of biomarker data with target product profiles (Marton and Weiner 2013). Successful implementation begins with a strategy that is intelligent, rational, and feasible. The assays need to be robust enough to endure some measure of variability in the protocol which inevitably is introduced when run at a variety of laboratories with similar but not identical capabilities. Technical and logistical issues that can be hurdles to clinical implementation need to be considered at the design stage and may pertain to: assay formats, assay complexity, technical expertise of the scientist, choice of sample matrix, and sample transportation and sample storage conditions. The clinical protocol must articulate the rationale for the proposed biomarker sample collection scheme and clearly identify the schedule at which assessments will be made. Detailed descriptions of procedures for sample collection, labeling, processing, storage, and transportation must be included in the supporting documents (typically in the procedures manual, operations manual, or laboratory manual). Since some of this information will be repeated in different sections, care must be taken to avoid inconsistencies. At trial sites, personnel must be adequately trained by the sponsor on complex procedures. Finally, careful site monitoring is essential both prior to trial start as part of site qualification and site initiation visits and during study conduct as part of scheduled audits. These should be clearly described in the monitoring plan (Williams 2006). The second part of successful implementation depends on the development and deployment of various assays that will be used to measure the intended biomarkers (Lee et al. 2005; Dancey et al. 2010). Once a biomarker plan is designed, assay development should start as early as possible. The time required for development and validation will depend on several factors, including the assay platform, complexity (single-plex or multiplex), whether the assay has a degree of pre-validation, certification or accreditation requirements (Clinical Laboratory Improvement Amendments (CLIA), College of American Pathologists (CAP), Good Laboratory practice (GLP), etc.), prior experience of the laboratories with the assays, and whether centralized testing is adopted. Additional time should be allocated for unexpected issues that may arise even for pre-validated assays. Validation must be performed at both the lab that develops the assays and partnered labs that may be contracted to run these assays. To ensure data quality, contract research labs will have to be qualified for each of the assays. Hence, it is preferable to run these assays at a central lab, if it is feasible and appropriate. The third and final part of successful implementation depends on appropriate data management and analysis. As more and more assay platforms advance into high-throughput and multiplexed formats, the amount of data generated from these assays is staggering. In particular, -omics-level assays (McDermott et al. 2013) and imaging modalities pose specific challenges in data management and analysis. Adequate precautions must be taken to ensure that metadata is captured optimally, adequate backups of data files exist, and loss of fidelity is avoided during data transfer. Detailed instructions should be written up to prevent lack of clarity in making positive, negative, or quantitative calls. A comprehensive analysis plan must be drafted prior to completion of assay validation so that it can also be part of

12 Translational Biomarkers: Application in the Clinical Development 243 validation and quality assurance. Adequate training for personnel must be provided where subjective or discretionary judgements will have to be made to minimize siteto-site variability and ensure comparability of data generated. This is especially important for histopathological (Kerr et al. 2015; Ilie et al. 2016) and quantitative imaging- based biomarkers. In summary, a well-thought-out biomarker plan, precise sample collection and handling instructions, adequate clinical site training, deliberate assay development and validation, meticulous qualification of testing laboratories, proper data management and analysis, and a rigorous quality assurance protocol are all key drivers of successful implementation of biomarker strategies. 12.11 Summary The translation of insights from preclinical research into early clinical trials is critical for rapid, cost-effective, and efficacious drug development. Translational medicine allows leveraging of emerging clinical biomarker data to both inform the next phase of clinical trials and provide feedback into additional preclinical studies for current and next-generation drug candidates. Preclinical data is used to identify PD markers that help assess the activity of drug candidates and to choose biomarkers appropriate for patient stratification and response to treatment. Since preclinical models are imperfect in predicting human response, the translational medicine teams must use biomarkers and efficacy surrogates to validate target selection and drug mechanisms as early as possible in clinical development. Further preclinical research can explore hypotheses generated from clinical biomarker data to develop biomarkers for the next stage of clinical testing. This seamless and bidirectional translation of knowledge between preclinical and clinical research is necessary for rapid, efficient, and intelligent drug development to ultimately deliver safe and effective medicines to patients. The tools and techniques necessary for the implementation of such translational medicine and biomarker strategies in cancer and immuno-oncology are rapidly advancing. However, as previously mentioned, the unprecedented number of different treatment options in cancer introduces unique challenges in identifying safe and efficacious combination therapies. Nevertheless, the translational scientist now has more tools than ever and must adeptly use these to aid the design and clinical development of combination immunotherapies. References Agoram BM.
